Deformations of ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} -Harmonic Spinors on 3-Manifolds
Gregory J. Parker

Abstract
. A ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} -harmonic spinor on a 3-manifold Y Y is a solution of the Dirac equation on a bundle that is twisted around a submanifold 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} of codimension 2 called the singular set. This article investigates the local structure of the universal moduli space of ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} -harmonic spinors over the space of parameters ( g , B ) (g,B) consisting of a metric and perturbation to the spin connection. The main result states that near a ℤ 2 \mathbb{Z}_{2} -harmonic spinor with 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} smooth, the universal moduli space projects to a codimension 1 submanifold in the space of parameters. The analysis is complicated by the presence of an infinite-dimensional obstruction bundle and a loss of regularity in the first variation of the Dirac operator with respect to deformations of the singular set 𝒵 \mathcal{Z} , necessitating the use of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem.



1.
Introduction

The notion of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor was introduced by C. Taubes to describe the limits of renormalized sequences of solutions to generalized Seiberg-Witten equations.
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors are also the simplest type of Fueter section, and are therefore of interest in the study of gauge theories and enumerative theories on manifolds with special holonomy. Beyond their appearance in these theories,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors are intrinsic objects on low-dimensional manifolds and can be studied independently.

This article investigates the local structure of the universal moduli space of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors over the space of parameters on a compact 3-manifold. The main result states that this universal moduli space locally projects to a codimension 1 submanifold, i.e. a “wall”, in the space of parameters. This provides a key step toward confirming expectations that
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors should enter into the above theories via wall-crossing formulas. Results in this direction have also been obtained by R. Takahashi using different techniques
[
51
]
. The present work grew out of attempts to develop a more robust analytic framework for these results, with an eye towards applications to gluing problems
[
44
]
and other deformation problems. As observed by S. Donaldson
[
10
]
, the same analytic issues arise in many distinct geometric contexts, many of which remain unexplored
[
26
]
.

1.1.
Main Results

Let
(Y,g)
be a closed, oriented, Riemannian 3-manifold, and fix a spin structure with spinor bundle
S\to Y
. Given a smooth, closed submanifold
\mathcal{Z}\subset Y
of codimension 2, choose a real line bundle
\ell\to Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}
. The spinor bundle
S\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell
carries a Dirac operator denoted
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}
formed from the spin connection and the unique flat connection on
\ell
with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
is a solution
\Phi\in\Gamma(S\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell)
of the twisted Dirac equation on
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}
satisfying

\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}\Phi=0\hskip 42.67912pt\text{and}\hskip 42.67912pt\nabla\Phi\in L^{2}.

(1.1)

The submanifold
\mathcal{Z}
is called the
singular set
. The latter requirement implies (non-trivially) that
|\Phi|
extends continuously to the closed manifold
Y
with
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq|\Phi|^{-1}(0)
. The existence and abundance of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors with
\mathcal{Z}\neq\emptyset
on closed 3-manifolds was established by Doan–Walpuski in
[
9
]
and strengthened in
[
27
]
.

In addition to the submanifold
\mathcal{Z}
, the Dirac operator relies on a background choice of a Riemannian metric
g
on
Y
and possibly a perturbation
B
to the spin-connection. Let
\mathcal{P}=\{(g,B)\}
denote the parameter space of possible smooth choices. Given a pair
(g_{0},B_{0})
and a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0})
with respect to this pair, the goal of the present work is to study the local deformation problem, i.e. to describe the structure of the set of nearby pairs
(g,B)\in\mathcal{P}
for which there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor.

This problem cannot be addressed with the standard elliptic theory used for classical harmonic spinors
[
29
,
34
]
. Indeed, if
\ell
has a non-trivial twist around
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, the Dirac operator
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
degenerates along the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and fails to be uniformly elliptic. Instead, it is an
elliptic edge operator
– a class of operators well-studied in microlocal analysis
[
47
,
36
,
39
]
. For such operators elliptic regularity fails, nor must the extension to Sobolev spaces necessarily be Fredholm. In particular, for natural function spaces where the integrability condition in (
1.1
) holds,
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
possesses an infinite-dimensional cokernel. As a result, the problem of deforming a solution to a one for a nearby parameter cannot be addressed in a straightforward way by an application of the implicit function theorem. The following key idea, first described by Takahshi in
[
51
]
, addresses this issue:
the infinite-dimensional obstruction is cancelled by deformations of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}
.

Since the Dirac equation
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}
depends on
\mathcal{Z}
, but
\mathcal{Z}
is in turn determined by the vanishing of the norm
|\Phi|
of a spinor solving (
1.1
), the singular set and the spinor are coupled and must be solved for simultaneously. The problem thus has a similar character to a free-boundary problem, where the domain and solution must be found concurrently, though the “boundary” here has codimension 2. In particular, this analysis requires an understanding of the derivative of the Dirac operator with respect to deformations of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}
.

Upgrading the singular set
\mathcal{Z}
to a variable, define the
universal Dirac operator
to be the operator acting on pairs
(\mathcal{Z},\Phi)
of a singular set and spinor with reference to a background parameter
p\in\mathcal{P}
by

\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(\mathcal{Z},\Phi):=\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}\Phi

where the choice of parameter
p=(g,B)
is implicit on the right-hand side.

Definition 1.1
.

Given a parameter pair
p=(g,B)\in\mathcal{P}
the
moduli space of smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors
is the space

\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(p):=\left\{(\mathcal{Z},\ell,\Phi)\ \Big|\ \not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(\mathcal{Z},\Phi)=0\ \ ,\ \|\Phi\|_{L^{2}}=1\ \right\}\Big/\mathbb{Z}_{2}

(1.2)

where lines bundles
\ell
are considered up to topological isomorphism. The
universal moduli space of smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors
is the union

\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}:=\bigcup_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(p).

(1.3)

Because
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is
\mathbb{R}
-linear and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts by
\Phi\mapsto-\Phi
, the moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(p)
at
p\in\mathcal{P}
is a real projective space for each fixed pair
(\mathcal{Z},\ell)
.

Remark 1.2
.

It is expected that there exist
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors where
\mathcal{Z}
is not smooth, even when the parameter
p=(g,B)
is. Results of Taubes and Zhang show that, in general,
\mathcal{Z}
must be a closed, rectifiable, subset of (Hausdorff) codimension 2
[
73
,
58
]
. Definition
1.1
could be revised to define a larger moduli space

\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\text{rec}}

(1.4)

requiring only this weaker degree of regularity of
\mathcal{Z}
. Taubes has conjectured
[
57
, pg. 9]
that the singular set is a smooth submanifold of codimension 2 for generic
p
; more generally it is expected that it has the structure of an embedded graph except possibly on a set of parameters of infinite codimension. The results of
[
62
,
23
,
4
]
support this picture. This article considers only the case that
\mathcal{Z}
is smooth (although Banach manifolds of finite regularity curves are used along the way).

We now state the main results. The first result, Theorem
1.4
describes the linearized deformation theory near a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor; the next result, Theorem
1.5
, addresses the non-linear version. Throughout, we fix a central parameter
p_{0}=(g_{0},B_{0})
such that there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0})
with respect to
p_{0}
meeting the following requirements.

Definition 1.3
.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0})
with respect to a parameter pair
p_{0}=(g_{0},B_{0})
is said to be
regular
if the following three conditions hold:

(i)

(
Smooth
) the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subset Y
is a smooth, embedded link, and
\ell_{0}
restricts to the möbius bundle on every sufficiently small disk normal to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

(ii)

(
Isolated
)
\Phi_{0}
is the unique
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor for the pair
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},A_{0})
with respect to
p_{0}=(g_{0},B_{0})
up to normalization and sign.

(iii)

(
Non-degenerate
)
\Phi_{0}
has non-vanishing leading-order, i.e. there is a constant
c>0
such that

|\Phi_{0}|\geq c\cdot\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{1/2},

holds on a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

When
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is smooth,

\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}:H^{1}(S\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell)\to L^{2}(S\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell)

(1.5)

has closed range and infinite-dimensional cokernel, where
H^{1}
is the Sobolev space of sections whose covariant derivative is
L^{2}
. Let
\Pi_{0}
denote the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection to the orthogonal complement of the range, which is naturally isomorphic to the cokernel. The linearized deformation theory is described by the following theorem, which gives a precise manifestation of the key idea explained above:

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}
denote the linearization of the universal Dirac operator at a regular
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
. Then the cokernel component of the partial derivative

\Pi_{0}\circ\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}:H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\longrightarrow\text{coker}(\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})

(1.6)

with respect to the singular set is an elliptic pseudo-differential operator, and its Fredholm extension has index
-1
.

Here, sections of the normal bundle
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is the tangent space to the space of embeddings of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. In Section
4
, it is shown that there is an isomorphism
\text{coker}(\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})\simeq\Gamma(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
of the infinite-dimensional cokernel with a space of sections of a vector bundle on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
; composing with this isomorphism, (
1.6
) is a map of sections of vector bundles on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and the meaning of pseudodifferential operator is the standard one. The order of this pseudodifferential operator depends on the order of chosen isomorphism with
\Gamma(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
, but the image in
\text{coker}(\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
is independent of this choice (see Remark
6.3
).

The proof of Theorem
1.4
shows that the image of (
1.6
) is
\text{coker}(\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})\cap H^{3/2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})
, up to a finite-dimensional space. As a consequence,
\not{\mathbb{D}}
displays a
loss of regularity
. Here, this loss of regularity manifests as follows: the map (
1.6
) has finite-dimensional kernel, and the closure of its range in
L^{2}
has finite codimension. The range, however, is not closed since
\text{coker}(\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})\cap H^{3/2}
is only dense in the
L^{2}
-norm, thus in particular, the linearization is not surjective. One cannot circumvent this simply by considering singular sets of
3/2
lower regularity so that the image in
\text{coker}(\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
is closed, because this makes the
\text{range}(\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
component and the non-linear terms unbounded. Thus the loss of regularity means one cannot simultaneously arrange that the operator is both bounded and has surjective linearization.

Loss of regularity is an intriguing phenomenon intrinsic to many types of PDE
[
19
,
31
,
1
]
. In general, it arises just as above when for every natural function space
\mathcal{X}
for the domain, the codomain
\mathcal{Y}
of the operator
\not{\mathbb{D}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
may be chosen
either
so that the non-linear part of
\not{\mathbb{D}}
is bounded, in which case the derivative
\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}
does not have closed range,
or
it may be chosen so that the derivative is Fredholm, in which case non-linear part is unbounded. Deformation problems for equations displaying a loss of regularity cannot be addressed using the standard Implicit Function Theorem on Banach spaces; instead they usually must invoke a version of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem on tame Fréchet manifolds, denoted in our case by
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
. Using the linearized result Theorem
1.4
and the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem leads to our main result:

Theorem 1.5
.

There exists an open neighborhood
\mathscr{U}_{0}
of the universal moduli space
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
centered at
(p_{0},(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0}))
such that the projection
\pi
to the parameter space
\mathcal{P}
\widehat{\mathscr{M}}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{X}
\mathcal{Y}
\pi
\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}

restricts to a homeomorphism from
\mathscr{U}_{0}
to
\pi(\mathscr{U}_{0})
, and the image
\pi(\mathscr{U}_{0})
posseses a Kuranishi chart of virtual codimension 1.

To possess a Kuranishi chart of virtual codimension 1 means that the set is locally modeled by the zero-locus of a smooth map
\kappa:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}
(see e.g. Section 3.3 of
[
8
]
). In particular, if the map
(\ref{mainamap})
has trivial kernel, then
\kappa
is transverse to 0 and
\pi(\mathscr{U}_{0})
is a smooth Fréchet submanifold of codimension 1. In either case,
\mathscr{U}_{0}
also consists of regular
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors.

More generally, the universal eigenvalue problem has a spectral crossing along
\pi(\mathscr{U}_{0})
:

Corollary 1.6
.

There is an open neighborhood
\mathscr{V}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of
p_{0}
possessing a Kuranishi chart of virtual codimension 0 such that for
p\in\mathscr{V}_{0}
there exists triples
(\mathcal{Z}_{p},\Phi_{p},\Lambda_{p})
defined implicitly as smooth functions of
p
satisfying

\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{p}}\Phi_{p}=\Lambda_{p}\Phi_{p}

(1.7)

for
\Lambda_{p}\in\mathbb{R}
and such that
\pi(\mathscr{U}_{0})=\Lambda^{-1}(0)
.

Of course, the triple coincides with
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0},0)
at
p_{0}
. Analogous to Theorem
1.5
,
\mathscr{V}_{0}
consists of regular
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic eigenvectors, and if the map
(\ref{mainamap})
has trivial kernel then
\mathscr{V}_{0}
is an open neighborhood of
p_{0}
and
\Lambda:\mathscr{V}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
is transverse to 0. Once again, the conclusion holds replacing
\mathcal{P}
by any tame Fréchet submanifold
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
such that
\Lambda
remains transverse.

Remark 1.7
.

Theorem
1.5
provides additional evidence for Taubes’s conjecture (Remark
1.2
) that smoothness of the singular set is a generic property. It shows that smoothness is stable in the sense that if
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0})
is regular, then there exists a neighborhood
\mathscr{U}_{0}
in the universal moduli space (
1.3
) consisting of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors whose singular sets are also smooth. Theorem
1.5
does not rule out the possibility that there are also other points nearby
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0})
in the larger moduli space (
1.4
).

1.2.
Relations to Gauge Theory

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors appear as limiting objects into two distinct settings in gauge theory: i) generalized Seiberg-Witten theory in 2,3, and 4 dimensions, and ii) Yang-Mills and enumerative theories on manifolds with special holonomy in 6,7, and 8 dimensions.

1.2.1.
Gauge Theory in Low-Dimensions.

Generalized Seiberg–Witten theory unifies the majority of noteworthy equations in mathematical gauge theory
[
5
,
69
]
, including the standard Seiberg-Witten equations
[
40
,
32
]
, the Vafa-Witten equations
[
65
,
53
,
52
]
, the Kapustin-Witten equations
[
71
,
70
,
37
,
38
]
, the complex ASD equations
[
56
,
21
]
, and the ADHM-Seiberg-Witten equations
[
22
,
7
]
. Generalized Seiberg–Witten equations are systems of non-linear first order PDEs whose variables are a connection
A
on a principal
G
-bundle for a compact Lie group
G
, and a spinor
\Psi
.

When there is an
a priori
bound on the
L^{2}
-norm of
\Psi
, as for the standard Seiberg–Witten equations, the moduli space of solutions (modulo gauge transformations) is compact. In general, the moduli space may include sequences of solutions along which the
L^{2}
-norm of
\Psi
diverges. A variety of convergence theorems following pioneering work of Taubes
[
57
]
have shown that for many generalized Seiberg–Witten equations such sequences converge after renormalization to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor. Thus
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors are the natural candidates for constructing boundary strata to compactify these moduli spaces.

True
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors as defined in (
1.1
) arise as limits of renormalized sequences of solutions to the 2-spinor Seiberg–Witten equations
[
24
]
(see
[
43
,
44
]
for more detailed exposition). Generalized
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors, for which the spinor bundle
S
is replaced by an arbitrary Clifford module of real rank
4
, appear as limits of a variety of other equations
[
57
,
56
,
61
,
60
,
59
,
69
]
. In particular, the
limiting configurations
of Hitchin’s equations (square roots of holomorphic quadratic differentials) on a Riemann surface are a dimensional reduction of (generalized)
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors; thus these objects extend well-studied phenomena on the boundary of the Hitchin moduli space
[
35
,
16
,
17
]
to the higher-dimensional and non-holomorphic setting. The deformation theory of the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic 1-forms, for which one takes the Clifford module
S=\Omega^{0}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R})
has been treated by Donaldson
[
10
]
using a reduction to a scalar equation (the analogue of which is not available in the present setting); various other cases in dimension 4 are the subject of forthcoming work
[
28
]
.

1.2.2.
Fueter Sections.

The
Fueter equation
is a non-linear generalization of the Dirac equation on 3 and 4-manifolds for spinors taking values in a bundle of hyperkähler orbifolds rather than a Clifford module
[
45
,
55
]
. Solutions of the Fueter equation are called
Fueter Sections
.

Fueter sections play a key role in proposals for constructing gauge-theoretic and enumerative invariants on manifolds with special holonomy in dimensions 6, 7, and 8. In particular, in both cases, they are expected to contribute terms to wall-crossing formulas which relate these theories to generalized Seiberg-Witten theories on low-dimensional calibrated submanifolds and compensate for losses of compactness as parameters vary. See
[
5
,
11
,
7
,
22
,
24
,
26
,
67
,
68
]
for more detailed exposition (all of which rely on the earlier work
[
64
,
54
]
). In another directions, there are putative applications of Fueter sections to symplectic geometry
[
66
,
30
,
49
,
6
]
, and to constructing generalized Floer theories on 3-manifolds
[
12
,
13
]
. In all these cases, a well-developed theory of Fueter sections is lacking and many aspects remain speculative.

At least In the contexts of coming from gauge theory, it is expected that Fueter sections with singularities are unavoidable. Singularities arise when a Fueter section intersects the orbifold locus of the target hyperkähler orbifold. The data of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors as defined in (
1.1
) is equivalent to that of a Fueter section valued in the hyperkähler orbifold
X=\mathbb{H}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
(see
[
43
]
Section 2 or
[
8
]
Section 4 for details), with
\mathcal{Z}
being the pre-image of the single orbifold point. For more general hyperkähler orbifolds
X
there is a stratification by stabilizer subgroups into subsets of codimension 4k, and a singular set arises where a Fueter section hits these strata. The reader is cautioned that even though these strata are codimension at least 4 and the base manifold
Y
has dimension 3, the singular set of codimension 2 cannot be perturbed away when
\ell
is non-trivial on small disks normal to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
; indeed, Theorem
1.5
confirms the singular set is stable under perturbations in this setting. Much of the work involving Fueter sections (e.g.
[
8
,
68
,
67
,
20
,
46
]
) has dealt only with the case that
\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
. This article contributes a step toward understanding Fueter sections with singularities.

1.3.
Outline

Sections
2
–
4
study the semi-Fredholm theory of the Dirac operator with a fixed singular set. Section
2
begins by establishing analogues of several standard results from elliptic theory, and Section
3
introduces the local “polyhomogeneous” expansions that replace the standard notion of elliptic regularity for the singular Dirac operator. Although many results in these first two sections are particular instances of more general results from the microlocal analysis of elliptic edge operators proved in
[
47
,
36
]
and subsequent work, we endeavor to give a largely self-contained exposition here. Section
4
studies the infinite-dimensional cokernel of the singular Dirac operator, and proves the isomorphism asserted following (
1.6
) with a space of sections of a bundle on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Section
4
contains (in the author’s view) many of the more technical points in the article, and some readers may prefer to read only the statements in Section 4 on a first pass.

With the semi-Fredholm theory for fixed singular set established, Sections
5
–
6
proceed to study deformations of the singular set. Because the Dirac operator behaves naturally with respect to diffeomorphisms, deforming the singular set
\mathcal{Z}
is equivalent to deforming the metric
g_{0}
among the family of metrics that arise as pullbacks
F^{*}g_{0}
by diffeomorphisms
F
moving the singular set. Schematically,

\begin{pmatrix}\text{varying }\mathcal{Z}\\
\text{fixed }g_{0}\end{pmatrix}\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathcal{Z}}\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}\ \ \ \overset{\text{pullback}}{\Rightarrow}\ \ \ \frac{\partial}{\partial g}\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}^{g}\hskip 28.45274pt\begin{pmatrix}\text{varying }g\\
\text{fixed }\mathcal{Z}_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

The first-variation of the Dirac operator with respect to metrics on the right hand side is given by a well-known formula of Bourguignon-Gauduchon
[
3
]
discussed in Section
5
. Calculating the family of pullbacks by diffeomorphisms leads to an explicit formula for the derivative
\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}
of the universal Dirac operator (Corollary
5.9
). Section
6
proves Theorem
1.4
by calculating the projection to the cokernel using the description from Section
4
, during which the loss of regularity becomes manifest.

It is worth emphasizing that while there is a pleasing geometric reason for Theorem
1.4
, the fact that the operator (
1.6
) is elliptic emerges quite miraculously from the formulas during the proof. Since differentiating the symbol does not preserve ellipticity, Bourguignon-Gauduchon’s formula leads to a highly non-elliptic operator on
Y
; the content of Theorem
1.4
is to assert that under the isomorphisms from Section
4
associating this with an operator on sections of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, ellipticity somewhat surprisingly emerges! Theorem
6.1
provides a more technical version of Theorem
1.4
, and an explicit formula for the elliptic operator (
1.6
) is given during the proof.

Sections
7
–
8
use Theorem
1.4
and a version of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem to prove Theorem
1.5
. Section
7
gives a brief and practical introduction to Nash-Moser theory, and Section
8
shows that the universal Dirac operator satisfies the necessary hypotheses. The most challenging of these is to show that Theorem
1.4
persists on an open neighborhood of
(p_{0},\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
. In this, the difficulty is ensuring that some of the more subtle aspects of Sections
4
and
6
are stable.

2.
Semi-Fredholm Properties

Let
(Y,g_{0})
be a closed, oriented Riemannian 3-manifold, and fix a spin structure
\mathfrak{s}\to Y
. The associated spinor bundle is denoted by
S_{\mathfrak{s}}\to Y
, and its Clifford multiplication by
\gamma_{\mathfrak{s}}:T^{*}Y\to\text{End}(S_{\mathfrak{s}})
. The real inner product on
S_{\mathfrak{s}}
is denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, and the spin connection by
\nabla^{\text{spin}}
. More generally
1
1

1

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors arising as limits in gauge theory as in Section
1.2
may have
B\neq 0
, consider connections of the form
\nabla_{B}=\nabla^{\text{spin}}+B
where
B\in\Omega^{1}(\mathfrak{so}(S))
is a real-linear endomorphism. Fix a choice
B_{0}
of such a perturbation.

Next, let
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subset Y
be a smoothly embedded link, i.e. a union of disjoint embedded copies of
S^{1}
. Choose a real line bundle
\ell_{0}\to Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and let
A_{0}
denote the unique flat connection on
\ell_{0}
with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Let
(S_{0},\gamma_{0},\nabla_{0})
be the Clifford module defined using the fixed pair
(g_{0},B_{0})
by

S_{0}:=S_{\mathfrak{s}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell_{0}\hskip 56.9055pt\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{\mathfrak{s}}\otimes 1\hskip 56.9055pt\nabla_{0}=\nabla_{B_{0}}\otimes\text{Id}+1\otimes\nabla_{A_{0}}.

(2.1)

This Clifford module carries a real-inner product still denoted
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, and a singular Dirac operator:

Definition 2.1
.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-Dirac operator
associated to the Clifford module
(S_{0},\gamma_{0},\nabla_{0})
is defined on sections
\psi\in\Gamma(S_{0})
by

\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\psi:=\gamma_{0}(\nabla\psi).

In contexts where no ambiguity will arise, we omit the subscripts and write
S,\gamma,\nabla,
and
\not{D}
for the objects associated to the data
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},g_{0},B_{0})
(though the latter three always retain their subscripts).

In the case that
B_{0}=0
and
\ell_{0}
extends over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
(and
a fortiori
if
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=\emptyset
), this is the classical spin Dirac operator associated to the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}\to Y
obtained from twisting
\mathfrak{s}
by
\ell_{0}
. The case of interest to us is that in which
\ell_{0}
does not extend over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and instead restricts to the mobius line-bundle on the normal planes of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. The first condition in Definition
1.3
restricts to this case.

When
\ell_{0}
does not extend over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, the Dirac operator
\not{D}
is singular along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and its extension to Banach spaces of sections is only semi-Fredholm. This section introduces Sobolev spaces of sections and describes the semi-Fredholm mapping properties of
\not{D}
. More general versions of these results for larger classes of singular operators can be found in
[
72
,
47
,
10
,
39
,
18
]
. Here, we give a self-contained exposition.

2.1.
Edge Sobolev Spaces

The “edge” Sobolev spaces provide a natural domain on which the singular Dirac operator
\not{D}
is bounded (see
[
47
]
for definitions in the context of more general singular operators). Let
r
denote a smooth weight function equal to
\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z}_{0})
on a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and equal to
1
away from a slightly larger tubular neighborhood. For smooth sections compactly supported in
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, define the
rH^{1}_{e}
and
L^{2}
-norms respectively by

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\ dV\right)^{1/2}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ and }\hskip 28.45274pt\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}:=\left(\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\psi|^{2}\ dV\right)^{1/2},

where
\nabla
is the connection (
2.1
) on
S
, and
dV
denotes the volume form of the Riemannian metric
g_{0}
. In addition, define the
r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}
-norm as the dual norm of
rH^{1}_{e}
with respect to the
L^{2}
-pairing:

\|\xi\|_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{\varepsilon}}=\sup_{\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}=1}\langle\xi,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Definition 2.2
.

The
edge Sobolev spaces
of regularity
m=1,0,-1
are defined respectively by

\displaystyle rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\ \varphi\ |\ \ \|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}<\infty\}

\displaystyle L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\ \psi\ |\ \ \|\psi\|_{L^{2}}<\infty\}

\displaystyle r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\ \xi\hskip 1.70717pt\ |\ \ \|\xi\|_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}}<\infty\}

i.e. as the completions of compactly supported smooth sections with respect to the above norms. When it is clear from the context, the domain
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and bundle
S
are omitted for brevity. By construction,
r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}=(rH^{1}_{e})^{\star}
is the dual space with respect to the
L^{2}
-pairing.

These spaces are equivalent for different choices of the weight function
r
and of the pair
(g_{0},B_{0})
. Additionally,
rH^{1}_{e}
and
L^{2}
are Hilbert spaces with the inner products arising from the polarization of the above norms.

Although
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is not compact, the weight ensures following version of Rellich’s Lemma holds, proved by a standard diagonalization argument.

Lemma 2.3
.

The inclusion

rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)\ \hookrightarrow\ L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

is compact.
∎

2.2.
Mapping Properties

The following proposition gives the fundamental mapping properties of the singular Dirac operator on the spaces defined in the previous subsection.

Proposition 2.4
.

The operator

\not{D}:rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)\longrightarrow L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S).

is (left) semi-Fredholm, i.e. it satisfies:

•

\ker(\not{D})
is finite-dimensional, and

•

\text{range}(\not{D})
is closed.

Proof.

It is immediate from the definitions of
rH^{1}_{e},L^{2}
that
\not{D}
is a bounded operator. Given
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
, it suffices to show that there is a constant
C
such that the estimate

\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}\leq C\Big(\|\not{D}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\Big)

(2.2)

holds. Using the compactness of the embedding from Lemma
2.3
, both conclusions of the lemma then follow from standard theory (see, e.g.
[
42
]
Section 10.4.1).

The estimate (
2.2
) follows from the Weitzenböck formula and integration by parts, as we now show, though some caution must be taken about the boundary term along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Let
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
be a spinor, and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
N_{1/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
denote a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
of radius
1/n
. Additionally, let
\chi_{n}
denote a cut-off function equal to 1 on
Y\setminus N_{1/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and compactly supported in
N_{2/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
satisfying

|d\chi_{n}|\leq\frac{C}{n}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{r}.

Then, integrating by parts and using that
\not{D}
is formally self-adjoint,

\displaystyle\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\not{D}\varphi|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\langle\not{D}\varphi,\not{D}\varphi\rangle\chi_{n}\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\langle\varphi,\not{D}\not{D}\varphi\rangle\chi_{n}\ +\langle\varphi,\gamma(d\chi_{n})\not{D}\varphi\rangle\ dV.

The Weitzenböck formula shows that

\not{D}\not{D}=\nabla^{\star}\nabla+F

wherein
F
is a zeroth order term arising from the scalar curvature and the derivatives of the perturbation
B_{0}
. Substituting this and integrating by parts again yields

\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\not{D}\varphi|^{2}\ dV=\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\langle\varphi,F\varphi\rangle+\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\langle\varphi,d\chi_{n}\cdot\nabla\varphi+\gamma(d\chi_{n})\not{D}\varphi\rangle\ dV

where
\cdot
denotes contraction of 1-form indices. Since
F
is smooth on
Y
hence uniformly bounded, rearranging and using Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|\not{D}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\int_{N_{2/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+|d\chi_{n}|^{2}|\varphi|^{2}\ dV\right)

(2.3)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left(\|\not{D}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\underset{n\to\infty}{\lim}\int_{N_{2/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\ dV\right).

(2.4)

Since
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
, the latter limit vanishes, hence

\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\Big(\|\not{D}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\Big).

(2.5)

To conclude, we show the left-hand side of (
2.5
) dominates the
rH^{1}_{e}
norm. For
n
sufficiently large, choose local coordinates on
N_{1/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})\simeq S^{1}\times D_{1/n}
. Denote these by
(t,r,\theta)
for
t
the coordinate on the
S^{1}
factor and
(r,\theta)
polar coordinates on
D_{1/n}
. For each fixed
t_{0},r_{0}
, the fact that the holonomy around the loop
(t_{0},r_{0},\theta)
for
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
is
-1
implies that the operator
\nabla_{\theta}
has lowest eigenvalue
1/2
on this loop (see the local expressions in Section
3.1
). It follows that

\int_{N_{1/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}\ dV\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{N_{1/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\nabla_{\theta}\varphi|^{2}\ dV\leq\frac{1}{4}\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

(2.6)

on
N_{1/n}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
, and away from this neighborhood the weight
r
is uniformly bounded. Combining this estimate with (
2.5
) (possibly increasing the constant) yields (
2.2
), completing the lemma.
∎

The proof of Proposition
2.4
shows that the space
rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)
is equivalent to the space whose norm only includes
|\nabla\varphi|^{2}
, which dominates the second term in the
rH^{1}_{e}
-norm by (
2.6
) It follows that the integrability condition in (
1.1
) holds if and only if
\Phi\in rH^{1}_{e}
. We conclude:

Lemma 2.5
.

A non-zero spinor
\Phi
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor if and only if it is in the kernel of the operator

\not{D}:rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)\longrightarrow L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S).

(2.7)

∎

Note that although the estimate (
2.2
) resembles the standard bootstrapping inequality, it does not imply that an
L^{2}
solution of
\not{D}\psi=0
necessarily lies in
rH^{1}_{e}
. In order to establish (
2.2
) it was necessary to assume
a priori
that
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
, else the boundary term along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
need not vanish and the proof fails. Since
\not{D}
is uniformly elliptic on any compact subset
K\subset Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, however, standard theory applies to show that
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{loc}
(in fact
C^{\infty}_{loc}
) but the pointwise norm need not be integrable as
r\to 0
. Indeed, as we will see in Section
4
, the
rH^{1}_{e}
-kernel and the
L^{2}
-kernel are genuinely different spaces, with the latter infinite-dimensional. The term
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor refers only to non-zero kernel elements in
rH^{1}_{e}
.

2.2.1.
The Adjoint Operator.

Although the cokernel of (
2.7
) is not necessarily finite-dimensional as in standard elliptic theory, it can still be described as the solutions of the formal adjoint operator. As in the proof of Lemma
2.4
, formal self-adjointness of
\not{D}
and integration by parts shows that the relation

\langle\not{D}v,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle v,\not{D}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}}

(2.8)

holds for
v,\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
. As a consequence of (
2.8
), the Dirac operator extends to a bounded map

\not{D}:L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)\longrightarrow r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S),

where for
v\in L^{2}
, the spinor
\not{D}v\in r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}
is the linear functional defined by the relation (
2.8
). To emphasize the domain of definition of various manifestations of the Dirac operator, we write
\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}}
or
\not{D}|_{L^{2}}
.

We then have the following:

Lemma 2.6
.

The extension
\not{D}|_{L^{2}}
defined by (
2.8
) is the (true) adjoint of
\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}}
, and there is a closed orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)=\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\ \oplus\ \text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}}).

Proof.

Suppose that
\psi\in L^{2}
is perpendicular to the range, i.e.
\langle\psi,\not{D}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
for all
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
. The definition of
\not{D}|_{L^{2}}
via (
2.8
) shows that as a linear functional on
rH^{1}_{e}
, one has
\not{D}\psi=0.

∎

2.2.2.
The Second Order Operator.

The (left) semi-Fredholmness of
\not{D}
implies that the second order operator
\not{D}\not{D}
is Fredholm for purely formal reasons. More precisely, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 2.7
.

The second order operator
\not{D}\not{D}:rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)\longrightarrow r^{-1}H^{-1}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

is Fredholm and
\ker(\not{D}\not{D})=\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\simeq\text{coker}(\not{D}\not{D})
. In particular, there is is a constant
C
such that the elliptic estimate

\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}\leq C(\|\not{D}\not{D}\varphi\|_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}}+\|\pi_{1}(\varphi)\|_{L^{2}}).

(2.9)

holds, where
\pi_{1}(\varphi)
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto
\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
.

Proof.

(Cf.
[
51
]
Proposition 4.4)
By definition of
\not{D}|_{L^{2}}
via (
2.8
), if
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
and
\varphi\in\ker(\not{D}\not{D})
, then

0=\langle\not{D}\not{D}\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}}=\|\not{D}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}

hence
\varphi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
, which is finite dimensional by Proposition
2.4
.

To show that the range is closed and the cokernel finite-dimensional (and naturally isomorphic to
\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
), let
f\in r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}
be such that
\langle f,\Phi\rangle=0
for all
\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
. Consider the functional
E_{f}:rH^{1}_{e}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

E_{f}(\varphi):=\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|\not{D}\varphi|^{2}-\langle\varphi,f\rangle\ dV.

The Euler-Lagrange equation of
E_{f}
is

\not{D}\not{D}\varphi=f

so it suffices to show that
E_{f}
admits a minimizer. By standard theory (
[
14
]
Chapter 8) this holds if
E_{f}
is (i) coercive, and
(ii) weakly lower semi-continuous. The second of these is standard (see e.g.
[
14
]
Section 8.2.2). (i) means that

E_{f}(\varphi)\geq c_{1}\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}^{2}-c_{2}

(2.10)

holds for some constants
c_{i}
, and
\varphi
in the
L^{2}
-orthogonal complement of
\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
, which follows from the elliptic estimate (
2.2
) of Proposition
2.4
and Young’s inequality. Since we require
\langle f,\Phi\rangle=0
for
\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
, it follows that
\dim\text{coker}(\not{D}\not{D})\leq\dim\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
, and integration by parts establishes equality. This proves Fredholmness, and the estimate (
2.9
) is a routine consequence.
∎

As a consequence of the preceding lemma, we may define
P_{0}:r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}\to rH^{1}_{e}
as the solution operator given by

\displaystyle P_{0}(\xi)=\varphi\hskip 42.67912pt\text{s.t.}

\displaystyle\text{i)}\ \not{D}\not{D}\varphi=\xi\mod\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})

(2.11)

and

\displaystyle\text{ii)}\ \langle\varphi,\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}}=0\ \forall\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}}).

(2.12)

As with
\not{D}
, the subscript is omitted and we simply write
P
when it is clear from context.

To summarize, we have the following corollary:

Corollary 2.8
.

The following hold using the splitting
L^{2}=\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\oplus\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
of Lemma
2.6
.

(A)

The second order operator
\not{D}\not{D}
factors through the
\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
summand of

rH^{1}_{e}
\begin{matrix}\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\\
\oplus\\
\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\end{matrix}
r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}.
\not{D}
\not{D}
P

In addition, we can further split
\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})=\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\oplus\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})^{\perp}
wherein the first summand is finite-dimensional.

(B)

The projections
\Pi_{0},1-\Pi_{0}
to
\ker(D|_{L^{2}})
and its orthogonal complement may be written

1-\Pi_{0}=\not{D}P\not{D}\hskip 56.9055pt\Pi_{0}=1-\not{D}P\not{D}.

∎

2.3.
Higher Regularity

This subsection extends the results of the previous two subsections to “edge” and “boundary” Sobolev spaces of higher regularity (see
[
47
]
again for a more general exposition). Beginning with the “boundary” spaces, define the space of “boundary” vector fields

\mathcal{V}_{\text{b}}:=\{V\in C^{\infty}(Y;TY)\ \ |\ \ \ V|_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};T\mathcal{Z}_{0})\}

as those tangent to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
at the boundary. Let
\nabla^{\text{b}}
denote the covariant derivative with respect to such vector fields, so that in local coordinates
(t,x,y)
where
t
is a coordinate along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and
x,y
are coordinates in the normal directions it is given by

\nabla^{\text{b}}=dx\otimes r\nabla_{x}\ +\ dy\otimes r\nabla_{y}\ +\ dt\otimes\nabla_{t}

and is equal to the standard covariant derivative
\nabla
away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
, define the
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
-norm on compactly supported smooth sections by

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}|(\nabla^{\text{b}})^{m}\psi|^{2}\ +\ldots+|\nabla^{\text{b}}\psi|^{2}\ +\ |\psi|^{2}\ dV\right)^{1/2}.

(2.13)

Definition 2.9
.

The
mixed boundary and edge Sobolev spaces
are defined by

\displaystyle rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Big\{\varphi\ |\ \|(\nabla^{\text{b}})^{m}\varphi\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}}+\ldots+\|\nabla^{\text{b}}\varphi\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}}\ +\ \|\varphi\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}}<\infty\Big\}

\displaystyle H^{m}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Big\{\psi\ |\ \|(\nabla^{\text{b}})^{m}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\ldots+\|\nabla^{\text{b}}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\ +\ \|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=\|\psi\|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}^{2}<\infty\Big\}

\displaystyle r^{-1}H^{m,-1}_{\text{b},e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Big\{\xi\ |\ \|(\nabla^{\text{b}})^{m}\xi\|^{2}_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}}+\ldots+\|\nabla^{\text{b}}\xi\|^{2}_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}}\ +\ \|\xi\|^{2}_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}}<\infty\Big\}

equipped with the norms given by the positive square root of the quantities required to be finite. As for
m=0
, changing the weight
r
or
(g_{0},B_{0})
results in equivalent norms. More generally, one can define the spaces for
m\in\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
by interpolation.

We have the following version of the standard interpolation inequalities:

Lemma 2.10
.

The following interpolation inequalities hold for
m_{1}<m<m_{2}
:

\|\psi\|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|\psi\|^{\alpha}_{H^{m_{1}}_{\text{b}}}\|\psi\|_{H^{m_{2}}_{\text{b}}}^{1-\alpha}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{\ \ }\hskip 28.45274pt\|\varphi\|_{H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}}\leq C\|\varphi\|^{\alpha}_{H^{m_{1},1}_{\text{b},e}}\|\varphi\|_{H^{m_{2},1}_{\text{b},e}}^{1-\alpha}

where
\alpha=\frac{m_{2}-m}{m_{2}-m_{1}}
, and the constants may depend on the triple
m_{1},m,m_{2}
.

Proof.

Choose local cylindrical coordinates
(t,r,\theta)
on a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, where
t
a coordinate along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and
(r,\theta)
polar coordinates in the normal directions. The coordinate change
s=\log(r)
is a diffeomorphism between
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and the manifold
Y^{\circ}
given by attaching a cylindrical end
T^{2}\times(-\infty,r_{0})
near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Under this coordinate change,
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
is taken to the standard Sobolev spaces
e^{-s}H^{m}
with the an exponential weight. After multiplying by an exponential weight function, the inequalities for
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
follow from the standard ones on
Y^{\circ}
(see, e.g.
[
15
]
).

For the mixed boundary and edge spaces, note that
\|[\nabla,\nabla^{b}]\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^{2}}
, and iterating these commutators shows that

\|\varphi\|^{2}_{H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}}\sim\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}+\|\tfrac{\varphi}{r}\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}

(2.14)

is an equivalent expression for the norm, after which the interpolation inequalities for
H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
follow from those for
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
applied to
\nabla\varphi
and
\tfrac{\varphi}{r}
.
∎

Applying the elliptic estimate (
2.2
) to
(\nabla^{\text{b}})^{m}\varphi
and iterating commutators
[\nabla,\nabla^{\text{b}}]
also establishes the following higher-regularity elliptic estimates:

Corollary 2.11
.

There are constants
C_{m}
depending on up to
m+3
derivatives of the pair
(g_{0},B_{0})
such that the following elliptic estimates hold for
\varphi\in rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
:

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}(\|\not{D}\varphi\|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}+\|\varphi\|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}})

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}(\|\not{D}\not{D}\varphi\|_{r^{-1}H^{m,-1}_{\text{b},e}}+\|\varphi\|_{r^{-1}H^{m,-1}_{\text{b},e}})

∎

Using this, we immediately deduce the higher-regularity version of Corollary
2.8
.

Corollary 2.12
.

For all
m>0
, the following statements hold:

(A)

There is an
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
-closed decomposition

H^{m}_{\text{b}}=\ker(\not{D}|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}})\oplus\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}})

orthogonal with respect to the
L^{2}
-inner product. Moreover, the latter two spaces coincide with
\ker(\not{D}|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}})=\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}
and
\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}})=\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}
.

(B)

The second order operator
\not{D}\not{D}
factors through the
\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}
summand of

rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
\begin{matrix}\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}\\
\oplus\\
\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}\end{matrix}
r^{-1}H^{m,-1}_{\text{b},e}.
\not{D}
\not{D}
P

(C)

The projections to the two summands in Item (B) of Corollary
2.8
respect regularity in the sense that

(1-\Pi_{0})=\not{D}P\not{D}:H^{m}_{\text{b}}\to H^{m}_{\text{b}}\hskip 56.9055pt\Pi_{0}=1-\not{D}P\not{D}:H^{m}_{\text{b}}\to H^{m}_{\text{b}}

are bounded operators. ∎

3.
Local Expressions

This section studies the expressions for the Dirac operator and its solutions in local coordinates on a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. By Proposition
2.4
and Lemma
2.6
, there is a dichotomy between two distinct types of solution:

\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\hskip 56.9055pt\psi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\text{ \ \ s.t. \ \ }\psi\notin rH^{1}_{e}

(3.1)

with the former being the finite-dimensional space of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors.

It is instructive to first consider the model case of
Y_{\circ}=S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
equipped with the product metric, which is done in Section
3.1
. Sections
3.2
and
3.3
then deal with local expressions on a general 3-manifold.

3.1.
The Model Operator

Let
Y_{\circ}=S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
with coordinates
(t,x,y)
and equipped with the product metric
g_{0}=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}
. Take
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=S^{1}\times\{0\}
and
\ell_{0}\to Y_{0}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
the pullback of the mobius bundle on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
by the projection to the second factor.

The twisted spinor bundle of the product spin structure can be identified with
S=\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell_{0}
. A section
\psi\in\Gamma(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell)
may be written as

\psi=e^{i\theta/2}\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{+}\\
\psi^{-}\end{pmatrix}

(3.2)

where
\psi^{\pm}
are
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions and
(r,\theta)
are polar coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Indeed, on each normal plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
, the bundle
\underline{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\ell
can be constructed as the bundle with fiber
\mathbb{C}
glued along two (thickened) rays by the transition functions
+1
and
-1
. Consequently,
e^{i\theta/2}
, gives rise to a global nowhere-vanishing section of this bundle. When a section is written in the form (
3.2
), the connection arising from the spin connection and
\nabla_{A_{0}}
on
\ell_{0}
(with perturbation
B_{0}=0
) is simply
\nabla=\text{d}
. The Dirac operator then takes the form

\not{D}=\begin{pmatrix}i\partial_{t}&-2\partial_{z}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{z}&-i\partial_{t}\end{pmatrix}

(3.3)

where
z=x+iy
. That is to say, it is just the normal spin Dirac operator on
Y_{0}
, but the spinors have an additional
e^{i\theta/2}
term which is differentiated as expected.

Remark 3.1
.

Although it is convenient for computation, the expression (
3.3
) hides the singular nature of the Dirac operator. It can alternatively be written in the following equivalent way which makes the singular nature manifest.

Multiplication
e^{-i\theta/2}:\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes\ell_{0}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
provides an alternative trivialization, in which spinor are written
\psi=(\psi^{+},\psi^{-})

where
\psi^{\pm}
are still
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions. In this trivialization, Dirac operator is instead given by

\not{D}=\begin{pmatrix}i\partial_{t}&-2\partial_{z}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{z}&-i\partial_{t}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma(d\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}i\partial_{t}&-2\partial_{z}\\
2\overline{\partial}_{z}&-i\partial_{t}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{4}\gamma\left(\frac{dz}{z}-\frac{d\overline{z}}{\overline{z}}\right)

where
\gamma
denotes Clifford multiplication. Thus
\not{D}
is a uniformly elliptic operator plus a singular zeroth order term (i.e. one unbounded on
L^{2}
). Equivalently,
r\not{D}
is an elliptic operator with bounded zeroth order term, but
the symbol degenerates along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.
This type of operator is called an elliptic operator with edge-type degeneracies or simply an
elliptic edge operator
. The theory of operators of this type has been studied extensively in microlocal analysis and many results in Section
2
hold in considerable generality (see
[
72
,
47
,
10
,
39
,
18
]
and the references therein).

Example 3.2
.

Let us now identify the
L^{2}
-kernel of
\not{D}
on
Y_{\circ}
(Cf.
[
51
]
Section 3). As in Lemma
2.6
, this also identifies the cokernel of the operator on
rH^{1}_{e}
since
\text{coker}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\simeq\text{ker}(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})
continues to hold. Here, the weight function is given by
r
globally on
Y_{\circ}
. Writing a general section in Fourier series as

\psi=\sum_{k,\ell}e^{i\ell t}e^{i(k+\tfrac{1}{2})\theta}\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{+}_{k,\ell}e^{-i\theta}\\
\psi^{-}_{k,\ell}\ \ \ \ \ \end{pmatrix}

and using the polar expressions

\overline{\partial}_{z}=\frac{1}{2}e^{i\theta}(\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta})\hskip 56.9055pt\overline{\partial}_{z}=\frac{1}{2}e^{-i\theta}(\partial_{r}-\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}),

the Dirac equation (
3.3
) becomes the following system of ODEs for
\psi_{k,\ell}^{\pm}(r)
which decouple for distinct pairs
(k,\ell)
:

\frac{d}{dr}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k,\ell}^{+}\vskip 7.11317pt\\
\psi_{k,\ell}^{-}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{(k-\tfrac{1}{2})}{r}&-\ell\\
-\ell&-\frac{(k+\tfrac{1}{2})}{r}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{k,\ell}^{+}\vskip 7.11317pt\\
\psi_{k,\ell}^{-}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.4)

This system of equations can be solved by substituting the second equation into the first, after which the general solution is given in terms of modified Bessel functions (of the second kind). If
k\neq 0
, the pair
(k,\ell)
admits no solutions in
L^{2}(S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
; for
(k,\ell)=(0,\ell)
with
\ell\neq 0
,

\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}=\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}e^{-|\ell|r}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\\
\frac{\text{sgn}(\ell)}{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}

(3.5)

is an infinite-dimensional set of orthonormalized solutions in
L^{2}
, and
\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})
is their
L^{2}
-closure. Indeed, there can be no other solutions since (
3.5
) and equivalent expression with the modified Bessel function of the first kind (which is not
L^{2}
) exhaust the possible solutions in each Fourier mode by standard ODE theory.

Disregarding the issues of the integrability of the
\ell=0
solutions as
r\to\infty
(which is immaterial in the upcoming case of
Y
compact) and formally including this element leads to an isomorphism

L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})\simeq\text{ker}(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})

(3.6)

defined by the linear extension of
e^{i\ell t}\mapsto\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
. In this example there are no
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors.

There is a second choice of spin structure on
Y_{\circ}=S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
which has monodromy
-1
around the
S^{1}
factor parallel to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in addition to around the meridian. For this second spin structure, spinors may be written with half integer Fourier modes
e^{i\ell t}e^{it/2}
, and the calculation is identical but the solutions are indexed by
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}+\tfrac{1}{2}
. ∎

Example
3.2
suggests that the
L^{2}
-kernel of
\not{D}
on a closed 3-manifold is also infinite-dimensional, and thus the failure to prove Fredholmness of
\not{D}
in Section
2
was not simply a shortcoming of the techniques employed. Indeed, this will be shown to be the case. In fact, besides simply being infinite-dimensional,
\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})
displays the following salient properties in the model case, which generalize to the case of
Y
closed:

Expansion
:

Solutions
\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
have asymptotic expansions with terms
r^{k-\tfrac{1}{2}}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Isomorphism
:

There is an isomorphism
\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\simeq L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{\mathbb{C}})
given by associating a kernel element to each eigenfunction of the Dirac operator
i\partial_{t}
on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

Rapid Decay
:

For eigenvalues
|\ell|>>0
, solutions
\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
decay exponentially away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

The first item follows from the power series expansion of
e^{-|\ell|r}
. The remainder of Section
3
defines and establishes the asymptotic expansions in the first item more precisely, while precise statements and proofs of the second and third items are the subject of Section
4
.

Remark 3.3
.

There are no
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors in Example
3.2
because there are no solutions with finite
rH^{1}_{e}
-norm. There are, however, still explicit solutions given in terms of modified Bessel functions for
(k,\ell)=(\pm 1,\ell)
which have leading order
z^{1/2}
and
\overline{z}^{1/2}
which lie in
rH^{1}_{loc}
near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
but grow exponentially as
r\to\infty
. Therefore, intuitively, the existence of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor on a closed manifold
Y
is a rare phenomenon and occurs only when one of these exponentially growing solutions can be patched together with a bounded solution on the complement of a neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in
Y
.

3.2.
Local Expressions

From here on, we return to the case that
(Y,g_{0})
is a closed, oriented Riemannian 3-manifold and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
a smoothly embedded link. In order to write local expressions, we endow a tubular neighborhood
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
of (a component of)
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
with a particular set of coordinates.

Let
\gamma:S^{1}\to\mathcal{Z}_{j}
be an arclength parameterization of a chosen component
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
whose length is denoted by
|\mathcal{Z}_{j}|
, and fix a global orthonormal frame
\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
of the pullback
\gamma^{*}N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
of the normal bundle to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. We are free to arrange that
\{\dot{\gamma},n_{1},n_{2}\}
is an oriented frame of
TY
along
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
.

Definition 3.4
.

A system of
Fermi coordinates
for
r_{0}<r_{\text{inj}}
where
r_{\text{inj}}
is the injectivity radius of
Y
is the diffeomorphism
S^{1}\times D_{r_{0}}\simeq N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})
for a chosen component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
given by

(t,x,y)\mapsto\text{Exp}_{\gamma(t)}(xn_{1}+yn_{2}),

where
t\in(0,|\mathcal{Z}_{j}|]
is the arclength coordinate on
\mathcal{Z}_{j}\simeq S^{1}
. In these coordinates the Riemannian metric
g_{0}
can be written

g_{0}=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}\ +\ O(r)

(3.7)

Given such a coordinate system,
(t,r,\theta)
are used to denote the corresponding cylindrical coordinates, and
(t,z,\overline{z})
the complex ones on the
D_{r_{0}}
factor.

Remark 3.5
.

There are different conventions on the usage of “Fermi coordinates” in the literature, with some requiring that the curve is question is a geodesic. In that situation,
n_{x}
and
n_{y}
can be chosen to locally solve an ODE so that
g=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}+O(r^{2})
. Here, we make no such assumption and the difference from the product metric is
O(r)
. Explicitly, the correction to the product metric is

\big(2x\mathfrak{m}_{x}(t)+2y\mathfrak{m}_{y}(t)\big)\ dt^{2}\ +\ \big(\mu(t)y\big)\ dtdx\ +\ \big(-\mu(t)x\big)\ dtdy\ +\ O(r^{2})

where
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}(t)=\langle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}\dot{\gamma},n_{\alpha}\rangle
for
\alpha=x,y
and
\mu(t)=\langle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}n_{x},n_{y}\rangle=-\langle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}n_{y},n_{x}\rangle.

A choice of Fermi coordinates induces a trivialization of the frame bundle of
Y
on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
as follows: it is given by the global orthonormal frame
\{e_{t},e_{1},e_{2}\}
uniquely defined by the property that it restricts to
\{\partial_{t},\partial_{x},\partial_{y}\}
along
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
and is defined by radial parallel transport for
0<r<r_{0}
. There are two possibilities for the isomorphism class of the restricted spin structure:

Case 1:

The spin structure restricts to the product
\mathfrak{s}_{0}|_{N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})}\simeq N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})\times\text{Spin}(3)
, so that

S|_{N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes\ell_{0}

(3.8)

Case 2:

The spin structure restricts to
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
as the double cover of
Fr(Y)|_{N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})}\simeq N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})\times SO(3)
that is non-trivial in the
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
factor, so that

S|_{N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})}\simeq\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\otimes\ell_{t}\otimes\ell_{0}

(3.9)

where
\ell_{t}
is the pullback of the mobius bundle on
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
.

It is worth noting that, in general, there are some rather subtle topological restrictions on which combinations of Case 1 and Case 2 can occur when
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
has multiple components. For instance, if
Y=S^{3}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
has a single component, then the unique spin structure on
S^{3}
always restricts to Case 2 on a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
; if
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
has multiple components then the number which fall in Case 1 must be even.

First consider Case 1. The trivialization (
3.8
) may chosen so that the factors of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
are given by the
\pm i
eigenspaces of
\gamma(e^{t})
, in which case Clifford multiplication is given by

\gamma(e^{t})=\sigma_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix}\hskip 42.67912pt\gamma(e^{1})=\sigma_{x}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\hskip 42.67912pt\gamma(e^{2})=\sigma_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}.

As in the model case (Example
3.2
), spinors can be written in this trivialization in the form (
3.2
) where
\psi^{\pm}
are
\mathbb{C}
-valued function on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{j})
. In Case 2, the same holds after changing the trivialization by
e^{i\ell t/2}
which alters the Dirac operator by
\tfrac{i}{2}\gamma(dt)
. This leads to the following:

Lemma 3.6
.

In both Case (1) and Case (2), the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-Dirac operator in local coordinates around a component
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and the above trivialization takes the form

\not{D}=\not{D}_{\circ}+\mathfrak{d}

where

•

\not{D}_{\circ}
is the Dirac operator in the product metric on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})
, given by (
3.3
)

•

\mathfrak{d}
is a first order perturbation arising from the
O(r)
terms of
g_{0}
, the perturbation
B_{0}
and
\tfrac{i}{2}\gamma(dt)
in Case 2, so that

|\mathfrak{d}\psi|\leq C(r|\nabla\psi|+|\psi|)

holds pointwise.∎

3.3.
Asymptotic Expansions

This subsection establishes that
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors have local power series expansions by half integer powers of
r
. These result follow from the general regularity theory for elliptic edge operators in
[
47
]
.

Fix a choice of Fermi coordinates near each component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

Definition 3.7
.

A spinor
\psi\in L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)
is said to admit a
polyhomogenous expansion
with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}
if

\psi\sim\sum_{n,p\geq 0}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\begin{pmatrix}\ \ c_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \\
\ \ d_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \end{pmatrix}r^{n+1/2}\log(r)^{p}e^{-i\theta/2}

where
c_{n,k,p}(t),d_{n,k,p}(t)\in C^{\infty}(S^{1};\mathbb{\mathbb{C}})
, and where
\sim
denotes convergence in the following sense: for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the partial sums

\psi_{N}=\sum_{n\leq N}\sum_{k=-2n}^{2n+1}\sum_{p\leq n-1}\begin{pmatrix}\ \ c_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \\
\ \ d_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \end{pmatrix}r^{n+1/2}\log(r)^{p}e^{-i\theta/2}

satisfy the pointwise bounds

\displaystyle|\psi-\psi_{N}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{N}r^{N+1+\tfrac{1}{4}}\hskip 56.9055pt|\nabla_{t}^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}(\psi-\psi_{N})|\leq C_{N,\alpha,\beta}r^{N+1+\tfrac{1}{4}-|\beta|}

(3.10)

for constants
C_{N,\alpha,\beta}
determined by the background data and choice of local coordinates and trivialization. Here,
\beta
is a multi-index of derivatives in the directions normal to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

The work of Mazzeo
[
47
]
implies the following regularity result about
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors (see also Appendix A of
[
26
]
).

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose that
\Phi_{0}\in rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor. Then
\Phi_{0}
admits a polyhomogenous expansion with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}
. Moreover,
c_{n,k,p}
and
d_{n,k,p}
vanish unless
-2n\leq k\leq 2n+1
and
p\leq n-1
. Thus
\Phi_{0}
has a local expression

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}c(t)\sqrt{z}\\
d(t)\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}+\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k=-2n}^{2n+1}\sum_{p=0}^{n-1}\begin{pmatrix}\ \ c_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \\
\ \ d_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \end{pmatrix}r^{n+1/2}\log(r)^{p}e^{-i\theta/2}

(3.11)

where
c(t),d(t),c_{k,m,n}(t),d_{k,m,n}(t)\in C^{\infty}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
. In this form, non-degeneracy in the sense of Definition
1.3
is equivalent to the requirement that
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
is nowhere-vanishing. The same result holds for an
rH^{1}_{e}
-solution of the operator
\not{D}-\lambda\text{Id}
.

Proof.

The existence of such an expansion is a consequence of the regularity theory in
[
47
]
(Section 7, Proposition 7.17) and the fact that the indicial roots are
j+\tfrac{1}{2}
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
in this case. See also
[
23
,
26
]
. The constraints on the expansion compared to Definition
3.7
then follow from writing the equation
\not{D}\Phi_{0}-\lambda\Phi_{0}=0
in Fermi coordinates as

\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\partial\\
2\overline{\partial}&0\end{pmatrix}\Phi_{0}=-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{0}-\begin{pmatrix}-i\partial_{t}&0\\
0&i\partial_{t}\end{pmatrix}\Phi_{0}+\lambda\Phi_{0}

with
\mathfrak{d}
as in Lemma
3.6
, and formally solving term by term. ∎

The expansion (
3.11
) depends on the choice of Fermi coordinates in the following way. Another choice of Fermi coordinates arises from an alternative choice of normal frame
n_{x},n_{y}
. This change of frame may be taken to be the descent of a change of trivialization of the spin structure, thus may be written in complex coordinates on
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as

n_{1}+in_{2}\mapsto e^{-2i\sigma(t)}(n_{1}+in_{2})

where
\sigma(t):\mathcal{Z}_{0}\to S^{1}
(the minus sign in the exponent is due to the convention that Clifford multiplication is by cotangent vectors). The new complex coordinates
(t,z^{\prime},z^{\prime})
resulting from such a transformation are likewise related to the original coordinates by

(t,z^{\prime},\overline{z}^{\prime})=(t,e^{-2i\sigma(t)}z,e^{2i\sigma(t)}\overline{z}^{\prime}).

This shows the following:

Corollary 3.9
.

For a term of a polyhomogenous expansion

\psi(t,z,\overline{z})=\begin{pmatrix}\ a(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \\
\ b(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \end{pmatrix}r^{n+1/2}\log(r)^{p}e^{-i\theta/2}

the coefficients are naturally sections
a(t)\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{-k})
and
b(t)\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{-k+1})
. In particular, the leading coefficients
c(t),d(t)
of (
3.11
) are sections of
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{-1},N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
respectively. ∎

Remark 3.10
.

More generally,
L^{2}
kernel elements elements have similar asymptotic expansions, but it is no longer necessarily the case that the coefficients are smooth. In general, the coefficients only make sense as distributions (see Section 7 of
[
47
]
for a more general discussion). If
\psi\in\text{ker}(\not{D})\cap L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)
, then it admits a
weak asymptotic expansion
of the form

\psi\sim\begin{pmatrix}\frac{c_{0}(t)}{\sqrt{z}}\\
\frac{d_{0}(t)}{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}\ +\ \sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k=-2n-1}^{2n+2}\sum_{p=0}^{n-1}\begin{pmatrix}\ \ c_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \\
\ \ d_{n,k,p}(t)e^{ik\theta}\ \ \end{pmatrix}r^{n-1/2}\log(r)^{p}e^{-i\theta/2}

where
c_{n,k,p},d_{n,k,p}\in L^{-1/2-n}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
are understood in a distributional sense and are sections of an appropriate power of
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as in Corollary
3.9
.
There is no nice sense in which these weak expansions converge
. In particular, if
\psi\in L^{2}
has such an expansion, then the difference
|\psi-\psi_{N}|
will not necessarily lie in
L^{2}
. Consequently, there is no meaningful sense in which the later terms are “smaller” than the earlier ones. If there were stronger notions of convergence for such weak asymptotic expansions, it is possible that Theorem
1.5
could be proved without the use of Nash-Moser theory.

4.
The Obstruction Space

This section studies the infinite-dimensional cokernel of the operator

\not{D}:rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)\longrightarrow L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S),

(4.1)

which coincides with
\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})
by Lemma
2.6
. The main results of this section, Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
generalizes the three key properties noted below Example
3.2
to the case of a compact manifold.

Definition 4.1
.

Define the
Obstruction Space
associated to the data
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},g_{0},B_{0})
by

\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}):=\{\psi\in L^{2}\ |\ \psi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\}.

In addition, define
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}=\{\psi\in\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})\ |\ \langle\psi,\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}}=0\ \forall\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}_{rH^{1}_{e}})\}
. The
L^{2}
-orthogonal projections to
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}),\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
are denoted
\Pi_{0},\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
respectively.

Although this definition appears to be a redundant renaming of
\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})
, it is made in preparation for Section
8
. There, the obstruction space will be extended to a vector bundle over the data
(\mathcal{Z},g,B)
whereas
\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})
may not be locally trivial (analogously to the finite-dimensional case where the dimension may jump).

The upcoming Proposition
4.3
provides an isomorphism between
\text{\bf{Ob}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
and the space of sections of a vector bundle on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
(cf. the discussion following Theorem
1.4
). The fibers of this vector bundle are given as follows. Let
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}
denote the trivial bundle with fiber
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Sections of each summand may be decomposed in Fourier series (using the orientation given by the fixed choice of Fermi coordinates in Section
3.3
; denote by
H
the
modified Hilbert transform
, i.e. the pseudo-differential operator on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
whose symbol is given by
\text{sgn}(\ell)
where
\ell
is the Fourier index.

Definition 4.2
.

Define the
Calderón Subbundle

\mathcal{C}_{0}\subseteq\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\to\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as the trivial complex line bundle given by the first summand, and the
Calderón Subspace
as the subspace

\Lambda_{\circ}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\xi(t)\\
H\xi(t)\end{pmatrix}\ \Big|\ \xi\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\right\}\subseteq L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C}^{2}).

Clearly, there is a canonical isomorphism
\Lambda_{\circ}\simeq L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
.

The first main result of Section
4
is the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

There is an isomorphism

\text{ob}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\longrightarrow\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}.

(4.2)

It follows that
\text{ob}\oplus\iota:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\to\text{\bf{Ob}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is an isomorphism, where
\iota
is the inclusion of the second factor.

Proposition
4.3
may be viewed as a Poisson extension result for a “codimension 2” boundary-value problem. Although the proof does not require making these notions precise, the heuristic guides the remainder of Section
4
and is worth describing in some detail (see
[
36
]
for a formal theory of boundary-value problems for edge operators). There is a “boundary trace” operator

\text{tr}:\text{\bf{Ob}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}\longrightarrow H^{-1/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C}^{2})

given by taking the leading coefficients
(c_{0}(t),d_{0}(t))
in the polyhomogenous expansion in Remark
3.10
. The fact that these leading coefficients are sections of the trivial bundle is a consequence of Corollary
3.9
. More generally, the leading coefficients are valued in a “trace bundle” defined precisely in
[
36
, Thm. 2.5]
, but by a coincidence of the transformation rule for spinors and the exponents in the expansions of Remark
3.10
, this bundle is trivial in our case.

Example
3.2
shows that on
Y_{\circ}=S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\text{tr}:\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})\to\Lambda_{\circ}
is an isomorphism (to the closure in
H^{-1/2}
), with inverse given by the Poisson extension operator

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}:H^{-1/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\ker(\not{D}|_{L^{2}})

(4.3)

\displaystyle\xi(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}|\ell|^{-1/2}\langle\xi(t),e^{i\ell t}\rangle\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}

(4.4)

where
\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
are as in Equation (
3.5
), and where we implicitly use the isomorphism in Definition
4.2
. Note the regularization factor
|\ell|^{-1/2}
is needed because
\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
is defined to be normalized in
L^{2}
. More generally, on a compact manifold, there is a Calderón subspace
\Lambda=\text{tr}(\text{\bf Ob}^{\perp}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}))\subseteq H^{-1/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C}^{2})
(Lagrangian with respect to the natural symplectic form on
H^{-1/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C}^{2})
) and a Poisson operator
\mathfrak{P}:\Lambda\to{\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}^{\perp}
giving its inverse.

The Calderón subspace
\Lambda_{\circ}
for the model operator has the pleasing property that it is canonically identified with the space of sections of the vector bundle
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. In general, the Calderón subspace
\Lambda
has no reason to have such an identification, and in order to view the operator of Theorem
1.4
as a pseudodifferential operator, one must chose such an identification. The diagram below,
which does not commute
, depicts two natural (inequivalent) choices for identifying the model Calderón subspace
\Lambda_{\circ}
with
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
.

\Lambda_{\circ}
\cancel{\circlearrowleft}
\Lambda
L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
\pi_{\Lambda}
\chi\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
\mathfrak{P}

In the upper pathway,
\pi_{\Lambda}
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection to
\Lambda
in
H^{-1/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C}^{2})
, and
\mathfrak{P}
is the Poisson extension operator on
Y
. In the lower pathway,
\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}
is the model Poisson extension operator on
Y_{\circ}
pasted onto the compact
Y
with a cut-off function
\chi
supported near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection to
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
from Definition
4.1
. The above is to say that the two natural choices are to project to
\Lambda
in the boundary space then extend, or first extend using the model Poisson operator and then project on the 3-manifold
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. While the first option (top path) is arguably more natural from the perspective of
[
36
]
, it leads to difficulties showing certain error terms arising from the metric are bounded (let alone compact). To prove Proposition
4.3
, the second (bottom path) was found to be more robust, and the definition of the map
ob
is a minor modification of the composition
\text{ob}=\Pi_{0}^{\perp}\circ\chi\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}
.

In this approach, we also use the
L^{2}
(rather than
H^{-1/2}
) normalization to eliminate the normalization factor in (
4.4
). (See also Remark
6.3
).

The map
ob
provides, in a very loose sense, some sort of “coordinates” on the obstruction by identifying with the easily-described space
\Lambda_{\circ}
. To calculate the image of a spinor
\text{ob}^{-1}\circ\Pi_{0}^{\perp}\Psi
in
\Lambda_{\circ}
, one may choose a basis. Associated to our choice of the bottom pathway in the above diagram, there is a natural basis for
\text{\bf{Ob}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
, given by the image of Fourier modes. Let
e^{i\ell t_{j}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
denote the
\ell^{th}
Fourier mode on the
j^{th}
component of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, where
\ell\in{2\pi\mathbb{Z}}/{|\mathcal{Z}_{j}|}
. Given Proposition
4.3
, set

\Psi_{j\ell}=\text{ob}(e^{i\ell t_{j}}).

This basis satisfies the following. In the upcoming proposition statement, we tacitly assume that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
consists of a single component and omit the subscript
j

Proposition 4.4
.

(A) When
\not{D}
is complex linear, the
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
-component of a spinor
\psi\in L^{2}
under (
4.2
) is given by

\text{ob}^{-1}(\Pi_{0}^{\perp}\psi)=\sum_{\ell}\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}}\phi_{\ell}.\hskip 85.35826pt\iota^{-1}(\psi)=\sum_{k}\langle\psi,\Phi_{k}\rangle\Phi_{k}.

(4.5)

where
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}
is the hermitian inner product, and
\Phi_{k}
a (real) basis of
\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
. Moreover,

\Psi_{\ell}=\chi\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\zeta_{j\ell}+\xi_{\ell}

where

•

\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}

are the
L^{2}
-orthonormalized Euclidean obstruction elements from Example
3.2
(in the trivialization
3.6
) and
\chi
is a cutoff function supported on a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

•

\zeta_{\ell}
is a perturbation with
L^{2}
-norm
O(|\ell|^{-1})
which decays exponentially away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in the following sense:

\|\zeta_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C}{|\ell|}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{n}{c_{1}}\right).

(4.6)

where
A_{n\ell}
denotes the collection of annuli

A_{n\ell}=\left\{\tfrac{n}{|\ell|}R_{0}\leq r\leq\tfrac{n+1}{|\ell|}R_{0}\right\}

(4.7)

for some constant
R_{0}
, and
r
denotes the geodesic distance to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Additionally, in Fermi coordinates on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and in the trivialization of Lemma
3.6
,
\zeta_{\ell}
is a linear combination of only Fourier modes
e^{ipt}
in the range
\ell-{|\ell|}/{2}\leq p\leq\ell+{|\ell|}/{2}
.

•

\xi_{\ell}
is a perturbation of
L^{2}
-norm
O(|\ell|^{-2})
i.e. satisfying

\|\xi_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{|\ell|^{2}}

for a universal constant
C
.

(B) In the case that
\not{D}
is only
\mathbb{R}
-linear,
\Psi_{l}^{\text{Re}}=\text{ob}(\text{Re}(\varphi_{\ell}))
and likewise for the imaginary part form a real basis and

\Psi_{\ell}^{\text{Re}}=\chi\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\zeta^{\text{Re}}_{\ell}+\xi^{\text{Re}}_{\ell}\hskip 56.9055pt\Psi_{\ell}^{\text{Im}}=i(\chi\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ})+\zeta^{\text{Im}}_{\ell}+\xi^{\text{Im}}_{\ell}

satisfying identical bounds where the inner product in (
4.5
) is replaced by

\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}=\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}^{\text{Re}}\rangle\ +\ i\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}^{\text{Im}}\rangle.

Moreover, in the case that
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
has multiple components, either (A) or (B) holds mutatis mutandis with an additional index
j
ranging over the components of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

The reader is cautioned that the basis
\Psi_{\ell}
is not necessarily orthogonal, and cannot be orthogonalized without disrupting the decay properties in the second bullet point.

The remainder of Section
4
proves Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
concurrently. Section
4.1
studies the Poisson extension on a normal neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and proves a preliminary version of Proposition
4.4
. Sections
4.2
and
4.3
construct the map
ob
and show, respectively, that it is Fredholm and has index zero. Section
4.4
makes a compact correction so that
ob
is an isomorphism, thereby completing the proofs. Section
4.5
discusses the higher-regularity analogues of both propositions.

Notation 4.5
.

Throughout the remainder of the section we tacitly assume (i)
\not{D}
is complex linear, and (ii)
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
consists of a single component. The proof in the real-linear and multi-component situation is a trivial extension. In the remainder of the section we make the following conventions to avoid cluttering notation:

(i)

The subscript
j
is omitted, and
\langle-,-\rangle
denotes the Hermitian inner product.

(ii)

The data
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},g_{0},B_{0},S_{0},\ell_{0},\nabla_{0},\gamma_{0})
is fixed and the subscript
0
is omitted.

(iii)

The subscript
\circ
denotes the structures in (ii) in the model case of Example
3.2
.

(iv)

The subscript
N
denotes the structures in (ii) on a tubular nbhd. of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
(cf. Section
4.1
).

(v)

The choice of Fermi coordinates from Section
3.3
is fixed throughout.

(vi)

The ‘pullback’ normalization on
Y
is used so that the domain of
ob
is
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
( not
H^{-1/2}
).

4.1.
The Model Obstruction

This section proves a preliminary version of Propositions
4.3
-
4.4
on the normal bundle
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Choose
r_{N}>0
small, and let
\chi_{N}
be a cut-off function vanishing for
r>r_{N}
and equal to 1 for
r<r_{N}/2
. Set

\displaystyle(N,g_{N})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(N\mathcal{Z}_{0}\ ,\ \chi_{N}g_{0}+(1-\chi_{N})g_{\circ})

(4.8)

\displaystyle B_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\chi_{N}B_{0}

(4.9)

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(1-\not{D}_{N}P_{N}\not{D}_{N})

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\ell}^{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{N}(e^{i\ell t})

(4.12)

where
g_{\circ}=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}
is the product metric,
\not{D}_{N},P_{N}
are formed using
(g_{N},B_{N})
,
\Pi_{N}
is the projection to the obstruction as in Corollary
2.8
, and and
\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}(e^{i\ell t})=\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
is the
L^{2}
-normalized version of (
4.4
) so that
\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\ker(\not{D}_{\circ}|_{L^{2}})
is an isomorphism.

The Dirac operator may be written

\not{D}_{N}:=\not{D}_{\circ}+\mathfrak{d}

with
\not{D}_{\circ},\mathfrak{d}
as in Lemma
3.6
. If the spin structure falls in Case 2 as in (
3.2
), then we truncate
\gamma(idt/2)
to
\chi_{N}\gamma(idt/2)
for convenience. The preliminary version of Propositions
4.3
–
4.4
is the following.

Lemma 4.6
.

For
r_{N}
sufficiently small,
