\mathfrak{P}_{N}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})_{0}\to\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})

is an isomorphism, where
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})_{0}
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal complement of the constant Fourier mode.

Remark 4.7
.

Lemma
4.6
implicitly includes the assertion that
\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{rH^{1}})=0
. The
\ell=0
mode is omitted simply because the
r^{-1/2}
asymptotics fail to be
L^{2}
on the non-compact space
N
. These modes are treated separately in the index calculation in Section
4.3
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.6
,
\mathfrak{d}
satisfies

|\mathfrak{d}\varphi|\leq C(r|\nabla\varphi|+|\varphi|)

(4.13)

pointwise on
\text{supp}(\chi_{N})
, and
\mathfrak{d}=0
elsewhere, hence
\|\mathfrak{d}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\leq Cr_{N}\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}
. Thus for
r_{N}
sufficiently small,

\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}\leq C\|\not{D}_{\circ}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|(\not{D}_{\circ}+\mathfrak{d})\varphi\|_{L^{2}}+C\|\mathfrak{d}\|_{L^{2}}\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow\hskip 28.45274pt\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}}\leq C^{\prime}\|\not{D}_{N}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}.

Consequently,
\ker(\not{D}_{N}|{rH^{1}_{e}})=0
and

\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}:rH^{1}_{e}\to rH^{-1}_{e}

is an isomorphism by Lemma
2.7
with inverse
P_{N}
.

Since
\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})_{0}\to\ker(\not{D}_{\circ}|_{L^{2}})
is an isomorphism by Example
3.2
, in order to show that
\mathfrak{P}_{N}
is an isomorphism it suffices to show that
\Pi_{N}:\ker(\not{D}_{\circ}|_{L^{2}})\to\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})
is an isomorphism, where
\Pi_{N}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{N}
are as in (
4.10
–
4.11
). For injectivity, observe that for
\Psi\in\ker(\not{D}_{\circ}|_{L^{2}})
,

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\text{Id}-\not{D}_{\circ}P_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ}\Psi\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\text{Id}-\not{D}_{N}P_{N}\not{D}_{N}\Psi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\not{D}_{N}P_{N}\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{\circ}P_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ}\Psi\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{N}\Psi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\not{D}_{N}P_{N}\mathfrak{d}\Psi\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{N}\Psi\|_{L^{2}}+Cr_{N}\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}},

where the last inequality follows from integrating
\|\mathfrak{d}\Psi\|_{r^{-1}H^{-1}}=\sup\langle\mathfrak{d}\Psi,\varphi\rangle
by parts and applying (
4.13
) for
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
, then using the fact that
\not{D}_{N},P_{N}
are bounded. For
r_{N}
sufficiently small, it follows that
\Pi_{N}
is injective with closed range on
\ker(\not{D}_{\circ}|_{L^{2}})
.

For surjectivity, we argue by contraction: suppose that there were
\eta\in\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})
such that
\langle\Pi_{N}\Psi,\eta\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
held for all
\Psi\in\ker(\not{D}_{\circ}|_{L^{2}})
. Assume that
\|\eta\|_{L^{2}}=1
is normalized. Since
\eta\in\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})
, it is orthogonal to
\text{ran}(\not{D}_{N}|_{rH^{1}})
, thus writing
\not{D}_{N}=\not{D}_{\circ}+\mathfrak{d}
we have

0=\langle\not{D}_{N}\varphi,\eta\rangle\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ |\langle\not{D}_{\circ}\varphi,\eta\rangle|=|\langle\mathfrak{d}\varphi,\eta\rangle|\leq Cr_{N}\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}},

i.e. the component of
\eta
in the range of
\not{D}_{\circ}|_{rH^{1}}
is small. Consequently, there is a
\Psi_{\circ}\in\ker(\not{D}_{\circ}|_{L^{2}})
such that
\eta=\Psi_{\circ}+w
with
\|w\|_{L^{2}}\leq Cr_{N}
, (hence
1-Cr_{N}\leq\|\Psi_{\circ}\|_{L^{2}}
). But this would imply that

0=\langle\Pi_{N}\Psi_{\circ},\eta\rangle=\langle\Psi_{\circ}+\not{D}_{N}P_{N}\mathfrak{d}\Psi_{\circ}\ ,\ \Psi_{\circ}+w\rangle\geq 1-C^{\prime}r_{N},

a contradiction once
r_{N}
is sufficiently small.
∎

The next lemma is analogue of Proposition
4.4
on
(N,g_{N})
:

Lemma 4.8
.

For
r_{N}
sufficiently small, the set
\{\Psi_{\ell}^{N}\}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
form a basis of
\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})
, and may be written

\Psi_{\ell}^{N}=\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\zeta_{\ell}^{N}+\xi_{\ell}^{N}

where
\zeta_{\ell}^{N},\xi_{\ell}^{N}
satisfy the conclusions of Proposition
4.4
.

The proof of Lemma
4.8
is an iteration argument bootstrapping the decay of certain error terms. Since
\mathfrak{d}=O(r)
, and the initial error term has size
\|O(r)\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}\|_{L^{2}}=O(|\ell|^{-1})
, which follows from direct integration of
r^{k}e^{-|\ell|r}
for the correct power of
k
. The iteration process corrects the error term by first solving for the error using
\not{D}_{\circ}
. Provided the corrected solution retains the exponential decay properties of
\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
, the new error terms picks up a factor of
r
, thus a factor of
|\ell|^{-1}
after integration. The process may then be iterated to obtain arbitrarily large powers. Lemma
4.8
(cf Proposition
4.4
) employ the first stage of the iteration to obtain an exponentially decaying correction
\zeta_{\ell}
and a
O(|\ell|^{-2})
correction
\xi_{\ell}
. Corollary
4.10
continues the iteration to obtain higher regularity estimates.

The iteration process relies on the following lemma, which is applied repeatedly to conclude that corrected solutions in the proof of Lemma
4.8
retain the desired exponential decay properties. Morally, it should be viewed as a statement about the exponential decay of the Green’s function of
\not{D}_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ}
in certain Fourier modes and is proved in Appendix
A
using a discrete maximum principle argument due to Taubes
[
33
, App. A.2.1.]
(which serves as a proxy for explicit computations with the Green’s function). For the statement of the lemma,
A_{n\ell}
denotes the sequence of annuli (
4.7
) from Part (B) of Proposition
4.3
, and we set
B_{n\ell}=A_{(n-1)\ell}\cup A_{n\ell}\cup A_{(n+1)\ell}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
m
be a non-negative integer, and assume that
|\ell|\geq 2m
. Suppose that
u_{\ell}\in rH^{1}_{e}(N)
is the unique solution of

\not{D}_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ}u_{\ell}=f_{\ell}

(4.14)

where
f_{\ell}\in r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}
satisfies the following two properties:

(1)

f_{\ell}
has only Fourier modes in
e^{ipt}
for
p
in the range

\ell-L_{0}\leq p\leq\ell+L_{0}

(4.15)

where
|L_{0}|\leq|\ell|/2
.

(2)

For
m
as above, there are constants
C_{m},c_{m}
independent of
\ell
such that
f_{\ell}
satisfies the bounds

\|f_{\ell}\|^{2}_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}(B_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C_{m}}{|\ell|^{2+2m}}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{2n}{c_{m}}\right)

(4.16)

on the sequence of annuli
B_{n\ell}
.

Then there are constants
C^{\prime}_{m},c_{m}^{\prime}
independent of
\ell
such that
u_{\ell}
similarly satisfies

\|u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}_{m}}{|\ell|^{2+2m}}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{2n}{c_{m}^{\prime}}\right).

(4.17)

Moreover,
u_{\ell}
has only Fourier modes in the same range as
f_{\ell}
. ∎

We now prove Lemma
4.8
using Lemma
4.9
:

Proof.

Let
\Psi_{N}
be as defined by (
4.12
). With
\not{D}_{N}=\not{D}_{0}+\mathfrak{d}
as in Lemma
3.6
,
\mathfrak{d}
can be explicitly written in the form

\mathfrak{d}=\sum_{ij=1}^{3}a_{i,j}(t,x,y)\sigma_{i}\partial_{j}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\Gamma_{k}(t,x,y)\sigma_{k}

where
|a_{ij}|\leq Cr
and
|\Gamma|\leq C
and
\sigma_{i}=\gamma(e^{i})
with
\sigma_{0}=I
in the second sum. Decomposing
a_{ij}(t,x,y),\Gamma_{k}(t,x,y)
into the Fourier modes in the
t
-direction on
N\simeq S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, this operator can be written as

\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{d}^{\text{low}}+\mathfrak{d}^{\text{high}}

where
\mathfrak{d}^{\text{low}}
consists of the Fourier modes of
a_{ij},\Gamma_{k}
with Fourier index
|p|\leq|\ell|/4
.

Since
\Psi^{N}_{\ell}=\Pi_{N}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}=(\text{Id}-\not{D}_{N}P_{N}\not{D}_{N})\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
by definition,

\displaystyle\Psi^{N}_{\ell}-\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\not{D}_{N}P_{N}(\mathfrak{d}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ})=-\not{D}_{N}P_{N}(f_{\ell}^{\text{low}}+f_{\ell}^{\text{high}})

where
f_{\ell}^{\text{low}}:=\mathfrak{d}^{\text{low}}\Psi_{\ell}^{N}
and
f_{\ell}^{\text{high}}:=\mathfrak{d}^{\text{high}}\Psi_{\ell}^{N}
. Set

\displaystyle\zeta_{\ell}^{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\not{D}_{N}u_{\ell}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{where }\hskip 28.45274ptu_{\ell}:=-P_{\circ}(f_{\ell}^{\text{low}})

\displaystyle\xi_{\ell}^{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\not{D}_{N}v_{\ell}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{where }\hskip 28.45274ptv_{\ell}:=-P_{N}(f_{\ell}^{\text{high}}-(\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ})u_{\ell})

so that
(\zeta_{\ell}^{N}+\xi_{\ell}^{N})=\Psi_{\ell}^{N}-\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
as intended, since
u_{\ell}+v_{\ell}
satisfies
\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}(u_{\ell}+v_{\ell})=f_{\ell}^{\text{low}}+f_{\ell}^{\text{high}}
.

The desired decay properties for
\zeta_{\ell}^{N}
follow from applying Lemma
4.9
in the case that
m=0
. To elaborate, the first hypothesis of that lemma is satisfied by construction, because
f_{\ell}^{\text{low}}=\mathfrak{d}^{\text{low}}\Psi_{\ell}^{N}
was defined to be the Fourier modes in the necessary range. To verify the second hypothesis, observe that

\|f_{\ell}^{\text{low}}\|_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}(B_{n,\ell})}\leq\sup_{\|u\|=1}\langle u,f_{\ell}^{\text{low}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq\sup_{\|u\|=1}\|u\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}\|rf_{\ell}^{\text{low}}\|_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}\leq\|rf_{\ell}^{\text{low}}\|_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}

hence using the bounds
|a_{ij}|\leq Cr
and
|\Gamma_{k}|\leq C
for
\mathfrak{d}^{\text{low}}
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{n\ell}}r^{2}|f_{\ell}^{\text{low}}|^{2}\ dV

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{n^{2}}{|\ell|^{2}}R_{0}^{2}\int_{B_{n\ell}}|r\nabla_{j}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}|^{2}+|r\nabla_{t}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}|^{2}+|\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}|^{2}\ rdrd\theta dt

(4.18)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{n^{2}}{|\ell|^{2}}R_{0}^{2}\int_{B_{n\ell}}(1+r^{2}|\ell|^{2}+1)\frac{e^{-2|\ell|r}}{r}|\ell|\ rdrd\theta dt

(4.19)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{n^{5}}{|\ell|^{2}}R_{0}^{5}e^{-nR_{0}}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{|\ell|^{2}}e^{-2n/c_{1}}.

(4.20)

where
R_{0}
is as in the second bullet of Proposition
4.4
. Thus we conclude from Lemma
4.9
and the fact that
\not{D}_{N}
is bounded that

\displaystyle\|u_{\ell}\|_{rH^{1}_{e}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C_{0}}{|\ell|}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{n}{c_{0}}\right)\hskip 42.67912pt\Rightarrow\hskip 42.67912pt\|\zeta^{N}_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C_{0}}{|\ell|}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{n}{c_{0}}\right)

as desired.

It remains to show the asserted bound on
\xi_{\ell}^{N}
holds. Since
\not{D}_{N}:rH^{1}_{e}\to L^{2}
and
P_{N}:rH^{-1}_{e}\to rH^{1}_{e}
are bounded, it suffices to show that

\|f_{\ell}^{\text{high}}-(\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ})u_{\ell}\|_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}}\leq\frac{C}{|\ell|^{2}}.

(4.21)

Addressing the two terms on the left separately, one has
\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{0}\not{D}_{0}=\mathfrak{d}\not{D}_{0}+\not{D}_{0}\mathfrak{d}+\mathfrak{d}^{2}
which shows

\|(\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{0}\not{D}_{0})u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}}\leq C\sum_{n}\sup_{A_{n\ell}}(r^{2}\|u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(A_{n\ell})})\leq\frac{C}{|\ell|^{4}}.

(4.22)

For
f_{\ell}^{\text{high}}
, note that the coefficients
a_{ij},\Gamma_{k}
are smooth and
\mathfrak{d}^{\text{high}}
and have only Fourier modes
p
with
|p|\geq|\ell|/4
. Applying the Sobolev embedding for each fixed
(x,y)
therefore shows that

\|a^{\text{high}}\|_{C^{0}(Y)}\leq\sup_{x,y}\|a^{\text{high}}(t)\|_{C^{0}(S^{1})}\leq C\sup_{x,y}\|a^{\text{high}}(t)\|_{H^{1}(S^{1})}\leq\frac{C}{|\ell|^{2}}\sup_{x,y}\|a^{\text{high}}(t)\|_{H^{3}(S^{1})}\leq\frac{C}{|\ell|^{2}}

(4.23)

and likewise for
\Gamma^{\text{high}}
. Combining the bounds (
4.22
) and (
4.23
) shows (
4.21
), completing the proof.
∎

The above procedure may be iterated to bootstrap the bounds on
\xi^{N}_{\ell}
without disrupting the bounds on
\zeta_{\ell}^{N}
. In the following statement,
\nabla_{z}
is tacitly used to denote a covariant derivative in a direction normal to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and
\nabla_{t}
a tangential one.

Corollary 4.10
.

For every
m
there is an alternative decomposition

\zeta_{\ell}^{N}+\xi_{\ell}^{N}=\zeta_{\ell}^{(m)}+\xi_{\ell}^{(m)}

where

•

There are constants
C_{m}
and
C_{m}^{\prime}
such that

\|\zeta_{\ell}^{(m)}\|_{L^{2}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C_{m}}{|\ell|}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{n}{c_{m}}\right)\hskip 28.45274pt\|(r\nabla_{z})^{\alpha}(\nabla_{t})^{\beta}\zeta_{\ell}^{(m)}\|_{L^{2}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}_{m}|\ell|^{\beta}}{|\ell|}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{n}{c_{m}^{\prime}}\right).

(4.24)

for
A_{n\ell}
is as in Proposition
4.3
and multi-indices
\alpha,\beta
.

•

The latter perturbation satisfies

\|\xi_{\ell}^{(m)}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{C_{m}}{|\ell|^{2+m}}.\hskip 71.13188pt\|(r\nabla_{z})^{\alpha}(\nabla_{t})^{\beta}\xi_{\ell}^{(m)}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}_{m}|\ell|^{\beta}}{|\ell|^{2+m}}

(4.25)

Moreover,
\zeta_{\ell}
contains only Fourier modes
e^{ipt}
with
\ell-\tfrac{|\ell|}{2}\leq p\leq\ell+\tfrac{|\ell|}{2}.
The constants
C_{m},c_{m}
are independent of
\ell
, and depend on up to the
H^{m+3}
-norm of the metric, and
C^{\prime}_{m},c_{m}^{\prime}
on up to the
H^{m+|\alpha|+|\beta|+3}
-norm.

Proof.

For
\alpha=\beta=0
, this follows from applying Lemma
4.9
inductively. Instead of solving for
\xi_{\ell}
with
f_{\ell}^{\text{high}}-(\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{0}\not{D}_{0})u_{\ell}
on the right hand side as in the proof of Lemma
4.8
, instead set
(f_{\ell}^{\text{low}})^{1}=-(\not{D}_{N}\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{0}\not{D}_{0})u_{\ell}
and apply Lemma
4.9
again to the low Fourier modes to obtain a second correction
\zeta_{\ell}^{\prime}
and set
\zeta_{\ell}^{(1)}=\zeta_{\ell}+\zeta_{\ell}^{\prime}
. Proceeding in this fashion, each iteration yields an additional power of

r
from the difference
\not{D}_{N}-\not{D}_{\circ}
. Integrating against the exponential, this becomes an additional power of
|\ell|^{-1}
in the new remainder.

To control the range of Fourier modes, define the low modes instead by truncating at
L_{0}=|\ell|/4m
, so that each iteration expands the range of modes appearing in
\zeta_{\ell}^{N}
by
L_{0}/2m
. The bounds on
\xi_{\ell}^{N}
then follow as before, using higher Sobolev norms in (
4.23
) to bound the remainder after
m
iterations. The higher derivative estimates follow from repeating the argument applying estimates for nested sequences of commutators
[r\nabla_{z},\not{D}_{N}]
and
[\nabla_{t},\not{D}_{N}]
. Each application of
\nabla_{t}
requires increasing the bound by a factor of
|\ell|
, but each application of
\nabla^{\text{b}}
-derivatives only by a universal constant.
∎

4.2.
Fredholm Properties

This subsection defines the map
ob
from Proposition
4.3
and proves that it is Fredholm (more precisely, this is a preliminary version of
ob
, which is later corrected by a compact operator). Let
\chi_{1}
be a smooth cut-off function supported in the region
r<r_{N}/2
, where
r_{N}
is sufficiently small that Lemmas
4.6
and
4.8
hold, and equal to
1
in the region
r\leq r_{N}/4
. Define
\text{ob}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\text{\bf{Ob}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
by

\text{ob}(\xi):=\Pi^{\perp}_{0}(\chi_{1}\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}(\eta))

(4.26)

where
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection to
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
as in Definition
4.1
, so that
e^{i\ell t}\mapsto\Pi^{\perp}_{0}(\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{N})
, where
\Psi_{\ell}^{N}
are as in Section
4.1
. The definition is extended to include the
\ell=0
Fourier modes by setting
\Psi_{0}^{N}:=\Psi_{0}^{\circ}
(this
\ell=0
mode is not
L^{2}
on
Y_{\circ}
, but
\chi_{1}\Psi_{0}^{N}\in L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})
).

Lemma 4.11
.

\text{ob}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\text{\bf{Ob}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
as defined by (
4.26
) is Fredholm.

Proof.

The
\ell=0
mode may be ignored as it spans a finite-dimensional space; likewise the distinction between
\Pi_{0},\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
may be ignored since it has finite rank. Precomposing with the isomorphism from Lemma
4.6
shows Fredholmness of
ob
is equivalent to Fredholmness of the map
M
defined by

\displaystyle M:\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\Psi-\not{D}v_{\Psi}\hskip 42.67912pt\text{where }\hskip 42.67912ptv_{\Psi}:=P\not{D}(\chi_{1}\Psi).

Since
\Pi_{0}=\text{Id}-\not{D}P\not{D}
by Corollary
2.8
.

Define a pseudo-inverse

\displaystyle M^{\dagger}:\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\ \mapsto\

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\Phi-\not{D}_{N}u_{\Psi}\hskip 42.67912pt\text{where }\hskip 42.67912ptu_{\Phi}:=P_{N}\not{D}_{N}(\chi_{1}\Phi).

To prove the lemma, it suffices to verify that
M^{\dagger}M=\text{Id}+A_{1}
and
MM^{\dagger}=\text{Id}+A_{2}
for compact operators
A_{1},A_{2}
. First, note that standard elliptic theory implies the following: if
K\Subset Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is compactly contained in the complement of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, then the restriction

R:\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}\to rH^{1}_{e}(K)

(4.27)

is compact. Indeed, since
\not{D}
is uniformly elliptic away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, this follows from standard elliptic bootstrapping and Rellich’s Lemma. The equivalent statement holds on
K_{N}\Subset N
, but compactness then also
a priori
requires that
K_{N}
be bounded in the non-compact
N
.

A straightforward computation shows

\displaystyle(MM^{\dagger}-\text{Id})\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}^{2}-1)\Phi-\chi_{1}\not{D}_{N}u_{\Phi}-\not{D}v_{M^{\dagger}\Phi}.

(4.28)

\displaystyle(M^{\dagger}M-\text{Id})\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\chi_{1}^{2}-1)\Psi-\chi_{1}\not{D}v_{\Psi}-\not{D}_{N}u_{M\Psi}.

(4.29)

and we claim the right hand sides of both expressions are compact. For the first expression,
\text{supp}(\chi_{1}^{2}-1)\Subset Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
hence compactness follows from what was said about the restriction map (
4.27
). Likewise, (
4.27
) implies that the map
\Phi\mapsto u_{\Phi}
is compact since it may be written as the composition

u=P_{N}\circ d\chi_{1}.\circ R|_{\text{supp}(d\chi_{1})}.

Similarly,
\Psi\mapsto v_{\Psi}
is compact. Since the remaining terms on the right hand side of factor through these, we conclude that
MM^{\dagger}-\text{Id}
is compact. The only difference for
M^{\dagger}M-\text{Id}
is that
(\chi_{1}^{2}-1)
is not compactly supported on
Y_{N}
. Nevertheless, a standard diagonalization using the decay properties of
\Psi^{N}_{\ell}=\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\zeta^{N}_{\ell}+\xi_{\ell}^{N}
shows that it is compact on elements of
\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})
(choose subsequences on that simultaneously converge on
r\leq n
and on the span of
|\ell|\leq n
).
∎

4.3.
The Index via Concentration

This subsection proves
\text{ob}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
, which is Fredholm by Lemma
4.11
, has index 0. This is done by introducing a family of perturbations depending on
\mu\in\mathbb{R}

2
2

2

This approach was suggested to the author by Clifford Taubes.

\not{D}_{\mu}:=\not{D}+\mu J

where
J
is a complex anti-linear map with
J^{2}=-\text{Id}
. As
\mu\to\infty
, elements of
\text{\bf{Ob}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
become increasingly concentrated near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and for
\mu
sufficiently large we may conclude that the
\mu
-version of
M_{\mu}
is an isomorphism. There are two subtleties in this. First, one must be careful to ensure the family
\text{ob}_{\mu}
can be viewed on a fixed Banach space (as
\ker(\not{D}_{\mu}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
may jump in dimension as
\mu
varies). Second, the role of the
\ell=0
modes for the index must be clarified.

To elaborate on the second point: recall that on
Y_{\circ}
from Example
3.2
there are two linearly independent solutions in the
\ell=0
Fourier mode, these being
(1/\sqrt{z},0)
and
(0,1/\sqrt{\overline{z}})
. It is not at first clear which subset of these should contribute to the index; it will be shown that as
\mu\to 0
this four (real) dimensional space splits into two subspaces of exponentially growing and decay modes, and only the decaying modes contribute.

Lemma 4.12
.

The Fredholm map

\text{ob}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}

has index zero.

Proof.

Let
\overline{\text{ob}}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})\to\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
be defined by
\overline{\text{ob}}=\text{ob}\oplus\iota
where
\iota
is the inclusion. Similarly, let
\overline{\not{D}}=(\not{D},\pi_{1})
where
\pi_{1}:rH^{1}\to\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection. The problem may be recast as a problem on fixed Banach spaces by considering the operator

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\text{ob}}&0\\
0&\overline{\not{D}}\end{pmatrix}:\begin{matrix}L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\mathcal{K}_{0}\\
\oplus\\
rH^{1}_{e}\end{matrix}\ \longrightarrow\ \begin{matrix}\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})\\
\oplus\\
\text{range}(\not{D})\oplus\mathcal{K}_{0}\end{matrix}=L^{2}(Y;S_{0})\oplus\mathcal{K}_{0}

where
\mathcal{K}_{0}
is shorthand for
\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
.
\overline{\not{D}}
is an isomorphism (hence Fredholm with index 0) by fiat, so
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
is Fredholm by Lemma
4.11
. It therefore suffices to show that
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
has Index 0.

Recall that the definition (
4.26
) depends implicitly on the choice of parametrix
P
employed in the projections
\Pi=\text{Id}-\not{D}P\not{D}
. If this parametrix
P
is replaced by another parametrix
P^{\prime}
for
\not{D}\not{D}:rH^{1}_{e}\to rH^{-1}_{e}
then the resulting

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{0}:=\overline{\text{ob}}^{\prime}\oplus\overline{\not{D}}

(4.30)

differs by compact operators, hence is Fredholm of the same index as
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
.

Now set
\not{D}_{\mu}:=\not{D}+\mu J
for
\mu\geq 0
. Since the Weitzenböck formula becomes

\not{D}_{\mu}^{\star}\not{D}_{\mu}=(\not{D}-\mu J)(\not{D}+\mu J)=\not{D}^{\star}\not{D}+\mu^{2},

(4.31)

the proofs of Proposition
2.4
and Lemma
2.7
apply to show that
\not{D}_{\mu}:rH^{1}_{e}\to L^{2}
has finite-dimensional kernel and closed range, and
\not{D}_{\mu}^{\star}\not{D}_{\mu}:rH^{1}_{e}\to rH^{-1}_{e}
is Fredholm. Let
P_{\mu}
be the corresponding parametrix defined by (
2.11
). The proofs of Lemmas
4.6
and
4.11
apply equally well to define a map
\text{ob}_{\mu}
and show that

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mu}=L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\mathcal{K}_{0}\oplus rH^{1}_{e}\ \longrightarrow\ L^{2}(Y;S)\oplus\mathcal{K}_{0}

is a Fredholm operator for each
\mu
. Note that inclusion
\iota
and projection
\pi_{1}
are still those for the
\mu=0
operator and its kernel
\mathcal{K}_{0}
.
\overline{Q}_{\mu}
is not
a priori
a continuous family, since jumps in the dimension of
\mathcal{K}_{\mu}
result in discontinuities of
\overline{P}_{\mu}
as defined by (
2.11
). Instead, let
P_{\mu}
be a continuous family of parametrices for
\not{D}_{\mu}^{\star}\not{D}_{\mu}
. As in (
4.30
), the resulting family of operators differs by compact operators, resulting in a now continuous family of Fredholm operators
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{\mu}
with the same index as
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}}_{0}
. After this alteration, it suffices to show that the index is zero for
\mu>>0
.

For
\mu
sufficiently large, the Weitzenböck formula (
4.31
) implies that
\ker(\not{D}_{\mu}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})=0
, so it may be arranged by a further homotopy of parametrices that
\mathcal{Q}_{\mu}
is formed using
\overline{P}_{\mu}=(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}\not{D}_{\mu})^{-1}
once
\mu
is large. For fixed large
\mu
, removing the
\mathcal{K}_{0}
summands form both the domain and range does not disrupt Fredholmness nor alter the index, so these may be safely ignored. Furthermore, there is new splitting
L^{2}=\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}|_{L^{2}})\oplus\text{range}(\not{D}_{\mu}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
in which one may now write

\mathcal{Q}^{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}\text{ob}_{\mu}&0\\
0&\not{D}_{\mu}\end{pmatrix}:\begin{matrix}L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\\
\oplus\\
rH^{1}_{e}\end{matrix}\ \longrightarrow\ \begin{matrix}\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}|_{L^{2}})\\
\oplus\\
\text{range}(\not{D}_{\mu})\end{matrix}

where
\text{ob}_{\mu}
is the
\mu
-version of (
4.26
). Since
\not{D}_{\mu}
is injective, hence an isomorphism onto its range, it suffices now to show that
\text{ob}_{\mu}
is an isomorphism for
\mu>>0
. Finally, since
\not{D}_{\mu}
is injective once
\mu
is sufficiently large independent of small variations in the metric, it may be arranged by a further homotopy through Fredholm operators that the metric is a product for
r\leq r_{0}
. The proof is then completed by the subsequent two lemmas.
∎

The next lemma shows that the perturbation
\mu J
means the
L^{2}
-kernel enjoys an additional decay factor of
e^{-\mu r}
compared to the
\mu=0
case, thus it is concentrated more strongly near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. The proof is an elementary exercise in solving ODEs by diagonalizing matrices since the Fourier modes decouple. Let
(N,g_{\circ})
be the tubular neighborhood from Section
4.1
equipped with the product metric, and
\not{D}_{N,\mu}
the perturbed Dirac operator on it.

Lemma 4.13
.

The perturbed Dirac operator

\not{D}_{N,\mu}:rH^{1}_{e}\longrightarrow L^{2}

is injective, and its extension to
L^{2}
has
\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{N,\mu}|_{L^{2}})
characterized by the following.

•

There is a real 2-dimensional subspace of
\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{N,\mu}|_{L^{2}})
in the
\ell=0
modes. It is given by the span over
\mathbb{R}
of

\Psi^{+}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{e^{-\mu r}}{\sqrt{z}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\hskip 56.9055pt\Psi^{-}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\frac{e^{-\mu r}}{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}

•

There is a real 4-dimensional subspace of
\ker(\not{D}_{N,\mu}^{\star}|_{L^{2}})
in the
\pm\ell
modes spanned over
\mathbb{R}
by spinors

\Psi_{|\ell|,k}=\frac{e^{\pm i\theta/2}}{r^{1/2}}e^{-\sqrt{\ell^{2}+\mu^{2}}}e^{\pm i\ell t}v_{k}

where
v_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
for
k=1,\ldots,4
. ∎

It may be assumed that
\text{ob}_{\mu}
sends the real and imaginary parts of the constant mode to
\Psi_{0}^{\pm}
respectively.

Lemma 4.14
.

For
\mu>>0
,

\text{ob}_{\mu}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}|_{L^{2}})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.13
,
\Pi_{N,\mu}\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})=\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{N,\mu}|_{L^{2}})
is an isomorphism (where the extension to the
\ell=0
modes is as stated preceding the lemma). As in the proof of Lemma
4.11
, it therefore suffices to show the following maps are isomorphisms: define
M_{\mu}:\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{N,\mu}|_{L^{2}})\to\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}|_{L^{2}})
and
M_{\mu}^{\dagger}:\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}|_{L^{2}})\to\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{N,\mu}|_{L^{2}})
by

\displaystyle M_{\mu}(\Psi)

\displaystyle\ =\

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\Psi-\not{D}_{\mu}v_{\Psi}\hskip 51.21504pt\text{where }\hskip 42.67912ptv_{\Psi}:=P_{\mu}\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}(\chi_{1}\Psi).

\displaystyle M_{\mu}^{\dagger}(\Phi)

\displaystyle\ =\

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\Phi-\not{D}_{N,\mu}u_{\Psi}\hskip 42.67912pt\text{where }\hskip 42.67912ptu_{\Phi}:=P_{N,\mu}\not{D}^{\star}_{N,\mu}(\chi_{1}\Phi).

Here,
P_{\mu},P_{N,\mu}
are the true inverses. Note also that
(1-\pi_{1})=\text{Id}
once
\not{D}_{\mu}
is injective, so the different between the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projections
\Pi_{\mu},\Pi_{\mu}^{\perp}
is again immaterial.

By the explicit forms in Lemma
4.13
, every
\Psi\in\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu}|_{L^{2}})
on
N
satisfies

\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\text{supp}(d\chi_{1}))}\leq Ce^{-\mu r_{0}/c_{1}}\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}

(4.32)

on
\text{supp}(d\chi_{1})
. It then follows from the expression (
4.29
) that

\|(M_{\mu}^{\dagger}M_{\mu}-\text{Id})\Psi\|_{L^{2}}\leq Ce^{-\mu r_{0}/c_{1}}\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}},

hence for
\mu
sufficiently large,
M_{\mu}^{\dagger}M_{\mu}
is an isomorphism thus
M_{\mu}
is injective.

Surjectivity follows by the same argument with
M_{\mu}M_{\mu}^{\dagger}
where (
4.32
) is replaced by the bound

\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\text{supp}(d\chi_{1}))}\leq\frac{C}{\mu}\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}(Y)}

(4.33)

for
\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu})
on
Y
. To prove (
4.33
), let
\rho
denote a cut-off function supported equal to 1 on
Y-N_{r_{0}/8}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
so that
\rho=1
on
\text{supp}(\chi_{1})
. Integrating by parts shows

\displaystyle\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\rho\langle J\Phi,\not{D}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\rho\langle J\not{D}\Phi,\Phi\rangle+\langle d\rho.J\Phi,\Phi\rangle\ dV

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\rho\langle\not{D}\Phi,J\Phi\rangle+\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\langle d\rho.J\Phi,\Phi\rangle\ dV

since
\not{D}J=J\not{D}
and
J^{\dagger}=-J
. Consequently, since
d\rho
is bounded by a universal constant,

2\text{Re}\langle\rho J\Phi,\not{D}\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}}.

(4.34)

Then, if
\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}^{\star}_{\mu})
,

\displaystyle 0=\langle\rho J\Phi,(\not{D}-\mu J)\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mu\langle\rho\Phi,\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\rho J\Phi,\not{D}\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}}\ \ \ \ \overset{(\ref{thisone})}{\Rightarrow}\ \ \ \ \mu\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\rho=1)}\leq C\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}(Y)}.

The latter gives (
4.33
) which implies
M_{\mu}
is surjective for
\mu
sufficiently large. This completes the lemma and thus the proof of Lemma
4.12
.
∎

4.4.
The Obstruction Map

This subsection completes the proof of Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
. This is done by altering the preliminary version of
ob
defined by (
4.26
), which is Fredholm of index 0 by Lemma
4.12
, by a compact operator.

Let
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})_{L_{0}}
denote the subspace spanned by
e^{i\ell t}
for
|\ell|\geq L_{0}
.

Lemma 4.15
.

For
L_{0}
sufficiently large, the restricted map

\text{ob}|_{L_{0}}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})_{L_{0}}\to\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}

is injective. Moreover,
\Psi_{\ell}=\text{ob}|_{L_{0}}(e^{i\ell t})
admits a decomposition satisfying the conclusions of Proposition
4.4
.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{P}_{N}=\Pi_{N}\mathfrak{P}_{\circ}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})
is a bounded linear isomorphism with bounded inverse by Lemma
4.6
, it suffices to show that
M=(1-\pi_{1})\circ\Pi_{0}:\ker(\not{D}_{N}|_{L^{2}})\to\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
is injective. Thus let
\Psi^{N}\in\text{im}(\text{ob}|_{L_{0}})
be such that
\|\Psi^{N}\|_{L^{2}}=1
. We may write
\Psi^{N}=\Psi^{\circ}+\zeta+\xi
as in Lemma
4.8
, where each term is the sum over
\zeta_{\ell}
for
|\ell|\geq L_{0}
of the corresponding terms in Lemma
4.8
.

Each
\Phi\in\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
is polyhomogeneous by Proposition
3.8
, thus for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is a bound
|\Phi^{\text{high}}|\leq C_{m}L_{0}^{-m}
, where
\Phi^{\text{high}}
denotes the restriction to the Fourier modes
|\ell|\geq L_{0}/2
in the
t
-direction in Fermi coordinates. For
\Phi_{\alpha}
a basis of
\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\chi_{1}\Psi^{N})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{K}\langle\chi_{1}(\Psi^{\circ}+\zeta+\xi),\Phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{L^{2}}\leq C_{m}L_{0}^{-m}

where the Fourier mode restrictions from Lemma
4.8
is used to bound the
(\Psi^{\circ}+\zeta)
terms, and the bounds from Corollary
4.10
are used to bound the
\xi
term.

The same bounds of Lemma
4.8
and Corollary
4.10
imply that
\Psi^{\circ}+\zeta
is exponentially small on
\text{supp}(\chi_{1})
, thus since
\not{D},P
are bounded,

\displaystyle\|\not{D}P\not{D}(\chi_{1}\Psi^{N})\|_{L^{2}}=\|\not{D}P(d\chi_{1}\Psi^{N})\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\text{Exp}(-\tfrac{L_{0}}{c})+CL_{0}^{-m}.

Combining these, we find that

\displaystyle\|(1-\pi_{1})\Pi_{0}(\chi_{1}\Psi^{N})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(1-\pi)(\text{Id}-\not{D}P\not{D})(\chi_{1}\Psi^{N})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\Psi^{N}\|_{L^{2}}-\|\pi_{1}(\chi_{1}\Psi^{N})\|_{L^{2}}-\|(1-\pi_{1})\not{D}P\not{D}(\chi_{1}\Psi^{N})\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-C_{m}L_{0}^{-m}

and injectivity follows once
L_{0}
is sufficiently large for
m=4
.

The final statement that
\Psi_{\ell}
admits a decomposition satisfying the conclusion of Proposition
4.4
is immediate since
\Psi^{N}_{\ell}
satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
4.4
by Lemma
4.8
. Indeed, repeating the argument above for each index
\ell
individually shows that the difference
\Psi_{\ell}-\Psi_{\ell}^{N}=O(L_{0}^{-m})
may can be absorbed into
\xi_{\ell}
without disrupting the bound for each
|\ell|\geq L_{0}
.
∎

Given Lemma
4.15
,
\text{ind}(M)=0
means that the (complex) codimension of
\text{Im}(M|_{L_{0}})\subseteq\text{\bf Ob}
is
2L_{0}+1
, and we can make the following definition:

Definition 4.16
.

The
Obstruction Basis
is defined as

\Psi_{\ell}:=\begin{cases}\Pi_{0}^{\perp}(\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{N})\hskip 56.9055pt|\ell|>L_{0}\\
\Psi_{\ell}\hskip 93.89418pt|\ell|\leq L_{0}\end{cases}

where
\Psi_{\ell}
for
|\ell|\leq L_{0}
is chosen to be an orthonormal basis of the orthogonal complement of
\text{Im}(M|_{L_{0}})\subseteq\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
. It then follows that the map amended in these low modes

\displaystyle\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})

\displaystyle(e^{i\ell t},\Phi)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\Psi_{\ell}+\Phi

is an isomorphism. Additionally, by the proof of Lemma
4.15
, each
\Psi_{\ell}
admits a decomposition

\Psi_{\ell}=\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\zeta_{\ell}+\xi_{\ell}

(4.35)

satisfying the desired conclusion of Proposition
4.4
(the statement of which are vacuous on the finite range
|\ell|\leq L_{0}
).

The above map
\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}
is a preliminary version of the map
ob
. Thus far, we have shown that
\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}
obeys the necessary bounds for the decomposition in Proposition
4.4
. What remains to be shown is that the projection can be calculated by the sequence of inner product (
4.5
). Arranging this requires altering the definition of
\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}
to obtain the final map
ob
.

Indeed,
a priori

since the basis
\Psi_{\ell}
is not necessarily orthonormal, the coefficients of
\Psi=c_{\ell}\Psi_{\ell}
are not calculated by the
L^{2}
-inner product, i.e. in general

(\text{ob}^{\text{pre}})^{-1}(\Pi_{0}\psi)\neq\left(\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}e^{i\ell t}\ ,\ \sum_{\alpha}\langle\psi,\Phi_{\alpha}\rangle\Phi_{\alpha}\right),

where
\alpha
indexes a basis of
\ker(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}})
. Rather frustratingly, one cannot orthonormalize and retain the decay properties of Proposition
4.4
(disrupting these would lead to certain error terms being unbounded later, so the decay properties are essential). To amend this without orthonormalizing, we precompose
\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}
with a change of basis
3
3

3

Equivalently, this may be viewed as endowing
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
with an alternative inner product with comparable norm.

U:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
. Specifically, let
U
be defined by the linear extension of

U(c_{k}e^{ikt}):=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}(c_{k}e^{ikt}),\Psi_{\ell}\rangle\ e^{ikt}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle c_{k}\Psi_{k},\Psi_{\ell}\rangle\ e^{ikt}.

(4.36)

Lemma 4.17
.

For
L_{0}
sufficiently large,
U:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
is an isomorphism, and

\text{ob}:=\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}\circ U^{-1}

satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
4.3
and Proposition
4.4
].

Proof.

Provided that
U
is an isomorphism, the conclusion of the propositions follow from directly from the definition (
4.36
). Indeed,
ob
is clearly an isomorphism if
U
is since it has already been established that
\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}
is an isomorphism (in Definition
4.16
), which is the assertion of Proposition
4.3
. Additionally, using (
4.36
), one has that for a spinor
\psi\in L^{2}

\displaystyle\text{ob}^{-1}(\Pi_{0}\psi)=UU^{-1}(\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}U^{-1}(\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)),\Psi_{\ell}\rangle\ e^{i\ell t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\text{ob}(\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)),\Psi_{\ell}\rangle e^{i\ell t}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}\rangle\ e^{i\ell t}

which is (
4.5
). Since
\Psi_{\ell}
is unaltered from the case of
\text{ob}^{\text{pre}}
in Definition
4.16
, the conclusions of Proposition
4.4
follows. It therefore suffices to show
U
is an isomorphism, for which we show that

U=\text{Id}+K

where
\|K\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq CL_{0}^{-1/8}
.

To prove this bound on
K
, write
\Psi_{\ell}=\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\Xi_{\ell}
where
\Xi_{\ell}=\zeta_{\ell}+\xi_{\ell}
. We claim the following four bounds hold where all inner products are the hermitian inner product on
L^{2}
:

(i)

\langle\Psi_{k},\Psi_{\ell}\rangle=\delta_{k\ell}
unless both
|k|>L_{0}
and
|\ell|>L_{0}
.

(ii)

\langle\Xi_{k},\Xi_{\ell}\rangle\leq\tfrac{C}{|k||\ell|}
.

(iii)

\langle\Xi_{k},\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}\rangle\leq\tfrac{C}{|k||\ell|}
.

(iv)

\langle\chi_{1}\Psi^{\circ}_{k},\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}\rangle=\delta_{k\ell}+a_{k\ell}
where
|a_{k\ell}|\leq\tfrac{C}{|k|^{1/2}|\ell|^{1/2}}
and if
|k-\ell|\geq|k\ell|^{1/4}
then
|a_{k\ell}|\leq\tfrac{C}{|k|^{2}|\ell|^{2}}
.

(i) holds by Definition
4.16
. (ii) is immediate from the bounds on
\zeta_{\ell}+\xi_{\ell}
and Cauchy-Schwartz. For (iii), recall from Definition
4.16
that
\Xi_{\ell}=\Pi_{0}^{\perp}(\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{N})
, hence

\displaystyle\langle\Xi_{k},\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}\rangle=\langle\Xi_{k},\Pi_{0}^{\perp}(\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\Xi_{k},\Pi_{0}^{\perp}(\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{N})-\Pi_{0}^{\perp}(\chi_{1}\zeta_{\ell}^{N}+\chi_{1}\xi_{\ell}^{N})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\Xi_{k},\Xi_{\ell}\rangle+\langle\Xi_{k},\Pi_{0}^{\perp}(\chi_{1}\zeta_{\ell}^{N}+\chi_{1}\xi_{\ell}^{N})\rangle

after which the bound follows from (ii) and the bounds on
\zeta_{\ell}^{N},\xi_{\ell}^{N}
from Lemma
4.8
. Finally, for (iv) the integral may be written explicitly as

(1+\text{sgn}(k)\text{sgn}(\ell))\int_{N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\chi_{1}^{2}|k|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}|\ell|^{\tfrac{1}{2}}\frac{e^{-(|\ell|+|k|)r}}{r}e^{i(k-\ell)t}|g|^{1/2}d\text{vol}

were
|g|^{1/2}=|g_{\circ}|^{1/2}+O(r)
is the volume form in Fermi coordinates with
g_{\circ}
the product metric. For the term coming from
|g_{\circ}|^{1/2}
the integral is (exponentially close to)
\delta_{k\ell}
by orthogonality in the product case. The
a_{k\ell}
term arises from integrating the
O(r)
term, for which direct integration shows that
|a_{k\ell}|\leq C|k|^{-1/2}|\ell|^{-1/2}
. Additionally, since the metric is smooth, the
e^{i(k-\ell)t}
Fourier mode of the volume form is bounded by
|k-\ell|^{m}
for
m
large; the stronger bound in the case that
|k-\ell|\geq|k\ell|^{1/4}
follows.

With (i)-(iv) established, we calculate the
L^{2}
-norm of
Kc(t)
for
c(t)=\sum_{k}c_{k}e^{ikt}
,

\displaystyle\big\|(Kc(t)\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\ell|\geq L_{0}}\Big|\sum_{|k|\geq L_{0}}\langle c_{k}\Psi_{k},\Psi_{\ell}\rangle-\delta_{k\ell}\Big|^{2}

(4.37)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|\ell|\geq L_{0}}\Big|\sum_{|k|\geq L_{0}}c_{k}a_{k\ell}+c_{k}\langle\Xi_{k},\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}\rangle+c_{k}\langle\chi_{1}\Psi_{k}^{\circ},\Xi_{\ell}\rangle+c_{k}\langle\Xi_{k},\Xi_{\ell}\rangle\Big|^{2}

(4.38)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|c(t)\|_{L^{2}}\sum_{|\ell|,|k|\geq L_{0}}|a_{k\ell}|^{2}+\frac{1}{|k|^{2}|\ell|^{2}}

(4.39)

where we have used Cauchy-Schwartz and (i)-(iv) from above.

The
|k|^{-2}|\ell|^{-2}
term is easily summable, with sum bounded by
1/L_{0}
. For the
a_{k\ell}
term, we split the sum over
k
into two parts, and apply the two cases of item (iv):

\leq\frac{C}{L_{0}}+\sum_{|\ell|\geq L_{0}}\Big(\sum_{|k-\ell|\leq|k\ell|^{1/4}}\frac{1}{|k||\ell|}+\sum_{|k-\ell|\geq|k\ell|^{1/4}}\frac{1}{|k|^{4}|\ell|^{4}}\Big)

(4.40)

The
|\ell|^{4}|k|^{4}
term is once again summable and bounded by a constant multiple of
1/L_{0}
. For the remaining term, observe that
|k-\ell|\leq|k\ell|^{1/4}
implies that
|\ell|/2\leq|k|\leq 2|\ell|
provided
L_{0}
is large enough. This in turn implies that
|k-\ell|\leq 4|\ell|^{1/2}
, from which it follows that

\sum_{|\ell|\geq L_{0}}\sum_{|k-\ell|\leq|k\ell|^{1/4}}\frac{1}{|k||\ell|}\leq\sum_{|\ell|\geq L_{0}}\frac{1}{|\ell|^{2}}\sum_{|k-\ell|\geq|k\ell|^{1/4}}1\leq\sum_{|\ell|\geq L_{0}}\frac{1}{|\ell|^{3/2}}\leq\frac{C}{L_{0}^{1/4}}.

(4.41)

It follows that
\|K\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq CL_{0}^{-1/8}
hence
U=\text{Id}+K
is an isomorphism after possibly increasing
L_{0}
. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.17
, thus the proofs of Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
.
∎

To conclude this subsection, we briefly note the following higher-regularity extension of the previous lemma:

Lemma 4.18
.

The map
U
defined by
4.36
restricts to an isomorphism

U:H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

for every
m>0
.

Proof.

As in the proof of the previous Lemma
4.17
, write
U=\text{Id}+K
. It suffices to show that
K:H^{m}\to H^{m+1/8}
is bounded, i.e. that
K
is a smoothing operator of order
\tfrac{1}{8}
. Knowing this, the lemma follows from the “elliptic estimate”

\|\phi\|_{m}\leq C_{m}\ (\|U\phi\|_{m}+\|\phi\|_{m-1/8})

(4.42)

derived by writing
Id=U-K
and using the triangle inequality and the fact that
U:L^{2}\to L^{2}
is an isomorphism.

Saying that
K:H^{m}\to H^{m+1/8}
is bounded is to say that the sum

\sum_{|\ell|\geq L_{0}}\Big|\sum_{|k|\geq L_{0}}\langle c_{k}\Psi_{k},\Psi_{\ell}\rangle-\delta_{k\ell}\Big|^{2}|\ell|^{2m+1/4}

is bounded by a constant multiple of
\|c(t)\|_{H^{m}}
. For
m=0
, this is immediate from (
4.41
), where a factor of
|\ell|^{1/4}
can be spared without disrupting the summability. For
m>0
the assertion follows from repeating the bounds of (
4.37
–
4.39
) in the proof of Lemma
4.17
using the additional bounds that

\frac{|\ell|^{2m+1/4}|a_{k\ell}|^{2}}{|k|^{2m}}\leq{C_{m}}{|\ell|^{1/4}}|a_{k\ell}|^{2}.

(4.43)

and applying Cauchy-Schwartz with the grouping
(\tfrac{a_{k\ell}}{|k|^{m}})(c_{k}|k|^{m})
. The equivalent bound to (
4.43
) likewise holds with
b_{k\ell}=\langle\Xi_{k},\chi_{1}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}\rangle+\langle\chi_{1}\Psi_{k}^{\circ},\Xi_{\ell}\rangle+\langle\Xi_{k},\Xi_{\ell}\rangle
in place of
a_{k\ell}
; both of these follow from similar considerations as the proofs of (i)–(iv) in Lemma
4.17
, using the Fourier mode restriction on
\zeta_{\ell}
the higher-order bounds of Corollary
4.10
.
∎

4.5.
The Higher Regularity Obstruction

This subsection refines Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
to cover the cases of higher regularity. The Dirac operator

\not{D}:H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)\to H^{m}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S)

has infinite-dimensional cokernel equal to
\text{\bf Ob}\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}
by Corollary
2.12
. It is not
a priori
clear that this cokernel coincides with the natural restriction
\text{\bf Ob}^{m}:=\text{Im}(\text{ob}|_{H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})})
. The next lemma asserts that this is indeed the case.

Lemma 4.19
.

There is equality

\text{\bf Ob}^{m}=\text{\bf Ob}\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}

as subspaces of
H^{m}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})
. In particular,
\text{ob}|_{H^{m}}
restricts to an isomorphism making the following diagram commute.
H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
Ob
Ob
\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})
\cap L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})
\iota
\text{ob}|_{H^{m}}
\iota
ob

Proof.

Lemma
4.18
shows that there are equivalences of norms

\displaystyle H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

\displaystyle\overset{U}{\sim}

\displaystyle H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

is a bounded linear isomorphism with bounded inverse. It is therefore enough to show that

\sum_{\ell}c_{\ell}\Psi_{\ell}\in H^{m}_{\text{b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{\ell}|c_{\ell}||\ell|^{2m}<\infty.

The right hand side equivalent to the
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
-norm of
\sum c_{\ell}\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
, and the statement then follows from the fact that the projection operator
\not{D}P\not{D}:H^{m}_{\text{b}}\to H^{m}_{\text{b}}
is bounded by Corollary
2.12
.
∎

5.
The Universal Dirac Operator

This section begins the analysis of the Dirac operator allowing the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
to vary. This is done by introducing a “universal” Dirac operator which is the infinite-dimensional family of Dirac operators parameterized by embedded singular sets near
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. The main result of this section, Proposition
5.5
calculates the derivative of this universal Dirac operator with respect to variations in the singular set.

For the remainder of the article we assume
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0})
is regular in the sense of Definition
1.3
.

5.1.
Trivializations

Before calculating the derivative with respect to embeddings, we define the universal Dirac operator more precisely as a map on Banach vector bundles. In this, care must be taken to construct explicit trivializations of these vector bundles; indeed, the present situation is more subtle than the case of scalar-valued functions appearing in
[
10
]
, and imprecision about certain isomorphisms can lead to incorrect formulas for the derivative with respect to deformations of the singular set.

Consider deformations of the singular set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as follows. Let

\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\text{Emb}^{2,2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};Y)

denote an open neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in the space of embedded links of Sobolev regularity
(2,2)
. For each
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, let
(S_{\mathcal{Z}},\gamma,\nabla)
denote the Clifford module defined analogously to
S_{0}
in (
2.1
) so that
S_{\mathcal{Z}}:=S_{\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Here
\ell_{\mathcal{Z}}\to Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}
is the real line bundle whose holonomy representation agrees with that of
\ell_{0}
(up to homotopy) equipped with its unique flat connection with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The Dirac operator
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}
is defined as in Definition
2.1
, and the Hilbert spaces
rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z},S_{\mathcal{Z}}),L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z},S_{\mathcal{Z}})
are defined for
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
analogously to
2.2
but using a weight
r_{\mathcal{Z}}\approx\text{dist}(-,\mathcal{Z})
.

Define families of Hilbert spaces

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\mathcal{Z},\varphi)\ |\ \mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}\ ,\ \varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z};S_{\mathcal{Z}})\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{0})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\mathcal{Z},\psi)\ |\ \mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}\ ,\ \psi\in L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z};S_{\mathcal{Z}})\}

which come equipped with projections
p_{1}:\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})\to\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
p_{0}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{0})\to\mathcal{E}_{0}
respectively.

Lemma 5.1
.

There are trivializations

\displaystyle\Upsilon:\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}\times rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})

\displaystyle\Upsilon:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{0})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}\times\ L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})

which endow the spaces on the left with the structure of locally trivial Hilbert vector bundles.

Assuming this lemma momentarily, we define

Definition 5.2
.

The
Universal Dirac Operator
is the section
\not{\mathbb{D}}
defined by

\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
p_{1}^{*}\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
\not{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{Z},\varphi):=\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}}\varphi

Before proving Lemma
5.1
, we first construct a chart around
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\in\text{Emb}^{2,2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};Y)
. A choice of Fermi coordinates
(t,x,y)
on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
induces an isomorphism
T_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\text{Emb}^{2,2}(\mathcal{Z};Y)\simeq H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
. For a fixed cut-off function
\chi(r):N_{r_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
equal to
1
for
r\leq r_{0}/2
and vanishing for
r\geq r_{0}
, define an exponential map as follows: given
\eta\in H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
with
\|\eta\|_{2}<\rho_{0}
set

F_{\eta}(t,z)=(t,z+\chi(r)\eta(t)).

(5.1)

Then define

\displaystyle\text{Exp}:H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\text{Emb}^{2,2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};Y)

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}:=F_{\eta}[\mathcal{Z}_{0}],

where
F_{\eta}[\mathcal{Z}_{0}]
denotes the image under
F_{\eta}
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}:=B_{\rho_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})\subset H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
be the open ball of radius
\rho_{0}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For
\rho_{0}
sufficiently small,
F_{\eta}:Y\to Y
is a diffeomorphism for each
\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, and the map
\text{Exp}:\mathcal{E}_{0}\to\text{Emb}^{2,2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};Y)
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

Proof.

Since
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\leq C\|\eta\|_{H^{2}}\leq C\rho_{0}
by the Sobolev embedding theorem, it follows that

\text{d}F_{\eta}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
\chi\eta^{\prime}_{x}&1+\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{x}&\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{x}\\
\chi\eta^{\prime}_{y}&\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{y}&1+\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{y}\end{pmatrix}

is close to the identity, hence invertible for
\rho_{0}
sufficiently small.
F_{\eta}
is therefore a local diffeomorphism by the Inverse Function Theorem. To show it is a diffeomorphism, it then suffices to show it is injective. Note that
F_{\eta}
preserves the normal disks
\{t_{0}\}\times D_{r_{0}}
to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, and for each
t_{0}
,
F_{\eta}
increases the coordinate in the direction parallel to
\eta(t_{0})
, hence it is injective on each normal disk.

For the second statement, observe that
F_{\eta}(t,0,0)=(t,\eta(t))
is distinct for distinct
\eta\in C^{1}
, hence
Exp
is injective. For surjectivity, since any embedding
\mathcal{Z}
close to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in
H^{2}
is also close in
C^{1}
, such an embedding must be a graph over
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in Fermi coordinates. Thus
\mathcal{Z}=\text{Exp}(\eta)
for
\eta
the function defining this graph. Continuity of
Exp
and its inverse are verified by standard methods.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

For each
\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
,
F_{s\eta}
for
s\in(-1,1)
is a family of diffeomorphisms whose derivative along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
is equal to
\eta
, but it is not the flow of a time-independent vector field on
Y
extending
\eta
. This choice simplifies several formulas.

We now prove Lemma
5.1
by constructing the trivializations
\Upsilon
. The only slight subtlety here is the association of spinor bundles for different metrics. To highlight the metric dependence, we denote by
S_{h}
the spinor bundle (without tensoring with
\ell_{0}
) formed with the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
using the metric
h
.

The spinor bundles for two distinct metrics
h_{1},h_{2}
are isomorphic, though not canonically. A convenient choice of isomorphism is given via parallel transport on cylinders, following
[
3
, Sec. 5]
. Let
h_{s}
be a 1-parameter family of metrics interpolating between
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
, for
s\in[0,1]
, consider the (generalized) 4-dimensional cylinder

X=([0,1]\times Y,ds^{2}+h_{s}).

X
is spin since
w_{2}(X)=w_{2}(Y)=0
, and Spin structures on
X
are in 1-1 correspondence with those on
Y
. Let
S^{\pm}_{X}\to X
denote the positive and negative spinor bundles on
X
arising from the spin structure corresponding to the fixed spin structure
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
on
Y
. There is a natural isomorphism
S_{X}^{+}|_{Y\times\{s\}}\simeq S_{h_{s}}
(see
[
32
, Sec. 4.3]
or
[
29
, Pg. 4]
). Let
\nabla_{X}
denote the spin connection on
S_{X}^{+}
. Parallel transport along the curve
\gamma_{y}(s)=(s,y)
in the
-s
direction defines a linear isometry

\tau_{h_{0}}^{h_{s}}(y,s):(S_{h_{s}})_{y}\to(S_{h_{0}})_{y},

where the subscript denotes the fiber over a point
y\in Y
. Together, parallel transport for
s=1
along all such curves define an isomorphism

\tau_{h_{0}}^{h_{1}}:S_{h_{1}}\to S_{h_{0}}

(5.2)

denoted by the same symbol which is a fiberwise isometry, and likewise for any
s\in[0,1]
.

We now prove Lemma
5.1
by constructing the trivialization
\Upsilon
. This trivialization is the composition of three isomorphism specified during the proof.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

For each
\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, let
g_{\eta}:=F_{\eta}^{*}g_{0}
denote the pullback metric. In addition, we continue to denote
\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}=F_{\eta}[\mathcal{Z}_{0}]
. The proof now has four steps.

Step 1:
The pullback
F_{\eta}^{*}
induces a canonical isomorphism

S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}\simeq F_{\eta}^{*}S_{g_{0}}\otimes F_{\eta}^{*}\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}.

(5.3)

There are furthermore canonical isomorphisms

F_{\eta}^{*}S_{g_{0}}\simeq S_{g_{\eta}}\hskip 56.9055ptF_{\eta}^{*}\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}\simeq\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}},

(5.4)

between the pullback of the spinor bundle and the spinor bundle of the pullback metric, and the real line bundles (the latter up to a global choice of sign). In fact, it is straightforward to check that these isomorphisms naturally intertwine the connections in the sense that they send
F_{\eta}^{\star}\nabla^{\text{spin}}_{g_{0}}\mapsto\nabla^{\text{spin}}_{g_{\eta}}
and
F_{\eta}^{\star}\nabla_{A_{\eta}}\mapsto\nabla_{A_{0}}
, where
A_{\eta}
denotes the flat connection with holonomy in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}
. The tensor product of these isomorphisms is denoted

\iota:F^{*}_{\eta}S_{g_{0}}\otimes F_{\eta}^{*}\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}\to S_{g_{\eta}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}.

(5.5)

Step 2:
For
s\in[0,1]
, consider the family of metrics
g_{s\eta}=F_{s\eta}^{*}g_{0}
interpolating between
g_{0}
and
g_{\eta}
. Let

\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{\eta}}:S_{g_{\eta}}\to S_{g_{0}}

denote the fiberwise isometry defined in (
5.2
) setting
h_{0}=g_{0}
and
h_{1}=g_{\eta}
. In a slight abuse of notation, we use the same symbol to denote the induced fiberwise isometry
S_{g_{\eta}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\to S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
which would be more correctly written as
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{\eta}}\otimes\text{Id}
.

Step 3:
For each
\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, define
\Upsilon_{\eta}:=(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{\eta}})\circ\iota\circ F_{\eta}^{*}
as the composition

S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
S_{g_{\eta}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
F_{\eta}^{*}S_{g_{0}}\otimes F_{\eta}^{*}\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}
S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{\eta}}
\iota
F_{\eta}^{*}

where
F_{\eta}^{*}
denotes the pullback as before, and
\iota,\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{\eta}}
are as defined in Step 1 and Step 2 respectively.

Together, the maps
\Upsilon_{\eta}
for
\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
yield a universal trivialization: let
\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{E}_{0}
be the bundle whose fiber over
\eta
is the Riemannian manifold
(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\eta},g_{0})
, and
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{E}_{0}
be the vector bundle whose restriction to the fiber
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}
over
\eta
is
S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}\to Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}
. Together, the maps
\Upsilon_{\eta}
yield a map

\Upsilon:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{E}_{0}\times(S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})

given by
\Upsilon_{\eta}
on the fiber over
\eta\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, which is diffeomorphism on each such fiber (these fibers being themselves the total space of a vector bundle). Moreover, for each fixed
\eta
, this diffeomorphism is a linear isometry on the fibers of
S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}}\to(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\eta},g_{0})
.

Step 4:
The fiberwise isomorphism
\Upsilon_{\eta}
induces a map

\Upsilon_{\eta}:rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{\eta},S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{\eta}})\longrightarrow rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0},S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})

on sections via pullback, which is denoted by the same symbol. This map is an isomorphism by the naturality of the pullback, and it remains to show that it is bounded. This is obvious provided
\eta
and thus
g_{\eta}
has sufficient regularity
4
4

4

The case that
\eta\in C^{\infty}
is sufficient for the proof of Theorem
1.5
, but the low regularity case is included for completeness.
.

The maps on sections induced by
F_{\eta}^{*}
and
\iota
are isometries by construction, thus it suffices to show that the map on sections induced by
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{\eta}}
defined in (
5.2
) is bounded between the versions of
rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})
formed with the metric and spin connections of
g_{0},g_{\eta}
respectively. To see this, note that because
\eta\in H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
, in Fermi coordinates the pullback metric
g_{\eta}
has entries of the form
h(t)g_{1}(t,x,y)
where
h(t)\in H^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and
g_{1}
is smooth (cf. Lemma
5.8
below). Since
H^{1}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})\hookrightarrow C^{0}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
by the Sobolev embedding, the two volume forms induce equivalent norms. The Christoffel symbols of the connection
\nabla_{B_{0}}
formed from the spin connection
g_{\eta}
and
B_{0}
, have one lower regularity, thus include terms of the form
f(t)g_{2}(t,x,y)
for
f(t)\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
where
g_{2}
is continuous. The equivalence of norms is then a consequence of the “mixed dimension” Sobolev multiplication on the solid torus
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\times D^{2}

\|f(t)\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\times D^{2})}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}},

for
f\in L^{2}(S^{1})
and
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{e}
. To prove the latter, simply observe that
I(t)^{2}=\int_{t\times D^{2}}|\varphi|^{2}dxdy
is
L^{\infty}
by the Sobolev restriction theorem and then apply Fubini’s theorem.

∎

5.2.
Universal Linearization

Using the trivialization constructed in Lemma
5.1
, we may now calculate the (vertical component of the) derivative of the universal Dirac operator considered as a map

\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}:H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\times rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})\longrightarrow L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0}),

(5.6)

where
S_{0}=S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
as in Section
2
. After trivializing, differentiating with respect to a deformation
\eta
of the singular set becomes differentiation of the Dirac operator with respect to the family of metrics
g_{s\eta}
for
s\in[0,1]
.

Proposition 5.5
.

In the local trivialization provided by
\Upsilon
, the linearization of the universal Dirac operator
on the spaces (
5.6
) is given by

\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}(\eta,\psi)=\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)+\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\psi

(5.7)

where

\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)=\left(\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}\circ\not{D}^{g_{s\eta}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\circ(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}})^{-1}\right)\Phi_{0}

is the first variation of the Dirac operator with respect to the family of metrics
g_{s\eta}
acting on
\Phi_{0}
.

Remark 5.6
.

(Cf. Section 4.1 of
[
10
]
) Since the configuration
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
does not lie along the zero-section in
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
, there is no canonical splitting

T_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})\simeq T_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}).

Thus expression of the derivative (
5.6
) relies on a choice of connection on the Banach vector bundle
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}
— here we have implicitly chosen the pullback of the product connection by
\Upsilon
. Different choices of trivialization will result in different connections and different expressions for the derivative
\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}
. Concretely, this choice manifests as the dependence of the family of metrics
g_{\eta}
on our choice of diffeomorphisms
F_{\eta}
. A different choice of family of diffeomorphisms differs from our choice of
F_{\eta}
by composing with (a family of) diffeomorphisms fixing
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Although there are many possible choices (see
[
44
]
and
[
63
]
) this choice simplifies many expressions. Of course, the salient properties of the linearization are independent of these choices.

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Take a path

\displaystyle\gamma:(-\epsilon,\epsilon)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{\eta(s)},\Phi(s))

such that
\gamma(0)=(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
. Using the chart
\text{Exp}:H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\to\mathcal{E}_{0}
, we may assume that
\eta(s)=s\eta
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the section of
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
obtained from radial parallel transport of
\Phi_{0}
in the connection induced by the trivialization
\Upsilon
. That is, set

\mathcal{H}=\Upsilon^{-1}(\mathcal{E}_{0}\times\{\Phi_{0}\}).

We may write each
\Phi(s)\in rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta})
as the point in
\mathcal{H}
plus a vertical vector
\phi(s)=\Upsilon^{-1}(\psi(s))
, i.e.

\gamma(s)=(\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta},\Upsilon_{s\eta}^{-1}(\Phi_{0})+\phi(s)\ )=(\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta},\Upsilon_{s\eta}^{-1}(\Phi_{0}+\psi(s))\ ).

The derivative in the trivialization given by
\Upsilon
is

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\Upsilon_{s\eta}\circ\not{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta},\Upsilon_{s\eta}^{-1}(\Phi_{0}+\psi))=\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\Upsilon_{s\eta}\circ\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta}}^{g_{0}}\circ\Upsilon_{s\eta}^{-1}(\Phi_{0}+\psi)

(5.8)

where
\Upsilon
denotes the trivialization for both
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{e}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
and
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
.

Recalling the definition of
\Upsilon_{s\eta}:=(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}})\circ\iota\circ F_{s\eta}^{*}
from Step 3 in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, the following diagram commutes, where the rightmost vertical arrow is the expression (
5.8
) which we wish to calculate.

rH^{1}_{e}(S_{g_{s\eta}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
L^{2}(S_{g_{s\eta}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
rH^{1}_{e}(S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
L^{2}(S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}})
rH^{1}_{e}(S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta}})
L^{2}(S_{g_{0}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta}})
\begin{pmatrix}\text{varying }g_{s\eta}\\
\text{fixed }\mathcal{Z}_{0}\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}\text{varying }\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta}\\
\text{fixed }g_{0}\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}\text{fixed }g_{0}\\
\text{fixed }\mathcal{Z}_{0}\end{pmatrix}
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}
\iota_{s\eta}\circ F_{s\eta}^{*}
\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta}}^{g_{0}}
\iota_{s\eta}\circ F_{s\eta}^{*}
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}
\Upsilon_{s\eta}\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta}}^{g_{0}}\Upsilon_{s\eta}^{-1}

The middle vertical arrow denotes Dirac operator on the bundle
S_{g_{s\eta}}\otimes\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
formed using the pullback metric
g_{\eta}
and the unique flat connection on
\ell_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}
.

By commutativity, the rightmost vertical arrow is equivalent to the conjugation of the middle arrow by
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}
and its inverse. Consequently, using the product rule (noting as well that
\psi(0)=0
and
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{\eta(0)}}=\text{Id}
),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\Upsilon_{s\eta}\circ\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{s\eta}}^{g_{0}}\circ\Upsilon_{s\eta}^{-1}(\Phi_{0}+\psi(s))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\left(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}\circ\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}\circ(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}})^{-1}\right)(\Phi_{0}+\psi(s))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}\circ\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}}\circ(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s\eta}})^{-1}\right)\Phi_{0}+\not{D}^{g_{0}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\dot{\psi}(0).

as claimed.
∎

5.3.
First Variation Formula

In order to analyze the derivative of the universal Dirac operator calculated in Proposition
5.5
, a more explicit formula is needed for the variation of the Dirac operator with respect to metrics (
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
in
5.7
). The formula for this variation is originally due to Bourguignon and Gauduchon
[
3
]
. A concise proof (in English) was later given in
[
2
]
. See also
[
41
]
.

Suppose, forgetting any reference to the above situation momentarily, that
g_{s}
is a path of metrics on a Riemannian spin manifold
W
. Let
\dot{g}_{s}
denote the derivative of this path at
s=0
, and let

\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s}}:S_{g_{s}}\to S_{g_{0}}

be the isomorphism of spinor bundles defined in (
5.2
). We obtain a 1-parameter family of operators

\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s}}\circ\not{D}_{g_{s}}\circ(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s}})^{-1}:\Gamma(S_{g_{0}})\to\Gamma(S_{g_{0}})

as the right arrow in the commutative diagram
\Gamma(S_{g_{s}})
\Gamma(S_{g_{s}})
\Gamma(S_{g_{0}})
\Gamma(S_{g_{0}})
\not{D}_{g_{s}}
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s}}
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s}}

for every
s
. Letting
\{e_{i}\}
be an orthonormal frame for the metric
g_{0}
and
\{e^{i}\}
its dual frame, Bourguignon and Gauduchon calculate:

Theorem 5.7
.

(Bourguignon–Gauduchon
[
3
]
)

The first variation of the Dirac operator with respect to the family of metrics
g_{s}
is given by

\left(\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s}}\circ\not{D}_{g_{s}}\circ(\tau_{g_{0}}^{g_{s}})^{-1}\right)\Psi=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}\dot{g}_{s}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla^{g_{0}}_{j}\Psi+\frac{1}{2}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{s}).\Psi+\frac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{s}).\Psi

(5.9)

where
.
denotes Clifford multiplication in the
g_{0}
metric.

Note that the first term is independent of the choice of frame for the same reason as the standard Dirac operator. Here, in an orthonormal frame, the
\text{div}_{g_{0}}(k)
is the 1-form
-(e_{i}\mathbin{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\lnot$}}}\nabla_{i}k_{ij}e^{i})e^{j}.
To give some quick intuition for this slightly unappetizing formula, the first term comes from differentiating the symbol of the Dirac operator (Clifford multiplication), and the second two terms arise from differentiating the Christoffel symbols.

We will apply Bourguignon-Gauduchon’s formula (
5.9
) in the case that the family of metrics is the one given by the pullbacks

\dot{g}_{\eta}:=\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}g_{s\eta}=\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}F^{*}_{s\eta}g_{0}.

(5.10)

As in Definition
3.4
, the metric in Fermi coordinates
(t,x,y)
on the tubular neighborhood
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
has the form

g_{0}=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}+h\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ where \ \ \ }|h_{ij}|\leq Cr.

Lemma 5.8
.

The derivative of the family of pullback metrics (
5.10
) is given by

\dot{g}_{\eta}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\eta_{x}^{\prime}\chi&\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{x}\chi&2\eta_{x}\partial_{x}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi&2\eta_{y}\partial_{y}\chi\end{pmatrix}+h_{1}+h_{2}

(5.11)

where

•

h_{1}
is a
O(1)
term whose entries are formed from products of derivatives of
h_{ij}
and
\eta
.

•

h_{2}
is a
O(r)
term whose entries are formed from products of
h_{ij}
and products of
\eta,\eta^{\prime}
.

Here,
\eta=\eta_{x}+i\eta_{y}
and
\eta^{\prime}=\tfrac{d}{dt}\eta
and
\dot{g}_{\eta}
is as in (
5.10
).

Proof.

Since the diffeomorphism
F_{s\eta}
is supported in the tubular neighborhood, it suffices to do the calculation in Fermi coordinates.

First, consider the case that
h=0
. Recall

F_{s\eta}(t,x,y)=(t,x+s\chi(r)\eta_{x}(t),y+s\chi(r)\eta_{y}(t)),

hence

\text{d}F_{s\eta}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
s\chi\eta^{\prime}_{x}&1+s\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{x}&s\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{x}\\
s\chi\eta^{\prime}_{y}&s\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{y}&1+s\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{y}\end{pmatrix}.

A quick calculation shows in this case the pullback metric is

\displaystyle\dot{g}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}(\text{d}F_{s\eta})^{T}g_{0}(\text{d}F_{s\eta})

(5.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&\eta_{x}^{\prime}\chi&\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{x}\chi&2\eta_{x}\partial_{x}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi&2\eta_{y}\partial_{y}\chi\end{pmatrix}.

(5.14)

Now in the case that
h\neq 0
, let
\widetilde{h}_{ij}=h_{ij}(t,z+F_{s\eta})
. Then the term added to the above is

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}(\text{d}F_{s\eta})^{T}\cdot h(t,z+F_{s\eta})\cdot(\text{d}F_{s\eta})

(5.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}(\text{d}F_{s\eta})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{h}_{11}+s\chi(\widetilde{h}_{12}\eta^{\prime}_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{13}\eta^{\prime}_{y})&\widetilde{h}_{12}+s\partial_{x}\chi(\widetilde{h}_{12}\eta_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{13}\eta_{y})&\widetilde{h}_{13}+s\partial_{y}\chi(\widetilde{h}_{12}\eta_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{13}\eta_{y})\\
\widetilde{h}_{21}+s\chi(\widetilde{h}_{22}\eta^{\prime}_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{23}\eta^{\prime}_{y})&\widetilde{h}_{22}+s\partial_{x}\chi(\widetilde{h}_{22}\eta_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{23}\eta_{y})&\widetilde{h}_{23}+s\partial_{y}\chi(\widetilde{h}_{22}\eta_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{23}\eta_{y})\\
\widetilde{h}_{31}+s\chi(\widetilde{h}_{32}\eta^{\prime}_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{33}\eta^{\prime}_{y})&\widetilde{h}_{32}+s\partial_{x}\chi(\widetilde{h}_{32}\eta_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{33}\eta_{y})&\widetilde{h}_{33}+s\partial_{y}\chi(\widetilde{h}_{32}\eta_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{33}\eta_{y})\end{pmatrix}.

Write the matrix above as
\widetilde{h}_{ij}+sA_{ij}
, so that e.g.
A_{11}=\chi\widetilde{h}_{12}\eta^{\prime}_{x}+\widetilde{h}_{13}\eta^{\prime}_{y}
. Then since

\text{d}F_{s\eta}^{T}=\text{Id}+s\begin{pmatrix}0&\chi\eta^{\prime}_{x}&\chi\eta^{\prime}_{y}\\
0&\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{x}&\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{y}\\
0&\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{x}&\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{y}\end{pmatrix}

and
\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{h}_{ij}\end{pmatrix}
is symmetric, (
5.15
) becomes

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{h}_{ij}\end{pmatrix}+s\begin{pmatrix}A_{ij}+A^{T}_{ij}\end{pmatrix}+O(s^{2})\right]=\underbrace{\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{h}_{ij}\end{pmatrix}}_{:=h_{1}}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}A_{ij}+A_{ij}^{T}\end{pmatrix}}_{:=h_{2}}.

(5.16)

Call these terms
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
as indicated. Since

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\widetilde{h}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}h_{ij}(t,x+s\chi\eta_{x},y+s\chi\eta_{y})=(\partial_{x}{h}_{ij})\chi\eta_{x}+(\partial_{y}{h}_{ij})\chi\eta_{y}

\displaystyle A_{ij}\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{k\ell}\chi\eta_{\alpha}^{\prime}\ \ \text{ \ or \ }\ \ h_{k\ell}\partial_{\alpha}\chi\eta_{\beta}

where
\alpha,\beta
range over
x,y
and (summation is implicit in the expression for
A
), these are respectively of the forms claimed for
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
.
∎

Combining the formula for the linearization of the universal Dirac operator of Proposition
5.5
with the formula of Bourguignon-Gauduchon (Theorem
5.9
) and the calculation of the pullback metric in Lemma
5.8
allows us to immediately deduce the following more concrete expression for the linearization.

Corollary 5.9
.

The linearization of the universal Dirac operator at
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
is given by

\displaystyle\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}(\eta,\psi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla^{g_{0}}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).+\frac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).+\mathcal{R}(B_{0},\chi\eta).\right)\Phi_{0}\ \

(5.18)

\displaystyle+\not{D}\psi

where
\mathcal{R}(B_{0},\eta)
is a smooth term involving up to first derivatives of
B_{0}
and linear in
\chi\eta
, and
.
denotes Clifford multiplication using the metric
g_{0}
. Explicitly,
\dot{g}_{\eta}
is given in Fermi coordinates by

\begin{pmatrix}0&\eta_{x}^{\prime}\chi&\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{x}\chi&2\eta_{x}\partial_{x}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi&2\eta_{y}\partial_{y}\chi\end{pmatrix}+h_{1}+h_{2}

with
h_{1},h_{2}
as in the above Lemma
5.8
.

Proof.

In the case that
B_{0}=0
, this follows immediately from Theorem
5.9
and the above calculation of the pullback metric in Lemma
5.8
. The line bundle is fixed after pulling back by
F_{\eta}
and plays no role. The perturbation
B_{0}
pulls back to
F_{s\eta}^{*}B_{0}
, and differentiating this yields the term
\mathcal{R}(B_{0},\chi\eta)
.
∎

A word of caution to the reader: the formula for this linearization is slightly deceptive in the following sense. The expression for
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
, which is the first line in (
5.18
) (cf. (
5.7
)), appears to be a first order term plus a zeroeth order term. But these are the orders in the
spinor

\Phi_{0}
, and we are viewing it as an equation in the
deformation

\eta
. The variation of the pullback metrics
\dot{g}_{\eta}
, as above, contains first derivatives of
\eta(t)
, and so the trace and divergence, which contain derivatives of
\dot{g}_{\eta}
contain second derivatives of
\eta(t)
. Thus this equation is actually
second order
in
\eta
, with the second and third terms being leading order. This is the reason
\eta
must be taken to be at least
H^{2}
in order for this partial derivative to be bounded into
L^{2}
.

Remark 5.10
.

For later use, we note that the proof of Lemma
5.8
shows that the complete formula for the pullback metric can be written

g_{s\eta}=g_{0}+s\dot{g}_{\eta}+\mathfrak{q}(s\eta,s\eta)

where
\mathfrak{q}(s\eta,s\eta)
is a matrix whose entries are
O(s^{2})
and are formed from finite sums of terms of the following form

•

Products of at least two terms of the form
\chi\eta^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, or
\partial_{\beta}\chi\eta_{\alpha}
, or
(\widetilde{h}-h)\leq C|\chi\eta|
.

•

Higher order terms of the form
(\widetilde{h}-h-h_{1})\leq C|\chi\eta|^{2}.

where the bounds on the terms involving
\widetilde{h}
follow from Taylor’s theorem. ∎

6.
Fredholmness of Deformations

This section proves Theorem
1.4
by calculating the obstruction component of the linearized universal Dirac operator. For the duration of this section, we continue to assume that
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ell_{0},\Phi_{0})
is a regular (Definition
1.3
)
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor.

Working in the trivialization of Lemma
5.1
and splitting the domain and codomain into their summands, the linearization has the following block lower-triangular form, where
\Pi_{0}:L^{2}\to\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
denotes the orthogonal projection as in Definition
4.1
:

\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}=\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{0}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}&0\\
\\
(1-\Pi_{0})\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}&\not{D}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ \begin{matrix}H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\\
\oplus\\
rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})\end{matrix}\ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \begin{matrix}\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})\\
\oplus\\
\text{range}(\not{D}|_{rH^{1}_{e}}).\end{matrix}

(6.1)

Composing with the inverse of the isomorphism
(\text{ob},\iota):L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}\to\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
from Proposition
4.3
, the upper left entry of (
6.1
) can be written as
(T_{\Phi_{0}},\pi_{1})
where
\pi_{1}
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto
\mathbb{R}\Phi_{0}
, and
T_{\Phi_{0}}
is the composition:

H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0}),
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}
\text{ob}^{-1}
T_{\Phi_{0}}

with
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}
as in Definition
4.1
. In particular,
T_{\Phi_{0}}
is a map of Hilbert spaces of sections of vector bundles on
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

The main result of the current section is the following theorem, which is a more precise statement of Theorem
1.4
in the introduction.

Theorem 6.1
.

The composition
T_{\Phi_{0}}
is an elliptic pseudo-differential operator of order 1/2. In particular, as a map

T_{\Phi_{0}}:H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\longrightarrow H^{3/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

(6.2)

it is Fredholm, and has index 0.

Using the block-diagonal decomposition (
6.1
), Theorem
6.1
and standard bootstrapping imply the following. Here, recall that
\text{\bf Ob}^{m}=\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}
.

Corollary 6.2
.

The linearized universal Dirac operator extends to a Fredholm of index 0

\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}:H^{m+2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\oplus rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}\longrightarrow\text{\bf Ob}^{m+3/2}\oplus\Big(\text{range}(\not{D})\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}\Big).

(6.3)

for every
m\geq 0
.∎

Remark 6.3
.

The order of
T_{\Phi_{0}}
depends on the choice of isomorphism
\text{ob}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\to{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
. For instance, one could just as easily have defined
ob
as the composition of the current version with
(\Delta+1)^{s}
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Writing
\text{Id}=\text{ob}\circ\text{ob}^{-1}
, however, the obstruction component
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}=\Pi^{\perp}_{0}\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}

which is independent of the choice of

ob
may be factored as

H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{\perp}\cap H^{3/2}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})
H^{3/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0}).
\Pi^{\perp}_{0}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}
T_{\Phi_{0}}
ob

In particular, the operator
\Pi_{0}^{\perp}\mathcal{B}_{0}
has “order” 1/2, insofar as its image on
H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is
\text{\bf{Ob}}\cap H^{3/2}_{\text{b}}
independent of the choice of
ob
, possibly up to a finite-dimensional subspace. Here order is used only loosely, as
\Pi_{0}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}
is not itself a pseudodifferential operator. Most importantly, the loss of regularity in Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
is intrinsic to the geometric problem and cannot be avoided by simply revising conventions.

The conventions here are chosen so that
ob
has order zero, i.e. so that the manifestation of
\Pi^{\perp}_{0}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}
as a true pseudodifferential operator — this being
T_{\Phi_{0}}
— acts on spaces of the same regularity. Other authors may adopt the convention that
ob
has order
-1/2
, which natural from the viewpoint of the Poisson operator as in (
4.4
).

6.1.
Conormal Regularity

The remainder of Section
6
proves Theorem
6.1
. Before beginning the proof in earnest, the current section studies the regularity of the projection operator
\Pi_{0}
.

The loss of regularity in Theorem
6.1
is a consequence of the fact that
Ob
does not simply inherit the obvious notion of regularity from
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Instead, one has

Key Observation:
The regularity of
\Pi_{0}(\psi)\in\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
depends on both the regularity of
\psi
and
its order of growth
along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.                 .

To elaborate, Proposition
4.4
shows that the regularity of
\Pi_{0}(\psi)
is a question about the rate of decay in
|\ell|
of the sequence of inner products

\big\{\ \langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ \big\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}.

(6.4)

Because the basis elements
\Psi_{\ell}
concentrate exponentially around
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
as
|\ell|\to\infty
, this rate of decay is intertwined with the growth of
\psi
along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. If, for example,
\psi
is compactly supported away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, then Proposition
4.4
implies the sequence (
6.4
) decays faster than polynomially and the projection
\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
is smooth regardless of the regularity of
\psi
on
Y
. The rest of this subsection characterizes this phenomenon more precisely. Although the regularity of
\Pi_{0}\psi\in\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is different from the ambient regularity on
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, our convention (see Remark
6.3
) means that the regularity on either side of the isomorphism
ob
, i.e. of
\Pi_{0}\psi\in\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and
