\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}\psi\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
coincide by Lemma
4.19
.

Definition 6.4
.

Suppose that a spinor
\psi
can be written locally in Fermi coordinates and an accompanying trivialization as

\psi=\chi\begin{pmatrix}f^{+}(t)h^{+}(\theta)\\
f^{-}(t)h^{-}(\theta)\end{pmatrix}r^{p}

(6.5)

where
f^{\pm}\in H^{k}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
,
h^{\pm}
are smooth, and
\chi
is a cutoff function supported in a neighborhood
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
.
Then the quantity

s=\boxed{1+k+p}

is called the
conormal regularity
of
\psi
.

The following simple lemma gives the fundamental relationship between the conormal regularity and the regularity of the projection. In it, we denote by H the Hilbert transform as defined preceding Definition
4.2
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose that
\psi\in L^{2}
has conormal regularity
s
. Then
\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)\in H^{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

and

\|\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)\|_{s}\leq C_{s}(\|f^{+}\|_{H^{k}}+\|f^{-}\|_{H^{k}}).

Proof.

Using Proposition
4.4
,
\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)
is calculated by the sequence of inner products

\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}\rangle=\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\zeta_{\ell}+\xi_{\ell}\rangle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ where }\hskip 28.45274pt\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}=\chi\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}e^{-|\ell|r}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\\
\frac{\text{sgn}(\ell)}{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}.

Assume first that
g_{0}=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}
on
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
. Using the expression (
6.5
) for
\psi
, the inner product with
\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}
yields

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\chi\begin{pmatrix}f^{+}h^{+}\\
f^{-}h^{-}\end{pmatrix}r^{p}\ ,\ \sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{e^{-|\ell|r}}{\sqrt{z}}\\
\text{sgn}(\ell)\tfrac{e^{-|\ell|r}}{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{S^{1}}\langle f^{+}+Hf^{-},e^{i\ell t}\rangle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{-|\ell|r}r^{p-1/2}\chi(r)\|h^{\pm}\|_{C^{0}}rdrd\theta dt\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\Big|\int_{S^{1}}\langle f^{+}+Hf^{-},e^{i\ell t}\rangle dt\Big|\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{-|\ell|r}r^{p+1/2}dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\big\langle\tfrac{1}{|\ell|^{p+1}}\left(f^{+}(t)+Hf^{-}(t))\right),e^{i\ell t}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})}

Since
f^{\pm}\in H^{k}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
, then
(f^{+}(t)+Hf^{-}(t))\in H^{k}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
as well, thus after applying the Fourier multiplier
1/{|\ell|^{p+1}}
it lies in
H^{1+k+p}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
as desired. For the case of a general metric, the integrands differ by a factor of
1+O(r)
from the volume form and the latter contributes only a term of higher regularity.

It is easy to show that the contributions to the inner product arising from
\zeta_{\ell}+\xi_{\ell}
satisfy the same bounds by invoking Corollary
4.10
and integrating by parts. Since these terms are dealt with explicitly in the proof of Theorem
6.1
, the details are omitted here.
∎

The following additional cases are a straightforward extension of the above.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
\psi\in L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})

(B)

Suppose that
\text{supp}(\psi)\Subset Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Then
\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)\in H^{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

for all
s>0
, and its
H^{s}
-norm is bounded by
C_{s}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}
.

(C)

Suppose
\psi
has the form

\psi=\begin{pmatrix}f^{+}(t)\varphi^{+}(t,r,\theta)\\
f^{-}(t)\varphi^{-}(t,r,\theta)\end{pmatrix}

(6.6)

where
f^{\pm}\in H^{k}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
and
\varphi^{\pm}
satisfy
|\varphi^{\pm}|+|\nabla_{t}\varphi^{\pm}|+\ldots+|\nabla_{t}^{k}\varphi^{\pm}|<C(\varphi)r^{p}
pointwise. Then
\text{ob}^{-1}\Pi_{0}(\psi)\in H^{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
for
s=1+k+p
, and its
H^{s}
-norm is bounded by
C_{s}C(\varphi)\|f^{\pm}\|_{H^{k}(S^{1})}.

Remark 6.7
.

Before calculating the operator
T_{\Phi_{0}}
explicitly, Corollary
6.6
already implies that a loss of regularity is an inevitable consequence of the
\sqrt{r}
asymptotics of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors. Indeed, Corollary
5.9
shows that
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
schematically has the form
\eta^{\prime}.\nabla\Phi_{0}+\eta^{\prime\prime}.\Phi
. Since
\eta\in H^{2}
, and
\Phi_{0}=O(r^{1/2})
with
\nabla\Phi_{0}=O(r^{-1/2})
, these terms have conormal regularity
s=1+1-1/2
and
s=1+0+1/2
respectively. It follows that
\Pi_{0}\mathcal{B}_{0}\in H^{3/2}
but
(1-\Pi_{0})\mathcal{B}_{0}
is in general no better than
L^{2}
.

6.2.
Obstruction Component of Deformations

This subsection proves Theorem
6.1
, except for the index statement, by calculating
T_{\Phi_{0}}
explicitly.

The formula for
T_{\Phi_{0}}
is expressed in terms of standard operators and the following zeroth order operator, for which we recall from Proposition
3.9
that
c(t)\in N\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{-1}
and
d(t)\in N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
denote the leading order (i.e.
r^{1/2}
) coefficients of
\Phi_{0}
. Define an operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

(6.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{\xi}(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle H(c(t)\xi(t))-\overline{\xi}(t)d(t).

(6.8)

where
H
is the Hilbert Transform as preceding Definition
4.2
. Recall here that
\mathcal{C}_{0}\simeq\mathbb{C}
is canonically trivial, hence multiplication in the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is the dual pairing
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}\otimes N\mathcal{Z}_{0}^{-1}\to\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

For
\eta(t)\in H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
,
T_{\Phi_{0}}
as in Theorem
6.1
is given by

T_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta(t))=-\tfrac{3|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|}{2}(\Delta+1)^{-\tfrac{3}{4}}\hskip 1.9919pt\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta^{\prime\prime}(t))\ +\ K(\eta)

(6.9)

where
|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|
denotes the length,
\Delta
denotes the positive-definite Laplacian on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}
is as in (
6.8
) above, and
\eta^{\prime\prime}(t)
denotes the (covariant) second derivative on
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.
K
is a lower-order term.

Remark 6.9
.

Lemma
6.8
shows that, up to composing with the appropriate power of
(\Delta+1)
, the symbol of
T_{\Phi_{0}}
is given by
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}
as in (
6.8
, which is determined entirely by the leading coefficients of
\Phi_{0}
. Thus strict ellipticity of
T_{\Phi_{0}}
is equivalent to non-degeneracy (Definition
1.3
), i.e. to the condition that the frequency function of
\Phi_{0}
(in the sense of
[
73
,
58
]
) has order
1/2
everywhere along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Since Nash-Moser is already required, it seems likely that the non-degeneracy assumption could be weakened to consider e.g. the hypoelliptic case.

Lemma
6.8
is proved by calculating the sequence of inner products

\text{ob}^{-1}(\Pi_{0}^{\perp}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta))=\sum_{\ell}\langle\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta),\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ e^{i\ell t}

(6.10)

quite explicitly, where
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
is as in Corollary
5.9
. The proof consists of five steps: Steps 1–2 calculate (
6.10
) in the case that
g_{0}
is locally the product metric and
\Phi_{0}
is given by its leading order term, and Steps 3–5 show that the small parade of error terms arising from higher order contributions result in a lower-order operator
K
.

Proof of Lemma
6.8
.

Suppose, to begin, that all the structure are given locally by the Euclidean ones. That is, assume

g_{0}=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}\hskip 28.45274pt\Phi_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}c(t)\sqrt{z}\\
d(t)\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}\hskip 28.45274pt\dot{g}_{\eta}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\eta_{x}^{\prime}\chi&\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{x}\chi&2\eta_{x}\partial_{x}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi\\
\eta^{\prime}_{y}\chi&\eta_{x}\partial_{y}\chi+\eta_{y}\partial_{x}\chi&2\eta_{y}\partial_{y}\chi\end{pmatrix},

and
B_{0}=0
; also assume that the obstruction elements of Proposition
4.3
have
\zeta_{\ell}+\xi_{\ell}=0
so that

\Psi_{\ell}=\chi\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}e^{-|\ell|r}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\\
\frac{\text{sgn}(\ell)}{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}

Step 1: product case, divergence term.
Let
e_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
denote an orthonormal frame for
g_{0}
with
e^{i}
the dual frame. Recall that for a symmetric 2-tensor
k
,
\text{div}_{g_{0}}k=(-\nabla_{i}k_{ij})e^{j}.

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\sigma_{2}\chi\eta_{x}^{\prime\prime}+\sigma_{3}\chi\eta_{y}^{\prime\prime}\right]\begin{pmatrix}c(t)\sqrt{z}\\
d(t)\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}+{\bf(I)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\chi\eta^{\prime\prime}_{x}\begin{pmatrix}-d(t)\sqrt{\overline{z}}\\
c(t)\sqrt{z}\end{pmatrix}+\chi\eta^{\prime\prime}_{y}\begin{pmatrix}id(t)/\sqrt{\overline{z}}\\
ic(t)/\sqrt{z}\end{pmatrix}\right]+{\bf(I)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\eta}^{\prime\prime}d(t)\chi\sqrt{\overline{z}}\\
\ \eta^{\prime\prime}c(t)\chi\sqrt{z}\end{pmatrix}\right]+{\bf(I)}

where we have written
\eta(t)=\eta_{x}(t)+i\eta_{y}(t)
, and

{\bf(I)}=-\frac{1}{2}\Big[(\partial_{x}\chi\eta^{\prime}_{x}+\partial_{y}\chi\eta^{\prime}_{y})\sigma_{t}+(2\partial_{xx}\chi\eta_{x}+\partial_{xy}\chi\eta_{y}+\partial_{yy}\chi\eta_{x})\sigma_{x}\newline
+(\partial_{xy}\chi\eta_{y}+\partial_{yy}\chi\eta_{x}+2\partial_{yy}\chi\eta_{y})\sigma_{y}\Big].\Phi_{0}.

Taking the inner product of the first term with
\Psi_{\ell}
yields

\displaystyle\langle\tfrac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{0},\Psi_{\ell}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\chi\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\eta}^{\prime\prime}d(t)\sqrt{\overline{z}}\\
\eta^{\prime\prime}c(t)\sqrt{z}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}\chi\begin{pmatrix}{e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{z}}\\
\text{sgn}(\ell){e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ +\ \langle({\bf I}),\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{1}}\Big\langle\begin{matrix}-\overline{\eta}^{\prime\prime}d(t)\\
\eta^{\prime\prime}c(t)\end{matrix}\ ,\ \begin{matrix}e^{i\ell t}\\
\text{sgn}(\ell)e^{i\ell t}\end{matrix}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ dt\ \ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sqrt{|\ell|}\chi^{2}e^{-|\ell|r}rdrd\theta\ +\ \langle({\bf I}),\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\text{sgn}(\ell)\eta^{\prime\prime}c-\overline{\eta}^{\prime\prime}d\ ,\ e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sqrt{|\ell|}\chi^{2}(r)e^{-|\ell|r}rdrd\theta\ +\ \langle({\bf I}),\Psi_{\ell}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|}{|\ell|^{3/2}}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta^{\prime\prime}),e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ +\ \langle K,e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

where
K
is as follows. First, note

\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{|\ell|}e^{-|\ell|r}rdrd\theta=\frac{1}{|\ell|^{3/2}}

and the presence of
\chi^{2}(r)
results in a difference from this of size
O(e^{-|\ell|r_{0}})
, let this remainder be the first part of
K
.

Then, since

\frac{1}{|\ell|^{3/2}}=\frac{1}{(|\ell|^{2}+1)^{3/4}}+O\left(\frac{1}{|\ell|^{3}}\right),

we can write

\text{ob}^{-1}(\tfrac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{0})=-\tfrac{|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|}{2}(\Delta+1)^{-\tfrac{3}{4}}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta^{\prime\prime})+K

where the lower order psuedo-differential operator from
O(|\ell|^{-3})
is absorbed into
K
. Finally, the term
(I)
is a sum of terms compactly supported away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, hence by Case (B) of Corollary
6.6
, it contributes a smoothing operator which we may likewise absorb into
K
.

Step 2: product case, symbol term.
The “symbol” term from
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
is given by

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left[\chi\eta_{x}^{\prime}\sigma_{t}\nabla_{x}\Phi_{0}+\chi\eta_{y}^{\prime}\sigma_{t}\nabla_{y}\Phi_{0}\right]+{\bf(II)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\left[\chi\eta^{\prime}_{x}\begin{pmatrix}ic(t)/\sqrt{z}\\
-id(t)/\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}+\chi\eta^{\prime}_{y}\begin{pmatrix}-c(t)\sqrt{z}\\
d(t)\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}\right]+{\bf(II)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\left[\begin{pmatrix}i\eta^{\prime}c(t)\chi/\sqrt{z}\\
-i\overline{\eta}^{\prime}d(t)\chi/\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}\right]+{\bf(II)}

where

\displaystyle{\bf(II)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Big[(\chi\eta^{\prime}_{x}\sigma_{x}+\chi\eta^{\prime}_{y}\sigma_{y})\nabla_{t}\Phi_{0}+(2\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{x}\sigma_{x}+\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{y}\sigma_{y}+\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{x}\sigma_{y})\nabla_{x}\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\ (2\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{y}\sigma_{y}+\partial_{x}\chi\eta_{y}\sigma_{x}+\partial_{y}\chi\eta_{x}\sigma_{x})\nabla_{y}\Phi_{0}\Big].

Taking the inner product of the first term with
\Psi_{\ell}
yields the following. This calculation is almost identical to the previous one, but with an additional integration by parts.

\displaystyle\langle\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}\Phi_{0},\Psi_{\ell}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\Big\langle\chi\begin{pmatrix}i\eta^{\prime}c(t)/\sqrt{z}\\
-i\overline{\eta}^{\prime}d(t)/\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \sqrt{|\ell|}e^{i\ell t}\chi\begin{pmatrix}{e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{z}}\\
\text{sgn}(\ell){e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\Big\langle\chi\begin{pmatrix}i\eta^{\prime}c(t)/\sqrt{z}\\
-i\overline{\eta}^{\prime}d(t)/\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \tfrac{\sqrt{|\ell|}}{i|\ell|\text{sgn}\ell}\partial_{t}e^{i\ell t}\chi\begin{pmatrix}{e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{z}}\\
\text{sgn}(\ell){e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\Big\langle\chi\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\eta^{\prime}c(t)/\sqrt{z}\\
-\overline{\eta}^{\prime}d(t)/\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \tfrac{1}{\sqrt{|\ell|}}e^{i\ell t}\chi\begin{pmatrix}\text{sgn}(\ell){e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{z}}\\
{e^{-|\ell|r}}/{\sqrt{\overline{z}}}\end{pmatrix}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\int_{S^{1}}\Big\langle\begin{matrix}\partial_{t}(\eta^{\prime}c(t))\\
-\partial_{t}(\overline{\eta}^{\prime}d(t))\\
\end{matrix}\ ,\ \begin{matrix}\text{sgn}(\ell)e^{i\ell t}\\
e^{i\ell t}\end{matrix}\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ dt\ \ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\ell|}}\chi^{2}e^{-|\ell|r}drd\theta

In the second line we have multiplied the second argument by
1
in the form
1=\tfrac{i\ell}{i|\ell|\text{sgn}\ell}
and noted
i\ell\Psi_{\ell}=\partial_{t}\Psi_{\ell}
, and then integrated by parts. Then,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\langle\text{sgn}(\ell)\eta^{\prime\prime}c-\overline{\eta}^{\prime\prime}d\ ,\ e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{L^{2}(S^{1})}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\ell|}}\chi^{2}(r)e^{-|\ell|r}rdrd\theta

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{4}\langle\text{sgn}(\ell)\eta^{\prime}c^{\prime}-\overline{\eta}^{\prime}d^{\prime}\ ,\ e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{L^{2}(S^{1})}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\ell|}}\chi^{2}(r)e^{-|\ell|r}rdrd\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle-\frac{1}{4}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|}{|\ell|^{3/2}}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta^{\prime\prime}),e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}+\langle-\frac{1}{4}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|}{|\ell|^{3/2}}\mathcal{L}_{\nabla_{t}\Phi_{0}}(\eta^{\prime}),e^{i\ell t}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}+\langle K,e^{i\ell t}\rangle

Where
K
is again an error of size
O(e^{-|\ell|r_{0}})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\nabla_{t}\Phi_{0}}
is defined exactly as
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}
but with
c^{\prime}(t),d^{\prime}(t)
in place of
c(t),d(t)
. Both
\mathcal{L}_{\nabla_{t}\Phi_{0}}
and the term
(II)
are lower order by Lemma
6.5
and Case (B) of Corollary
6.6
, so they may be absorbed into
K
. To see this, note both of these are comprised of terms of the form form
\eta^{\prime}\nabla_{t}\Phi_{0}=\eta^{\prime}r^{1/2}
, hence of conormal regularity
s=5/2
or have a factor of
d\chi
so are compactly supported away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. The term same applies to the term
\tfrac{1}{2}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{0}
, which we likewise absorb into
K
.

Remark 6.10
.

It appears that a coincidence has occurred in Steps 1–2: Lemma
6.5
implies that the two leading order terms from
Step 1
and
Step 2
are both order
1/2
as they have the same conormal regularity. The calculation shows they are actually
the same
up to a constant multiple and lower order terms. Steps 1–2 can be calculated in other ways, where this coincidence is related to the fact that
\Phi_{0},\Psi_{\ell}
being harmonic implies the stress-energy tensor is divergence-free.

We now return to the general case.

Step 3:
By Proposition
3.8
,
\Phi_{0}
can in general be written as

\Phi_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}c(t)\sqrt{z}\\
d(t)\sqrt{\overline{z}}\end{pmatrix}+\Phi_{1}

where the higher order terms satisfy

|\Phi_{1}|+|\nabla_{t}^{k}\Phi_{1}|\leq C_{k}r^{3/2}\hskip 56.9055pt|\nabla_{z}\Phi_{1}|+|\nabla_{t}^{k}(\nabla_{z}\Phi_{1})|\leq C_{k}r^{1/2}

(6.11)

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and identically for
\nabla_{\overline{z}}
. The resulting contribution to
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
is

-\frac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}\Phi_{1}+\frac{1}{2}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{1}

(6.12)

and using (
6.11
) and Part (C) of Corollary
6.6
shows that each term has conormal regularity one higher than the corresponding term for the leading order of
\Phi_{0}
. (
6.12
) therefore contributes an operator of order
-1/2
which can be absorbed into
K
.

Step 4:
As in Definition
3.4
, the metric in Fermi coordinates in general has the form

g_{0}=dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2}+h

where
h=O(r)
. Compared to the case of the product metric, we now have
e_{i}=\partial_{i}+O(r)
and

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}^{g_{0}}=\partial_{i}+\Gamma_{i}.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\dot{g}_{\eta}=\dot{g}_{\eta}^{\text{prod}}+h_{1}+h_{2}

(6.13)

\displaystyle dV_{g_{0}}=(1+O(r))rdrd\theta dt

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\langle-,-\rangle_{g_{0}}=(1+O(r))\langle-,-\rangle_{\text{Euc}}.

(6.14)

where
h_{1},h_{2}
are as in Corollary
5.9
. As such, each additional term in
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
has
either
an additional power of
r

or
one fewer derivative of
\eta
compared to the terms for the product case. Using Corollary
6.6
and the bounds

|\Phi_{0}|+|\nabla_{t}^{k}\Phi_{0}|\leq C_{k}r^{1/2}\hskip 56.9055pt|\nabla_{z}\Phi_{0}|+|\nabla_{t}^{k}(\nabla_{z}\Phi_{0})|\leq C_{k}r^{-1/2}

we see that all such terms have conormal regularity at least
s=5/2
. The term
\mathcal{R}(B_{0},\chi\eta)=O(1)\eta
arising in the case that
B_{0}\neq 0
likewise has conormal regularity
s>5/2
. In addition, changing
\tfrac{d}{dt}
to the covariant derivative only contributes to the lower order term
K
.

Step 5:
By Proposition
4.4
we may in general write

\Psi_{\ell}=\chi\Psi_{\ell}^{\circ}+\zeta_{\ell}^{(m)}+\xi_{\ell}^{(m)}

where the latter satisfy the bounds of Corollary
4.10
. Set

K_{1}(\eta):=\sum_{\ell}\langle\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta),\zeta^{(m)}_{\ell}\rangle e^{i\ell t}\hskip 56.9055ptK_{2}(\eta):=\sum_{\ell}\langle\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta),\xi^{(m)}_{\ell}\rangle e^{i\ell t}.

(6.15)

We claim that the second factors through the map
K_{2}:H^{2}\to H^{5/2}\to H^{3/2}
hence contributes a compact term. By Cauchy-Schwartz and the bound
\|\xi_{\ell}^{(m)}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{m}|\ell|^{-2-m}
from Corollary Corollary
4.10
,

\displaystyle\|K_{2}(\eta)\|^{2}_{H^{5/2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell}|\langle\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta),\xi^{(m)}_{\ell}\rangle|^{2}|\ell|^{5}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell}\|\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\ \|\xi^{(m)}_{\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\ |\ell|^{5}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\sum_{\ell}\frac{|\ell|^{5}}{|\ell|^{4+2m}}\ \ \ \ \leq\ C\|\eta\|_{H^{2}}^{2}\sum_{\ell}\frac{1}{|\ell|^{2m-1}}\leq C\|\eta\|^{2}_{H^{2}}

for, say,
m=2
. In the last line we have used that
|\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)|\leq(|\eta|+|\eta^{\prime}|+|\eta^{\prime\prime}|)r^{-1/2}
and the latter is integrable on normal disks.

Likewise, we claim
K_{1}
factors through the inclusion
H^{3/2+\delta}\hookrightarrow H^{3/2}
for
\delta<1/2
. This time, we apply Cauchy-Schwartz on each annulus
A_{n\ell}
(defined in
4.4
). Write
K_{1}=K_{1}^{\prime}+K_{1}^{\prime\prime}
where

K_{1}^{\prime}(\eta)=\langle\tfrac{1}{2}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).\Phi_{0},\zeta_{\ell}.\rangle\hskip 56.9055ptK_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\eta)=\langle-\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}\Phi_{0},\zeta_{\ell}\rangle

and we keep the superscript
(m)
implicit. For the first of these,

\displaystyle\|K_{1}^{\prime}(\eta)\|^{2}_{H^{3/2+\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\sum_{\ell}\sum_{n}\|\eta^{\prime\prime}|\Phi_{0}|\|^{2}_{L^{2}(A_{n\ell})}\ \|\zeta_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(A_{n\ell})}^{2}\ |\ell|^{3+2\delta}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\sum_{\ell}|\ell|^{3+2\delta}\sum_{n}\|\eta^{\prime\prime}r^{1/2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(A_{n\ell})}\ \frac{1}{|\ell|^{2}}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{n}{c_{1}}\right)

Then, since
r\sim\tfrac{(n+1)R_{0}}{|\ell|}
on
A_{n\ell}
, and each has area
O(|\ell|^{-2})
, the above is bounded by

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\eta^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{1})}\sum_{\ell}|\ell|^{3+2\delta}\sum_{n}\frac{(n+1)^{3}}{|\ell|^{5}}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{n}{c_{1}}\right)\ \ \leq\ \ C\|\eta^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{1})}\sum_{\ell}\frac{1}{|\ell|^{2-2\delta}}\ \leq\ C\|\eta\|^{2}_{H^{2}}.

The
K_{1}^{\prime\prime}
term is the same except we first use the Fourier mode restriction that
\zeta_{\ell}
has only Fourier modes
p
with
\ell-\tfrac{|\ell|}{2}\leq p\leq\ell+\tfrac{|\ell|}{2}
to write
1\sim\tfrac{i\partial_{t}}{|\ell|}
and then integrate by parts as in
Step 2
.

∎

6.3.
The Index of
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}

This section completes the proof of Theorem
6.1
by showing
T_{\Phi_{0}}
has Fredholm index 0. The key role and Fredholmness of a similar map was originally observed in
[
51
]
. Here, we present a simplified proof.

Lemma 6.11
.

When non-degeneracy as in Definition
1.3
holds,

\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}:L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})

is an elliptic pseudo-differential operator of index 0.

To begin, we have the following fact. Let
a(t)\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C})
be a smooth function and let

[H,a]=H\circ a(t)-a(t)\circ H

denote the commutator.

Claim 6.11.1
.

The commutator

[H,a]:H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C})\to H^{m+1}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C})

is a smoothing operator of order 1.

Proof.

Multiplication by
a(t)
and
H
are both elliptic pseudodifferential operators of order 0 (with lower order terms of integer order), hence so is the commutator. Using the composition property of principal symbols, its principal symbol of order 0 is

\sigma_{0}({[H,a]})=\sigma_{0}(H)\sigma_{0}({a})-\sigma_{0}(a)\sigma_{0}(H)=0

hence it is a pseudodifferential operator of order
-1
.
∎

We now prove the lemma:

Proof of Lemma
6.11
.

Given
\xi\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\simeq L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathbb{C})
we define a pseudo-inverse. Set

\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{\Phi_{0}}(\xi(t))=\overline{c}(t)H\xi(t)-d(t)\overline{\xi(t)}.

(6.16)

Using Claim
6.11.1
to move
H
past combinations of the smooth functions
c(t),d(t)
and their conjugates (and noting
H^{2}=\text{Id}
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}\circ\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{\Phi_{0}}(\xi(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((Hc(t)-d(t)\circ\text{conj}))(\overline{c}(t)H-d(t)\circ\text{conj})(\xi(t)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Hc\overline{c}H\xi-dc\overline{H\xi}-Hcd\overline{\xi}+d\overline{d}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(|c|^{2}+|d|^{2})\xi+[H,|c|^{2}]H\xi-dc(\overline{H\xi}+H\overline{\xi})-[H,cd]\overline{\xi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((|c|^{2}+|d|^{2})\text{Id}+K)\xi

for a smoothing operator
K
of order
\leq-1
. In the last line we have used
\overline{H\xi}+H\overline{\xi}=2\xi_{0}
where
\xi_{0}
is the zeroeth Fourier mode, which is clearly a smoothing operator. It follows that

\frac{1}{|c|^{2}+|d|^{2}}\mathcal{L}^{\star}_{\Phi_{0}}

provides a right pseudo-inverse for
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}
(commuting the scaling factor and
H
only contributes to the compact term). An equivalent calculation for the reverse composition shows it is also a left pseudo-inverse, thus
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}
is Fredholm.

A fixed choice of Fermi coordinates induces an isomorphism
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}\simeq\mathbb{C}
. Since
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{2}-\{0\},*)
is trivial, the pair
(c(t),d(t))
is homotopic through pairs satisfying the condition
|c(t)|^{2}+|d(t)|^{2}>0
to the constant pair
(1,0)
. The operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}
is therefore homotopic to the identity through Fredholm operators hence has index 0.
∎

Theorem
6.1
is now immediate:

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Lemma
6.8
shows that the operator

\text{ob}^{-1}(\Pi_{0}^{\perp}B_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta))=-\frac{3|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|}{2}(\Delta+1)^{-\frac{3}{4}}\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta^{\prime\prime}(t))+K

is given as the sum of following compositions:
H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
H^{3/2+\delta}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
H^{3/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
K
-\tfrac{3|\mathcal{Z}_{0}|}{2}\frac{d}{dt^{2}}
\mathcal{L}_{\Phi_{0}}
(\Delta+1)^{-\frac{3}{4}}
\simeq
\iota

where the diagonal arrow is the inclusion, hence compact. All the top arrows are Fredholm of index 0 using Lemma
6.11
; the conclusion therefore follows from the composition law for pseudodifferential operators.

∎

Given Theorem
6.1
, we now impose one more tacit assumption that this Fredholm operator of index zero is actually invertible. This is expected to hold generically (see
[
25
]
), though we do not prove such a result here. At the end of Section
8
, this assumption can be removed by the use of standard Kuranishi methods.

Assumption 6.12
.

The index zero operator
T_{\Phi_{0}}:H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\to H^{3/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})
is an isomorphism.

7.
Nash-Moser Theory

As explained in introduction, deducing the non-linear deformation result (Theorem
1.5
) from the linear one (Theorem
1.4
, Theorem
6.1
) requires the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem because of the loss of regularity in the operator
T_{\Phi_{0}}
. This section gives a brief and practical introduction to the framework of Nash-Moser Theory and states the relevant version of the implicit function theorem. The most complete reference for the full abstract theory is
[
19
]
. Here, we more closely follow the expositions in
[
50
,
48
,
1
]
which are more modest in scope but suffice for our purposes.

7.1.
Tame Fréchet Spaces

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
be Fréchet spaces given as the intersection of families of Banach spaces

\mathcal{X}:=\bigcap_{m\geq 0}X_{m}\hskip 56.9055pt\mathcal{Y}:=\bigcap_{m\geq 0}Y_{m}

(7.1)

whose norms are monotonically non-decreasing so that

\|x\|_{0}\leq\|x\|_{1}\leq\ldots

and likewise for
\mathcal{Y}
. The topologies on
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are the ones generated by the countable collection of norms, i.e. a set
U
is open if and only if for each point
x\in U
there are
r>0
and
m\geq 0
such that the ball
\{x\ |\ \|x\|_{m}<r\}\subset U
measured in the
m
-norm is contained in
U
.

Definition 7.1
.

A Fréchet space
\mathcal{X}
is said to be
tame
if it satisfies the two following additional criteria:

(I)

For all
m_{1}<m<m_{2}
there are constants
C_{m,m_{1},m_{2}}
such that the interpolation inequalities

\|x\|_{m}\leq C_{m,m_{1},m_{2}}\|x\|_{m_{1}}^{\alpha}\|x\|_{m+2}^{1-\alpha}

hold where
\alpha=\tfrac{m_{2}-m}{m_{2}-m_{1}}
.

(II)

\mathcal{X}
is equipped with a family of smoothing operators

S_{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
satisfying the following conditions.

(i)

\|S_{\varepsilon}x\|_{n}\leq C_{mn}\varepsilon^{m-n}\|x\|_{m}
for
n\geq m
and
\|S_{\varepsilon}x\|_{m}\leq C_{mn}\|x\|_{n}
for
n\leq m
.

(ii)

\|S_{\varepsilon}x-x\|_{m}\leq C_{mn}\varepsilon^{n-m}\|x\|_{n}
for
n\geq m.

(iii)

\|\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}S_{\varepsilon}x\|_{n}\leq C_{mn}\varepsilon^{m-n-1}\|x\|_{m}
for all
m,n\geq 0.

In practice, most reasonable choices of families of norms coming from Sobolev or Hölder norms are tame. Roughly speaking, smoothing operators
S_{\varepsilon}
are usually constructed by truncating local Fourier transforms at radius
\varepsilon^{-1}
in Fourier space. The Fréchet spaces used in the proof of Theorem
1.5
are introduced in Section
8.4
, and their smoothing operators are constructed in Appendix
B

Given two tame Frćhet spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
,

Definition 7.2
.

A
tame Fréchet map
on an open subset
U\subseteq\mathcal{X}

\mathcal{F}:U\to\mathcal{Y}

is a smooth map of vector spaces such that for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
the estimate

\|\mathcal{F}(x)\|_{m}\leq C_{m}\ (1+\|x\|_{m+r})

(7.2)

holds for all sufficiently large
m
.

The definitions of tame spaces and maps extend naturally to define a category of tame Fréchet manifolds with tame Fréchet maps between them (see
[
19
]
for details).
The key point about tame estimates is that each norm depends only on a fixed finite number
r
of norms larger than it. Thus, for example, a map with an estimate of the form (
7.2
) where
r=2m
would not be tame.

7.2.
The Implicit Function Theorem

Before stating a precise version of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem, let us briefly give some intuition. Here, our exposition follows
[
50
]
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is a map with
\mathcal{F}(0)=0
, and we wish to solve

\mathcal{F}(x)=f

(7.3)

for
f\in\mathcal{Y}
small. When
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are Banach spaces, the (standard) Implicit Function Theorem is proved using Newton iteration and
the Banach Fixed Point Theorem. More specifically, one begins with an initial approximation
x_{0}=0
, and (provided that
\text{d}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{F}
is invertible) defines

x_{k+1}=x_{k}+(\text{d}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{F})^{-1}(f-\mathcal{F}(x_{k})).

(7.4)

The sequence
x_{k}\to x_{\infty}
then converges to a unique fixed point solving equation (
7.3
) for
f\in\mathcal{Y}
sufficiently small. Alternatively, one can modify the iteration step (
7.4
) by inverting
\text{d}\mathcal{F}
at
x_{k}
instead of at
x_{0}
, taking

x_{k+1}=x_{k}+(\text{d}_{x_{k}}\mathcal{F})^{-1}(f-\mathcal{F}(x_{k})).

(7.5)

This iteration scheme has a much faster rate of convergence: like
\sim 2^{-2^{k}}
.

Consider now the case of
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
tame Fréchet spaces when
\text{d}\mathcal{F}
displays a loss of regularity of
r
. Given an initial bound on
f\in Y_{m}
, then
x_{1}
is bounded only in
X_{m-r}
thus
f-\mathcal{F}(x_{1})\in Y_{m-r}
and
x_{2}\in X_{m-2r}
and so forth. In this way, the standard Newton iteration scheme will exhaust the prescribed regularity in a finite number of steps. To circumvent this loss of regularity, Nash introduced iteration employing smoothing operators at each stage. More precisely, for some
\varepsilon_{k}\in(0,1]
, we set

x_{k+1}=x_{k}+(\text{d}_{S_{\varepsilon_{k}}(x_{k})}\mathcal{F})^{-1}S_{\varepsilon_{k}}(f-\mathcal{F}(x_{k})),

(7.6)

where the smoothing operators are those on
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively when applied to
x_{k}
and
f-\mathcal{F}(x_{k})
. The key point is that the rate of convergence is rapid enough to overcome the disruption of the smoothing operators, but only if we use this smoothing to modify the improved iteration (
7.5
), rather than the original iteration
(
7.4
). Thus, unlike to the Implicit Function Theorem on Banach spaces, the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem requires the linearization be invertible on a neighborhood of the initial guess, and requires bounds on the second derivatives to control the linearization over this neighborhood. Specifically, the theorem requires the following hypotheses on a tame map
\mathcal{F}:U\to\mathcal{Y}
:

Hypothesis (I)
.

There exists a
\delta_{0}>0
and an
m_{0}\geq 0
such that for
x\in U_{0}=B_{\delta_{0}}(0,m_{0})\cap\mathcal{X}
, the open ball of radius
\delta_{0}
measured in the
m_{0}
norm, then

\text{d}_{x}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}

is invertible.

Hypothesis (II)
.

With
x\in U_{0}
as above, there are fixed
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the unique solution
u
of

\text{d}_{x}\mathcal{F}(u)=f

satisfies the tame estimate

\|u\|_{m}\leq C_{m}\ \Big(\|f\|_{m+s}+\|f\|_{m_{0}}\cdot\|x\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\Big).

(7.7)

Hypothesis (III)
.

With
x\in U_{0}
as above, there are fixed
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the second derivative satisfies the tame estimate

\|\text{d}^{2}_{x}\mathcal{F}(u,\upsilon)\|_{m}\leq C_{m}\ \Big(\|u\|_{m+r}\|\upsilon\|_{m_{0}}\ +\ \|u\|_{m_{0}}\|\upsilon\|_{m+r}\ +\ \|u\|_{m_{0}}\|\upsilon\|_{m_{0}}\cdot(1+\|x\|_{m+r^{\prime}})\Big).

(7.8)

For our purposes, we require a slight extension of the standard Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem
that keeps track of subspaces that have some specified additional property, denoted
(\text{P})
.

Definition 7.3
.

A property (P) that is satisfied on linear (not necessarily closed) subspaces
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
is said to be
propagated
by the iteration scheme if

\displaystyle u\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\ ,\ f\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle S_{\varepsilon}(u)\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\ \ ,\ \ S_{\varepsilon}(f)\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1]

\displaystyle u\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(u)\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\ ,\ f\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(\text{d}_{x}\mathcal{F})^{-1}f\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}.

In particular, in the iteration scheme (
7.6
), if
f
has property (P) then
x_{k}
has property (P) for all
k\geq 0
.

We will use the following version of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem. The proof is identical to that in
[
50
]
, with the additional observation that Hypotheses
(I)
–
(III)
are only ever invoked at elements
x_{k}
occurring in the iteration, and at linear combinations of the
x_{k}
and their smoothings. The proof of smooth dependence on parameters is given in
[
19
, III.1]
.

Theorem 7.4
.

(Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem)
Suppose that
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are tame Fréchet spaces as in (
7.1
). Moreover, assume that a property (P) satisfied on linear subspaces
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
is propagated, and that Hypotheses
(I)
—
(III)
hold for
x\in U_{0}\cap\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

(A)

There exists an
m_{1}\geq m_{0}
depending on
s,s^{\prime},r,r^{\prime}
and a
\delta_{1}\geq 0
such that if
f\in\mathcal{Y}
with

f\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\hskip 42.67912pt\text{and}\hskip 42.67912pt\|f\|_{m_{1}}\leq\delta_{1}

then there exists a unique solution
x\in\mathcal{X}
of

\mathcal{F}(x)=f.

(B)

Suppose, in addition, that
\mathcal{F}
and
f
are parameterized (via a smooth tame map) by another tame Fréchet space
\mathcal{P}
with
f_{p_{0}}=0
at
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
. If the Hypotheses
(I)
–
(III)
hold uniformly on an open neighborhood
V_{0}\subset\mathcal{P}
of
p_{0}
and
\|f_{p}\|_{m_{1}}<\delta_{1}
for all
p\in V_{0}
, then the unique solution
x_{p}
of

\mathcal{F}_{p}(x)=f_{p}

also depends smoothly on
p
locally near
p_{0}
.

∎

In case (B),
smooth tame dependence on
p
means that we replace
\|x\|_{m+s^{\prime}}
and
\|x\|_{m+r^{\prime}}
on the right-hand sides of Hypothesis
(II)
and
(III)
by
\|(p,x)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}
and
\|(p,x)\|_{m+r^{\prime}}
. Case (B) is equivalent to the assertion that

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f_{p})\subset\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{X}

is locally a tame Fréchet submanifold that is a graph over
\mathcal{P}
.

8.
Tame Estimates

In this final section, we complete the proofs of Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
1.6
by verifying the hypotheses of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem
7.4
for the operator

\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}:\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}\hskip 85.35826pt\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}:=(\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}-\Lambda\,\text{Id}\ ,\ 1-\|\Phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}})

(8.1)

on tame Fréchet spaces
\mathcal{X}=\{(\eta,\Lambda,\varphi)\}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\{\psi,c\}
introduced in Section
8.4
. Here
\Lambda,c\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{P}=\{(g,B)\}
is the space of smooth metrics and perturbations (equipped with the standard Fréchet structure arising from the
H^{m}
Sobolev norms on
Y
).

In our case, the property (P) that is propagated by the iteration scheme is polyhomogeneity of the spinor. Set:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{(\eta,\Lambda,\varphi)\in\mathcal{X}\ |\ \varphi\text{ is polyhomogenous with index set }\mathbb{Z}^{+}\!+\tfrac{1}{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\ \ \ (\psi,c)\in\mathcal{Y}\hskip-0.28436pt\ \ |\ \psi\text{ is polyhomogenous with index set }\mathbb{Z}^{+}\!-\tfrac{1}{2}\}

Here, we use a slightly weaker notion of polyhomogeneity than is given in Definition
3.8
. More specifically, we do not constrain the
\theta
modes, so that
\varphi\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}},\psi\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
means that there are respectively asymptotic expansions

\displaystyle\varphi\sim\begin{pmatrix}c(t,\theta)\\
d(t,\theta)\end{pmatrix}r^{1/2}\ \ +\ \sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{p=0}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\ \ c_{n,p}(t,\theta)\ \ \\
\ \ d_{n,p}(t,\theta)\ \ \end{pmatrix}r^{n+1/2}(\log r)^{p}

(8.2)

\displaystyle\psi\sim\begin{pmatrix}c(t,\theta)\\
d(t,\theta)\end{pmatrix}r^{-1/2}+\ \sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{p=0}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\ \ c_{n,p}(t,\theta)\ \ \\
\ \ d_{n,p}(t,\theta)\ \ \end{pmatrix}r^{n-1/2}(\log r)^{p}

(8.3)

where
c_{n,p},d_{n,p}\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times S^{1})
and
\sim
denotes convergence in the sense of Definition
3.7
.

This section is divided into six subsections. Subsections
8.1
–
8.3
cover preliminary material used to verify the hypotheses of the Nash-Moser theorem. Specifically, subsections
8.1
and
8.2
are devoted to lemmas used in the verification of the Hypothesis
(I)
. Then in subsection
8.3
the precise form of the derivative and second derivative of
\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}
are derived using the non-linear version of Bourguignon-Gauduchon’s Formula (
5.9
). Subsection
8.4
introduces the tame Fréchet spaces
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
, and Subsection
8.5
derives tame estimates verifying Hypotheses
(I)-(III)
. The final subsection
8.6
invokes Theorem
7.4
to complete the proofs.

8.1.
The Obstruction Bundle

This subsection covers preliminary lemmas used in the verification of Hypothesis
(I)
which asserts that the linearization of
\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}
is invertible on a neighborhood of
((g_{0},B_{0}),\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
. Although the invertibility of the linearization ultimately comes down to the fact that there is an open neighborhood of invertible operators around the identity in a Banach space, the proper context in which to invoke this fact is somewhat subtle.
The first step is to upgrade the obstruction space
\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
to a vector bundle. This is the content of the current subsection.

We begin by defining the bundle
\text{\bf Ob}\to V_{0}
, where
V_{0}
is an open ball of radius
\delta_{0}
around
p_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
measured in the
m_{0}
-norm. Here,
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
is an integer to be chosen later (
m_{0}=11
works). Let
p\in V_{0}
. By parallel transport on cylinders as in Section
5.1
, we may think of the Dirac operator for any
p
as acting on sections of the spinor bundle
S_{0}
; we write

\not{D}_{p}:=\tau_{0}^{p}\circ\not{D}_{g,B}\circ(\tau_{0}^{p})^{-1}

(8.4)

for this incarnation of the Dirac operator with respect to
p=(g,B)
(and fixed singular locus
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
) on the spinor bundle
S_{0}
via parallel transport, which we now denote
\tau_{0}^{p}
(rather than
\tau_{g_{0}}^{g}
).

By the (standard) Implicit Function Theorem with the Fredholm operator
\not{D}_{p}^{\star}\not{D}_{p}:rH^{1}_{e}\to r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}
and (the second order analogue of) Proposition
3.8
we conclude:

Lemma 8.1
.

Provided
0<\delta_{0}
is sufficiently small, for every
p\in V_{0}
there is a unique eigenvector
(\Phi_{p},\mu_{p})\in rH^{1}_{e}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})
which satisfies

\not{D}_{p}^{\star}\not{D}_{p}\Phi_{p}=\mu_{p}\Phi_{p}

and is equal to
(\Phi_{0},0)
at
p_{0}=(g_{0},B_{0})
. Moreover, these satisfy

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{p}-\Phi_{0}\|_{H^{m_{0}-2,1}_{\text{b},e}}\ +\ |\mu_{p}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m_{0}}\|p-p_{0}\|_{m_{0}}.

and
\Phi_{p}
is polyhomogeneous with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}
.
∎

Next, let
rH^{1}_{\perp}
denote the
L^{2}
-orthogonal complement of
\Phi_{p}
in
rH^{1}_{e}
. A trivial extension of the arguments in Section
2
shows the following lemma. In the statement,
\not{D}_{p}^{\star}
denotes the adjoint of the Dirac operator with respect to the
L^{2}
-inner product formed using
g_{0}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

For
0<\delta_{0}
sufficiently small, the following hold for
p\in V_{0}
:

(A)

\not{D}_{p}:rH^{1}_{\perp}\to L^{2}
is injective with closed range.

(B)

\not{D}_{p}^{\star}\not{D}_{p}:rH^{1}_{\perp}\to r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}/\mathbb{R}\Phi_{p}
is an isomorphism and the solution operators defined by

P_{p}(f)=u\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{ s.t.}\ \ \ \ \ \ \begin{matrix}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}\not{D}_{p}u=f\mod\Phi_{p}\\
\langle u,\Phi_{p}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \end{matrix}

have uniformly (in
p
) bounded norm as operators
P_{p}:r^{-1}H^{-1}_{e}\to rH^{1}_{e}
.

∎

As a result of Item (A),
\mathfrak{R}:=\text{range}(\not{D}_{p}|_{rH^{1}_{\perp}})\subseteq L^{2}
is a smooth Banach subbundle, and we may define

Definition 8.3
.

The
Obstruction bundle
denoted
\text{\bf Ob}\to V_{0}
is defined as the
L^{2}
-orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{R}
so that there is an orthogonal splitting of the trivial bundle

V_{0}\times L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})=\text{\bf Ob}\oplus\mathfrak{R}

as smooth Banach vector bundles over
V_{0}
. For
m\leq m_{0}-3
, we denote the higher-regularity versions by
\text{\bf Ob}^{m}:=\text{\bf Ob}\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}
and
\mathfrak{R}^{m}:=\mathfrak{R}\cap H^{m}_{\text{b}}
.

Notice that
Ob
is a vector bundle by construction, without any mention of the map
ob
constructed in Section
4
. This prevents any circularity in the following proposition, the notation of which is explained in the proof.

Proposition 8.4
.

Provided
\delta_{0}
is sufficiently small, then for every
m\leq m_{0}-3
and in particular for
m=5/2
, the map

\displaystyle\Xi:V_{0}\times\left(H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\text{\bf Ob}^{m}

\displaystyle(p,\xi(t),c)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\tau_{0}^{p}\circ\text{ob}_{p}(\xi(t))\ +\ c\Pi_{p}\Phi_{p}

is a trivialization of the vector bundle
\text{\bf Ob}^{m}
. Moreover, for
(\xi(t),c)=(e^{i\ell t},0)
, the image
\text{ob}_{p}(e^{i\ell t})=\Psi_{\ell}^{p}
obeys the conclusions of Proposition
4.4
.

Proof.

The proof is a parameterized version of the construction in Section 4. Mimicking the first step in Section 4, define

\text{ob}_{p}^{\text{pre}}(\xi(t)=\Pi_{p}(\chi_{1}\mathfrak{P}^{p}_{N}(\xi(t)))

(8.5)

where
\mathfrak{P}_{N}^{p}
is the Poisson extension operator on the normal bundle formed as in Section
4.1
using the metric, perturbation, and Fermi coordinates of
p=(g,B)
. Note that (
8.5
) is an expression in Fermi coordinates and the accompanying trivialization of the spinor bundle
S_{p}
corresponding to the parameter
p=(g,B)
, rather than viewed on
S_{0}
via parallel transport. The construction of Fermi coordinates in Definition
3.4
depended on a choice of orthonormal frame along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
; to ensure smoothness as
p
varies, we adopt the convention that a frame is fixed for
p_{0}
, and the frame at
p
is defined by orthonormalizing via Gram-Schmidt beginning with the vector tangent to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. In a slight abuse of notation, (
8.5
) and what follows use
\Pi_{p}
and
\text{\bf Ob}_{p}
to denote the subspace and
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection in both
L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{p})
and to its image in
L^{2}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})
under parallel transport
(\tau_{0}^{p})^{-1}
.

The remainder of the constructions in Section 4 can be done in a parameterized way, to yield (i) a map
\text{ob}_{p}
obeying the conclusions of Proposition
4.3
, and (ii) a basis
\Psi_{\ell}^{p}
of
\text{\bf Ob}_{p}^{\perp}
obeying the conclusions of Proposition
4.4
uniformly for
p\in V_{0}
. Here
\text{\bf Ob}_{p}^{\perp}=\{\psi\in\text{\bf Ob}_{p}\ |\ \langle\psi,\Pi_{p}\Phi_{p}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
. Note here that the only distinction from the construction for
p=p_{0}
is that we not necessarily have
\Phi_{p}\in\text{\bf Ob}_{p}
in general, so the projection
\Pi_{p}
must be included here and in the definition of
\Xi
.

Our choice of parameterized Fermi coordinates ensures all these constructions are smooth in
p
(for values of
m
up to ones comparable to
m_{0}
, say
m\leq m_{0}-3
). It follows that
\text{ob}_{p}
, and thus
\Xi
are smooth maps of vector bundles. Moreover, at
p=p_{0}
,
\Xi_{0}=\text{ob}_{0}
is an isomorphism by Proposition
4.3
, and the conclusion follows from the openness of invertible maps on Banach spaces.
∎

8.2.
Invertibility on a Neighborhood

This subsection proves a partial version of Hypothesis
(I)
. Namely, we show that the
\text{\bf Ob}_{p}
component of the linearization at
(p_{0},\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
is an isomorphism; the complete version of this statement (at a general
(p,\mathcal{Z},\Phi_{p})
) is a straightforward extension and is completed in Section
8.5
.

It is worth drawing the reader’s attention to the importance of the upcoming Proposition
8.5
. In applications of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem the key point is often to show that the loss of regularity obeys some “stability” property with respect to the parameter. That is, to show that linearizations nearby the central parameter
p=p_{0}
are bounded into the same function spaces hence are
bounded
perturbations of the central linearization. In our situation here, the crux of this comes down to showing that that the notion of conormal regularity from Section
6.1
is preserved under perturbation of
p
. Proposition
8.5
below establishes this, and is the most crucial step in the proof of Theorem
1.5
with much of the remainder being essentially routine (but somewhat lengthy) verifications of tame estimates.

To begin, extend the map
T_{\Phi_{0}}
from Section
6
to include the
\lambda
-component by setting

\overline{T}_{\Phi_{0}}=\text{ob}^{-1}_{0}\circ\Pi_{0}\Big[(\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{0}(\eta,0,\lambda)\Big].

Assumption
6.12
and elliptic bootstrapping imply that
\overline{T}_{\Phi_{0}}:H^{3}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}\to H^{5/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 8.5
.

Provided that
m_{0}\geq 10
and
0<\delta_{0}<<1
is sufficiently small, then for
p\in V_{0}
, the
\text{\bf Ob}_{p}
component of the linearization at
(p_{0},\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
in the trivialization provided by
\Xi_{p}
, i.e.

\Xi^{-1}_{p}\circ\Pi_{p}(\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{0}):H^{3}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow H^{5/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R},

(8.6)

is an isomorphism, and the estimate

\|\eta\|_{H^{3}}+\|\lambda\|\leq C\|\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{0}(\eta,0,\lambda)\|_{\text{\bf Ob}^{5/2}\oplus L^{2}}

(8.7)

hold uniformly for
p\in V_{0}
.

Proof.

At
p=p_{0}
, then
\Xi^{-1}_{0}\circ\Pi_{0}=\text{ob}^{-1}\circ\Pi_{0}
so the map (
8.6
) is simply
\overline{T}_{\Phi_{0}}
thus an isomorphism (by assumption
6.12
). It therefore suffices to show that for
p\in V_{0}
with
m_{0}\geq 10
and
0<\delta_{0}<<1
sufficiently small, that (
8.6
) is bounded. Indeed, given this, Lemma
8.4
shows that (
8.6
) is a continuous family of bounded maps between fixed Banach spaces, hence is an isomorphism for
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small.

Boundedness is equivalent to the assertion that for
\overline{\eta}=(\eta,\lambda)\in H^{3}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}
one has

\Pi_{p}\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{\Phi_{0}}(\overline{\eta})\in\text{\bf Ob}_{p}\cap H^{5/2}_{\text{b}}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\|\Pi_{p}(\overline{\mathcal{B}}_{\Phi_{0}}(\overline{\eta}))\|_{H^{5/2}}\leq C\|\overline{\eta}\|_{H^{3}}.

(8.8)

where the latter estimate holds uniformly over
p\in V_{0}
. We establish (
8.8
) via a parameterized version of the conormal regularity Lemma
6.5
from Section
6.1
, which completes the proof.

Let
\text{ob}_{p},\Psi_{\ell}^{p},\Phi_{p}
, and
\tau_{0}^{p}
be as in the proof of Proposition
8.4
, and let
(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p})
denote the Fermi coordinates formed using the metric of
p=(g,B)
. Since parallel transport
\tau_{0}^{p}
is an isometry and preserves
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
(for
m\leq m_{0}-3
, by the differentiability of ODE solutions with respect to parameters), Corollary
4.10
implies that
\Pi_{p}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)\in H^{m}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})
if and only if the sequence of inner products

\text{ob}_{p}^{-1}\Pi_{p}\left((\tau_{0}^{p})^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}(\eta)}\right)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle(\tau_{0}^{p})^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta),\Psi_{\ell}^{p}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ e^{i\ell t_{p}}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta),\tau_{0}^{p}\Psi_{\ell}^{p}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}\ e^{i\ell t_{p}},

lies in
H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};\mathcal{C}_{0})
, where we again commit the abuse of notation of conflating
\Pi_{p}
on both sides of the parallel transport. Since
\Pi_{p}(\Phi_{p})\in H^{m}_{\text{b}}
for any
m
by Lemma
8.1
, the distinction between
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}},\overline{\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}}
is immaterial.

Now, we may write
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}
as a collection of terms of the form

\eta^{\prime}(t)\Theta_{0}(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{or}\hskip 28.45274pt\eta^{\prime\prime}(t)\Theta_{1}(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p}),

(8.9)

and apply Case (C) of Corollary
6.6
in the Fermi coordinates of
p
. Since
r_{p}\sim r
, the bounds
|\nabla_{t_{p}}^{m}\Theta_{k}|\leq Cr^{k-1/2}
hold for
m\leq m_{0}
equally well for
r_{p}
. It remains to write
\eta^{\prime}(t),\eta^{\prime\prime}(t)
in terms of the Fermi coordinates
(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p})
, since curves parallel to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in
g_{0}
need not be parallel anymore in
g
. Expanding in Taylor series along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in the normal directions,
\eta^{\prime}(t)=w(t_{p})+F(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p})
where
w(t_{p})\in H^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{C})
and

\displaystyle|F(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cr_{p}(|\nabla_{x_{p}}\eta^{\prime}(t)|+|\nabla_{y_{p}}\eta^{\prime}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Cr^{2}_{p}|\eta^{\prime\prime}(t)|

where we have written
x_{p}(t)=a_{0}(t)x+b_{0}(t)y+O(r)t+\ldots
and likewise for
y_{p}
. The crucial point here is that an extra factor of
r_{p}
arises since normal planes in the metrics of
p_{0}
and
p
differ to first order in
r_{p}
by a linear coordinate change of
x,y
. Similar arguments apply to the second derivative
\eta^{\prime\prime}(t)
. Applying Corollary
6.6
for the parameter
p
then shows that all terms in (
8.9
) have conormal regularity at least
5/2
. This establishes (
8.8
), completing the proof.
∎

8.3.
Quadratic and Error Terms

This section calculates linearization, the second derivative, and the initial error
f_{p}
at any arbitrary tuple
(p,\mathcal{Z},\Phi)
near
(p_{0},\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
. This is done with the non-linear version of Bourguignon-Gauduchon’s formula
[
3
]
for the metric variation of the Dirac operator.

To state Bourguignon-Gauduchon’s formula, let
p=(g,B)
be a parameter pair of a metric and perturbation on
Y
. The Dirac operator
\not{D}_{p}
is viewed as an operator on sections of
S_{0}
as in (
8.4
). Let
a_{g_{0}}^{g},\mathfrak{a}\in\text{End}(TY)
be defined respectively by

g(V,W)=g_{0}(a_{g_{0}}^{g}V,W)\hskip 85.35826pt\mathfrak{a}=(a_{g_{0}}^{g})^{-1/2}

where the latter is understood via the eigenvalues of
(a_{g_{0}}^{g})^{\star}a_{g_{0}}^{g}
, which are non-zero for
h
sufficiently close to
g_{0}
.

Theorem 8.6
.

(Bourguignon-Gauduchon,
[
3
]
)
The Dirac operator
\not{D}_{p}
is given by

\not{D}_{p}\Psi=\left(\sum_{i}e^{i}.\nabla^{B}_{\mathfrak{a}(e_{i})}\ +\ \frac{1}{4}\sum_{ij}e^{i}e^{j}.\left(\mathfrak{a}^{-1}(\nabla^{g_{0}}_{\mathfrak{a}(e_{i})}\mathfrak{a})e^{j}+\mathfrak{a}^{-1}(\nabla^{g}-\nabla^{g_{0}})_{\mathfrak{a}(e_{i})}\mathfrak{a}(e^{j})\right).\right)\Psi

(8.10)

where
e^{i}
and
.
are an orthonormal basis and Clifford multiplication for
g_{0}
, and
\nabla^{g}
denotes the unperturbed spin connection of the metric
g
and likewise for
g_{0}
.

8.3.1.
Error Terms:

We begin by applying Theorem
8.6
to calculate the initial error terms
f_{p}
for the application of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem (
7.4
). The initial error is given by

\boxed{f_{p}:=\not{D}_{p}\Phi_{0}.}

(8.11)

Let
U_{1}\subset\mathcal{P}
denote the ball around
p_{0}
of radius
\delta_{1}
measured in the
m_{1}+3
norm. Here,
m_{1}
(like
m_{0}
) is an integer to be chosen later. To simplify notation, we omit the reference to the spaces from the notation from the norms, so that e.g.
\|-\|_{m}
means the
H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
-norm for elements of the domain, the
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
-norm for elements of the codomain.

Lemma 8.7
.

The Dirac operator at parameter
p
can be written

\not{D}_{p}=\not{D}_{0}+\mathfrak{D}_{p}

(8.12)

where the latter satisfies

\|\mathfrak{D}_{p}\varphi\|_{m_{1}}\leq C_{m_{1}}\|p\|_{m_{1}+3}\|\varphi\|_{m_{1}}.

(8.13)

It follows that
\|f_{p}\|_{m_{1}}\leq C\delta_{1}
.

Proof.

Write
p=(g_{0},B_{0})+(k,b)
for
\|(k,b)\|_{m_{1}+3}\leq\delta
. In an orthonormal frame for
g_{0}
we have
a_{g_{0}}^{g_{0}+k}=\text{Id}+k
where we also use
k
to denote the corresponding matrix in this orthonormal frame. Then
\mathfrak{a}=(\text{Id}+k)^{-1/2}
. Substituting this into (
8.10
) shows that

\not{D}_{p}\varphi=\not{D}_{0}\varphi+\mathfrak{d}_{1}\varphi+\mathfrak{d}_{0}\varphi

where
\mathfrak{d}_{1},\mathfrak{d}_{0}
are respectively a first order and zeroth order operator satisfying
\|\mathfrak{d}_{1}\varphi\|_{m_{1}}\leq C\|p\|_{m_{1}+3}\|\varphi\|_{m_{1}}
and
\|\mathfrak{d}_{0}\varphi\|_{m_{1}}\leq C\|p\|_{m_{1}+3}\|\varphi\|_{m_{1}}
. To see this, note that the coefficients of
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
are formed from sums and products of entries of
k
(by expanding
(\text{Id}+k)^{-1/2})
, and these all lie in
C^{m_{1}+1}(Y)\hookrightarrow H^{m_{1}+3}(Y)
by the Sobolev embedding and the fact that
C^{m_{1}+1}
is an algebra. Likewise, coefficients of
\mathfrak{d}_{0}
lie in
C^{m_{1}}(Y)
because they are formed from sums and products of up to first derivatives of
k,b
. Since every term is at least linear in
p
, and
\|(k,b)\|_{m_{1}+3}\leq\delta_{1}<<1
, the bound (
8.13
) follows.

Since
f_{p}=\not{D}_{p}\Phi_{0}=\mathfrak{d}_{0}\Phi_{0}+\mathfrak{d}_{1}\Phi_{0}
and
\|\Phi_{0}\|_{m_{1}}\leq C_{m_{1}}
, the second statement is then immediate for
p\in U_{1}
. ∎

8.3.2.
Quadratic Terms

For the tame estimates on
\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}
and
\text{d}^{2}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}
, we must first investigate the higher-order terms of
\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}
. Expanding, we may write

\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}((\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})+(\eta,\varphi))=f_{p}\ +\ \text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(\eta,\varphi)\ +\ Q_{p}(\eta,\varphi)

where
Q_{p}
is comprised of second order and higher terms.

The middle term at
p_{0}
is given by Corollary
5.9
. For a general
p
, we can write the derivative of pullback metric as

\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}F_{s\eta}^{*}(g_{0}+k)=\dot{g}_{\eta}+\dot{k}_{\eta},

(8.14)

where
\dot{g}_{\eta}
is as calculated in (
5.14
) and analogously for
k
. Analogous to the formula for
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
in Corollary
5.9
, we set

\mathfrak{B}_{\Phi_{0},p}(\eta):=\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}\dot{k}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla^{g_{0}}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{k}_{\eta}).+\frac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{k}_{\eta}).+\mathcal{R}(b,\chi\eta).\right)\Phi_{0}

(8.15)

to be the term arising from the perturbation
(k,b)
to
p_{0}
. Here
\mathcal{R}(b,\chi\eta)
is a zeroth order term in
\eta
with coefficients depending on the perturbation
b
to
B_{0}
and its derivatives. The proof of the following proposition is given in Appendix
C
.

Proposition 8.8
.

The universal Dirac operator at the parameter
p\in U_{1}
for at a point
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})+(\eta,\varphi)
with
\|(\eta,\varphi)\|_{m_{0}}\leq C\delta
is given by

\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}((\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})+(\eta,\varphi))=f_{p}\ +\ \text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(\eta,\varphi)\ +\ Q_{p}(\eta,\varphi)

(8.16)

where

(A)

f_{p}=\not{D}_{p}\Phi_{0}
as in Lemma
8.7
.

(B)

The derivative is given by

\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(\eta,\varphi)=\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)+\not{D}_{0}\varphi\Big)\ +\ \Big(\mathfrak{B}_{\Phi_{0},p}(\eta)+\mathfrak{D}_{p}(\varphi)\Big)

where
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)
is as defined in
5.5
(cf. Corollary
5.9
), and
\mathfrak{D}_{p},\mathfrak{B}_{\Phi_{0},p}
are as in (
8.12
) and (
8.15
) respectively.

(C)

The non-linear terms may be written

\displaystyle Q_{p}(\eta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\varphi,p})(\eta)\ \ +\ \ M^{1}_{p}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ \ +\ \ M^{2}_{p}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ \ +\ \ F_{p}(\eta,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)

where

(i)

\mathcal{B}_{\varphi},\mathfrak{B}_{\varphi,p}
are defined identically to
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}},\mathfrak{B}_{\Phi_{0},p}
but with
\varphi
replacing
\Phi_{0}
.

(ii)

M_{1}^{p}
a finite sum of terms involving quadratic combinations of
\chi\eta^{\prime},\eta d\chi,\chi\eta
, and linearly depending on
\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
and smooth endomorphisms
m_{i}
, e.g.

m_{i}(y)(\chi\eta^{\prime})(\chi\eta^{\prime})\nabla_{j}(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)

where
m_{i}(y)
depend on
g_{0}+k
(and no derivatives).

(iii)

M_{2}^{p}
a finite sum of terms involving quadratic combinations of
\eta^{\prime\prime}\chi,\eta^{\prime}d\chi,\eta d^{2}\chi,\eta^{\prime}\chi,\eta d\chi,\eta\chi
, with at most one factor of
\eta^{\prime\prime}
, and linearly depending on
\Phi_{0}+\varphi
and smooth endomorphisms
m_{i}
, e.g.

m_{i}(y)(\chi\eta^{\prime\prime})(d\chi\eta^{\prime}).(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)

where
m_{i}(y)
depend on up to first derivatives of
g_{0}+k
and
B_{0}+b
.

(iv)

F_{p}
is formed from a finite sum of similar terms but involving cubic and higher combinations of
\eta,\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}
, with at most one factor of
\eta^{\prime\prime}
.

Straightforward differentiation now shows the following precise forms for the first and second derivatives. In these formulas, we use the notation that e.g.
F(p^{3},q^{2},s)
denotes a term depending cubicly on
p
and its derivatives, quadratically on
q
and its derivatives, and linearly on
s
and its derivatives:

Corollary 8.9
.

The derivative at a point
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})+(\eta,\varphi)
is given by

\displaystyle\text{d}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(v,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(v,\phi)

\displaystyle\ +\ (\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\varphi})(v)\ +\ (\mathcal{B}_{\phi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\phi})(\eta)

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime})\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ \ +\ M^{2}(v^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\phi

\displaystyle\ +\ F^{1}(\eta^{2},v,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ F^{2}(\eta^{3},\phi)

where the subscript
p
is kept implicit on the right hand side. Moreover, provided
\|\eta\|_{C^{1}}\leq 1
, the cubic and higher order terms
F^{1}
and
F^{2}
are of the form

F^{2}(\eta^{3},\phi)=O(\|\eta^{\prime}\|_{C^{1}})\Big[M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\nabla\phi\Big]+O(\|\eta^{\prime}\|_{C^{1}})\Big[M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\phi\Big]

(8.17)

and likewise for
F^{1}
, i.e. they include the same orders of derivatives as the quadratic terms but with additional powers of
\eta^{\prime},\eta
.

Alternatively, the terms linear in
\phi
in the expression of Corollary
8.9
combine to form the Dirac operator

\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}\phi=\not{D}_{p}\phi+\ (\mathcal{B}_{\phi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\phi})(\eta)+M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\nabla\phi+M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\phi+F^{2}(\eta^{3},\phi)

(8.18)

with respect to the pullback metric and perturbation
p_{\eta}:=F^{*}_{\eta}(p)
. In particular, no terms involving
\phi
have components in
\text{\bf Ob}^{p}
, provided
\langle\phi,\Phi_{p}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
.

Corollary 8.10
.

The second derivative at a point
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})+(\eta,\varphi)
is given by

\displaystyle\text{d}^{2}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}\Big((v,\phi),(w,\psi)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \ (\mathcal{B}_{\psi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\psi})(v)\ +\ (\mathcal{B}_{\phi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\phi})(w)

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{1}(w^{\prime},v^{\prime})\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime})\nabla\psi\ +\ M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},w^{\prime})\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{2}(w^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ M^{2}(v^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\psi

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{2}(v^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\psi\ +\ M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})\phi\ +\ M^{2}(w^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\phi

\displaystyle\ +\ F^{3}(\eta^{2},w,\phi)\ +\ F^{4}(\eta^{2},v,\psi)\ +\ F^{5}(\eta,v,w,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)

where the subscript
p
is kept implicit on the right hand side. The higher order terms again have the form (
8.17
) as in Corollary
8.9
.∎

8.4.
Tame Fréchet Spaces

This section introduces the tame Fréchet spaces used in the proof of Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
1.6
.

While there is a natural Fréchet space of normal vector fields
\eta
(this being
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
with the Fréchet structure arising from the
H^{m}
-norms), there are several possible choices of Fréchet spaces for the spinors, arising from different versions of the boundary and edge spaces. The relevant spinors are those lying in
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
for the domain and codomain respectively, i.e. those spinors with polyhomogeneous expansions (
8.2
-
8.3
). While the spaces
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
are themselves tame Fréchet spaces, these Fréchet structures are rather unwieldy and it is advantageous to enlarge the domain and codomain to spaces where it is easier to obtain estimates and then invoke Theorem
7.4
with the property (P) of polyhomogeneity which holds on
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

The mixed boundary and edge spaces
rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
and
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
defined in Section
2
enlarge the domain and codomain and their norms facilitate much easier estimates using the material of Sections
2
-
4
. Unfortunately, these spaces are slightly too large and it is impossible to control the higher order terms of the expansions (
8.2
-
8.3
) simply in terms of of these norms. To balance these conflicting advantages of
rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
, we opt for intermediate spaces which supplement the
rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
and
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
-norms with the norm of the higher order terms in (
8.2
-
8.3
) using a stronger weight.

Analogously to
rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
and
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
denote
r^{1+\nu}H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
and
r^{\nu}H^{m}_{\text{b}}
the spaces formed by adding an overall weight of
r^{-2\nu}
in the norm (
2.13
). Equivalently,

\varphi\in rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ r^{\nu}\varphi\in r^{1+\nu}H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}

so that the multiplication map
r^{\nu}
is a linear isomorphism bounded by a constant depending only on
m
, and similarly for
r^{\nu}H^{m}_{\text{b}}
. Fix
\nu=0.9
and define Banach spaces

\displaystyle r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\varphi\ \ \Big|\ \ \|\varphi\|_{r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}}:=\left(\|\varphi\|^{2}_{rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}}\ +\ \|(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})\varphi\|^{2}_{r^{1+\nu}H^{m-1,1}_{e}}\ \right)^{1/2}<\infty\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{m,0}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\psi\ \ \Big|\ \ \|\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{m,0}}:=\left(\ \|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}\ +\ \|(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})\psi\|^{2}_{r^{\nu}H^{m-1}_{\text{b}}}\ \right)^{1/2}<\infty\right\}

\displaystyle r^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{m,-1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\psi\ \ \Big|\ \ \|\psi\|_{r^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{m,-1}}:=\left(\ \|\psi\|^{2}_{r^{-1}H^{m,-1}_{\text{b},e}}\ +\ \|(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{3}{2})\psi\|^{2}_{r^{-1+\nu}H^{m-1,-1}_{\text{b},e}}\ \right)^{1/2}<\infty\right\}.

with the indicated norms. These spaces are defined using the Fermi coordinates and norms of the base parameter
p_{0}
and do not depend on
p\in\mathcal{P}
.

Using these, we now define the spaces used in the proofs of Theorem
1.5
and
1.6
.

Lemma 8.11
.

The spaces

\mathcal{X}:=\bigcap_{m\geq 0}X_{m}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus X_{m}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 56.9055pt\mathcal{Y}:=\bigcap_{m\geq 0}Y_{m}^{\prime}\oplus Y_{m}^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathbb{R}

where

\displaystyle X_{m}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\hskip 116.65646ptY_{m}^{\prime}:=\text{\bf Ob}\cap H^{m+2}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})

(8.19)

\displaystyle X_{m}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})\hskip 99.58464ptY_{m}^{\prime\prime}:={\mathfrak{R}}\ \cap\ \mathcal{H}^{m,0}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})

(8.20)

and
\mathbb{R}
has the standard norm are tame Fréchet spaces as in Definition
7.1
, and on an open neighborhood
U\subset\mathcal{X}
,

\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}:\mathcal{P}\times U\to\mathcal{Y}

is a tame Fréchet map.

Proof.

The interpolation inequalities in item (I) of Definition
7.1
are immediate from those on the standard spaces
H^{m}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
and those from Lemma
2.10
which apply equally well for different weights. The smoothing operators whose existence is the content of item (II) of Definition
7.1
are constructed in Appendix
B
.

That
\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}
is a tame Fréchet map is obvious for the
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
-norms, and for the
(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})
terms follows from the commutation relations in the upcoming Lemma
8.14
.
∎

Remark 8.12
.

Since
Ob
consists of solutions of the elliptic edge operator (this being
\not{D}
or
\not{D}-\Lambda_{p}\text{Id}
) which have expansions with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}-\tfrac{1}{2}
, edge bootstrapping (see
[
47
]
Equation (7.7) and the accompanying discussion) implies that for
\psi\in\text{\bf Ob}
obeys

\|(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})\psi\|_{rH^{m-1}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}.

Since
\nu\leq 1
it follows that the
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
and the
\mathcal{H}^{m,0}
norms are equivalent on
Y_{m}^{\prime}
.

As explained at the beginning of the subsection, the point is that the additional terms allows control of the higher order terms of expansions (
8.2
-
8.3
) in
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\cap r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}
. The following key lemma, proved in Appendix
B
makes this precise:

Lemma 8.13
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in r\mathcal{H}^{m+4,1}
is a spinor. Then the bound

|(\nabla^{\text{b}})^{m}\varphi|\leq C_{m}r^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{r\mathcal{H}^{m+4,1}}

holds pointwise on
Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.
∎

The final two lemmas needed before the verification of Hypotheses
(I)-(III)
are effectively bookkeeping that show the Dirac operator
\not{D}:r\mathcal{H}^{1,1}\to\mathcal{H}^{1,0}
has the same semi-Fredholm properties as on the spaces from Section
2
. Fixing a parameter
p
, let
r\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
be the
L^{2}
-orthogonal complement of
\Phi_{p}
in
r\mathcal{H}^{1,1}\subset rH^{1}_{e}
. Additionally, fixing a spinor
\varphi
near
0
, denote the extended Dirac operator with the
\mathbb{R}
-factor included at a parameter
p
by

\overline{\not{D}}_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}\not{D}_{p}&0\\
\langle-,\Phi_{0}+\varphi\rangle&\langle-,\Phi_{0}+\varphi\rangle\end{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}r\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\\
\oplus\\
\mathbb{R}\Phi_{p}\end{matrix}\ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \begin{matrix}\mathfrak{R}_{p}\\
\oplus\\
\mathbb{R},\end{matrix}

which arises as the partial derivative at
\Phi_{0}+\varphi
of (
8.1
) with respect to the spinor.

Lemma 8.14
.

Provided that
m_{0}\geq 10
and
0<\delta_{0}<<1
is sufficiently small, then for
p\in V_{0}
the extended Dirac operator

\overline{\not{D}}_{p}:r\mathcal{H}^{1,1}\oplus\mathbb{R}\to(\mathfrak{R}_{p}\cap\mathcal{H}^{1,0})\oplus\mathbb{R}

is an isomorphism and the estimate

\|\psi\|_{r\mathcal{H}^{1,1}}\leq C\|\overline{\not{D}}_{p}\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1,0}\oplus\mathbb{R}}

(8.21)

holds uniformly for
p\in V_{0}
.

Proof.

We begin by showing that the (unextended) Dirac operator
\not{D}
satisfies the following estimate: if
\not{D}\psi=f
then

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{rH^{1,1}_{\text{b},e}}+\|(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})\psi\|_{r^{1+\nu}H^{1}_{e}}\leq C\Big(\|f\|_{H^{1}_{\text{b}}}+\|(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})f\|_{r^{\nu}L^{2}})+\|\psi\|_{r^{\nu}H^{1}_{\text{b}}}\Big).

(8.22)

Since
\nu<1
by choice, the inclusion
rH^{1,1}_{\text{b},e}\hookrightarrow r^{\nu}H^{1}_{\text{b}}
is compact, hence so is the final term on the right.

We first prove (
8.22
) for
p=p_{0}
. That the first term is bounded by the right-hand side immediate from the estimate for
\not{D}:rH^{1,1}_{\text{b},e}\to H^{1}_{\text{b}}
(Corollary
2.11
). For the second term, we apply the elliptic estimate

\|\psi\|_{r^{1+\nu}H^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\Big(\|\not{D}\psi\|_{r^{\nu}L^{2}}+\|\psi\|_{r^{\nu}L^{2}}\Big)

(8.23)

for
\not{D}:r^{1+\nu}H^{1}_{e}\to r^{\nu}L^{2}
to term
(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})\psi
. This estimate cannot be derived by integration by parts as in Section
2
and instead follows from parametrix methods (see Theorem 6.1 of
[
47
]
or
[
72
]
). Then, since the commutation relations

\displaystyle(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})\partial_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})

(8.24)

\displaystyle(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})\tfrac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2}).

(8.25)

hold writing
\not{D}=\not{D}_{0}+\mathfrak{d}
as in Lemma
3.6
shows that

\not{D}(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})\psi=(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})\not{D}\psi+B\psi

where
B
is a lower order term such that
B:r^{\nu}H^{1}_{b}\to r^{\nu}L^{2}
is bounded. Applying (
8.23
) and substituting this expression yields (
8.22
) for
p=p_{0}
.

The estimate (
8.21
) follows from a standard proof by contradiction (e.g.
[
42
]
Lemma 10.4.9), provided
\varphi
is sufficiently small. For
p\neq p_{0}
it is straightforward to show that writing
{\not{D}}_{p}={\not{D}}_{p_{0}}+\mathfrak{d}_{p}
and using the commutations (
8.24
-
8.25
) yields

\|\mathfrak{d}_{p}\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1,0}}\leq C\delta_{0}\|\psi\|_{r\mathcal{H}^{1,1}}

completing the lemma.

∎

Finally, the projection operators to
Ob
and
{\mathfrak{R}}
are well-behaved on the new spaces analogously to Corollary
2.12
item (C).

Lemma 8.15
.

The projection operators

1-\Pi_{p}=\not{D}_{p}P_{p}\not{D}^{\star}_{p}:\mathcal{H}^{m}\to\mathfrak{R}_{p}\cap\mathcal{H}^{m}\hskip 56.9055pt\Pi_{p}=1-\not{D}_{p}P_{p}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}:\mathcal{H}^{m}\to\text{\bf Ob}_{p}\cap\mathcal{H}^{m}

are bounded.

Proof.

For the
{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}
-term of the
\mathcal{H}^{m}
-norm this follows directly from Corollary
2.12
. For the second term, notice that by (
8.23
) and the analogous estimate for
\not{D}^{\star}_{p}\not{D}_{p}:r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}\to r^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{m,-1}
, one has that

\not{D}_{p}P_{p}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}:r^{\nu}H^{m}_{\text{b}}\to r^{\nu}H^{m}_{\text{b}}

(8.26)

is bounded. Writing

(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})D_{p}P_{p}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}=D_{p}P_{p}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})+[(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2}),D_{p}P_{p}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}]

and applying (
8.26
) to the first term, then using that
[(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2}),D_{p}P_{p}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}]:H^{m}_{\text{b}}\to rH^{m-1}_{\text{b}}\subseteq r^{\nu}H^{m-1}_{\text{b}}
is bounded for the second term yields the result.

To finish, we therefore prove that the commutator
[(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2}),D_{p}P_{p}\not{D}_{p}^{\star}]:H^{m}_{\text{b}}\to rH^{m-1}_{\text{b}}\subseteq r^{\nu}H^{m-1}_{\text{b}}
is bounded, beginning with the product metric on
Y_{\circ}=S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
as in Example
3.2
. In the product case, the commutation relations (
8.24
-
8.25
) imply

\displaystyle\not{D}_{0}(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})\not{D}_{0}\phi-\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}\phi

\displaystyle P_{0}(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{3}{2})f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})P_{0}f+P_{0}(\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}\not{D}_{0}+\not{D}_{0}\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t})P_{0}f

where
P_{0}f=u\Leftrightarrow\not{D}_{0}\not{D}_{0}u=f
, and
\gamma_{t}=\gamma(dt)
. The latter expression follows from applying the first twice with
\not{D}_{0}\not{D}_{0}
and then applying
P_{0}
. Using these, one has

\displaystyle(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})\not{D}_{0}P_{0}\not{D}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{D}_{0}(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})P_{0}\not{D}_{0}+\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}P_{0}\not{D}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{D}_{0}P_{0}(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{3}{2})\not{D}_{0}-\not{D}_{0}P_{0}(\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}\not{D}_{0}+\not{D}_{0}\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t})P_{0}\not{D}_{0}+\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}P_{0}\not{D}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\not{D}_{0}P_{0}\not{D}_{0}(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})+\not{D}_{0}P_{0}\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}-\not{D}_{0}(P_{0}(\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}\not{D}_{0}+\not{D}_{0}\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t})P_{0})\not{D}_{0}-\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}P_{0}\not{D}_{0}

so that

[(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2}),\not{D}_{0}P_{0}\not{D}_{0}]=\not{D}_{0}P_{0}\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}-\not{D}_{0}(P_{0}(\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}\not{D}_{0}+\not{D}_{0}\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t})P_{0})\not{D}_{0}+\gamma_{t}\nabla_{t}P_{0}\not{D}_{0}.

Each term in the above has a factor of
r
better than
a priori
might be expected: the final term, for instance, is a composition of bounded operators

H^{m}_{\text{b}}\ \overset{\not{D}_{0}}{\longrightarrow}\ r^{-1}H^{m,-1}_{\text{b}}\ \overset{P_{0}}{\longrightarrow}\ rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}\ \overset{\nabla_{t}}{\longrightarrow}\ rH^{m-1,1}_{\text{b},e}\ \hookrightarrow\ rH^{m-1}_{\text{b}}

and similarly for the first and middle terms.

For the parameter
p=p_{0}
the error terms arising from the difference
\mathfrak{d}=\not{D}_{p_{0}}-\not{D}_{0}
also has an additional factor of
r
. Indeed,
|\mathfrak{d}\psi|\leq C(r|\nabla\psi|+|\psi|)
as in Lemma
3.6
is bounded
r^{1+\nu}H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}\to r^{1+\nu}H^{m}_{\text{b}}
. For a general
p
, the same argument applies in the Fermi coordinates formed using
p
for the boundary Sobolev spaces defined using
p
, whose norms are uniformly (tamely) equivalent.
∎

8.5.
Tame Estimates for the Linearization

This subsection verifies Hypotheses
(I)-(III)
of the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem from Section
Hypothesis (I)
, employing the formulas for
\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}
and
\text{d}^{2}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}
from Corollaries
8.9
and
8.10
.

Recall that
V_{0}\subset\mathcal{P}
denotes the open ball of radius
\delta_{1}
around
p_{0}
measured in the
m_{0}
-norm, and let
U_{0}\subset\mathcal{X}
denote the ball of the same radius around
(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})
, also measured in the
m_{0}
-norm.

Lemma 8.16
.

Hypothesis
(I)
of Theorem
7.4
holds for
\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}
, i.e. there is an
m_{0}\geq 0
such that for
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small,
p\in V_{0}
that implies the linearization

\text{d}_{(\varphi,\eta)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}

is invertible for
(\eta,\varphi)\in U_{0}\cap\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

Fix
m_{0}\geq 11
. We first investigate the obstruction component of the linearization. By decreasing
\delta_{1}
, we can ensure the
p\in V_{0}
and
(\eta,\varphi)\in U_{0}
implies that the pullback of the parameter by the diffeomorphism
F_{\eta}
defined in (
5.1
) satisfies

p_{\eta}=F_{\eta}^{*}(g,B)\in V_{0}^{\prime}

(8.27)

where
V_{0}^{\prime}
is the ball of radius
\delta_{0}
in the
m_{0}-1=10
-norm, hence Proposition
8.5
applies.

Using Corollary
8.9
, the
\text{\bf Ob}_{p_{\eta}}
-component of the linearization in the trivialization of Lemma
8.4
acting on
(v,\lambda,0)\in X_{0}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus X_{0}^{\prime\prime}
may be written

\displaystyle\overline{T}_{p,\eta}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Xi_{p_{\eta}}\circ\Pi_{p_{\eta}}(\text{d}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}(v,0,\lambda))

(8.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Xi_{p_{\eta}}\circ\Pi_{p_{\eta}}(\text{d}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{0},\Phi_{0})}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{0}(v,0,\lambda)\ +\ldots+\ F^{2}(\eta^{2},v,\Phi_{0}+\varphi))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{T}_{0,p}(v,\lambda)\ +\ \mathfrak{t}_{p_{\eta}}(v,\lambda)

where
\overline{T}_{0,p}
denotes the invertible map (
8.6
) from Proposition
8.5
, and
\mathfrak{t}_{p_{\eta}}
encompasses the error terms. Explicitly, via Corollary
8.9
)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{p_{\eta}}(v,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Xi_{p_{\eta}}\circ\Pi_{p_{\eta}}\Big[\mathfrak{B}_{\Phi_{0},p}(v)+\ (\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\varphi})(v)\ +\ M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime})\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)

(8.29)

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ \ +\ M^{2}(v^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ F^{2}(\eta^{2},v,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)-\lambda\varphi\Big].

Since
\overline{T}_{0,p}:H^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}\to H^{5/2}\oplus\mathbb{R}
is invertible by Proposition
8.5
, we show that the perturbation
\mathfrak{t}_{p_{\eta}}:H^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}\to H^{5/2}\oplus\mathbb{R}
is bounded, i.e. that there is a constant
C_{1}
such that

\|\mathfrak{t}_{p_{\eta}}(v,\lambda)\|_{H^{5/2}}\leq C\delta_{0}\Big(\|v\|_{H^{3}}\ +\ |\lambda|\Big).

(8.30)

holds for
(v,\eta)\in X_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{R}=H^{3}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}
.

(
8.30
) follows from the same argument as (
8.8
) in Proposition
8.5
. Indeed, Proposition
8.8
shows that each term of
\mathfrak{t}_{p_{\eta}}
is of the form either
v^{\prime}(t)\Theta_{0}(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p})
or
v^{\prime\prime}(t)\Theta_{1}(t_{p},x_{p},y_{p})
just as in (
8.8
). Here,
\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1}
can be written more explicitly using the notation of Corollary
8.8
as the sum of terms
m_{p_{\eta}}(y)\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
and
m_{0}(y)\nabla\varphi
or
m_{p_{\eta}}(y)(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
and
m_{0}(y)\varphi
, where
m_{p_{\eta}},m_{0}
are smooth endomorphisms bounded in terms of the
m_{0}
norms of
p_{\eta},p_{0}
respectively. For the subterms of
\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1}
involving
\Phi_{0}
, the argument as in Proposition
8.5
shows that there are pointwise bounds
