|\nabla_{t}^{m}\Theta_{k}|\leq Cr^{k-1/2}\|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+m_{0}}

for
k=1,2
. It then follows from Corollary
6.6
(applied in the Fermi coordinates of
p_{\eta}
) that these terms have cornormal regularity at least
5/2
for
v\in H^{3}
. For the subterms of
\Theta_{0},\Theta_{1}
involving
\varphi
rather than
\Phi_{0}
, the same applies using Lemma
8.13
in place of the universal bounds (
3.10
) on the expansion of
\Phi_{0}
. (
8.30
) follows, and we conclude that
8.28
is an isomorphism
\overline{T}_{p,\eta}:H^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}\to H^{5/2}\oplus\mathbb{R}
.

For
m=1
, the full linearization acting on
(v,\lambda,\phi)
now has the block-diagonal form

\text{d}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}&\pi_{p_{\eta}}\\
\\
(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})\mathcal{B}&\overline{\not{D}}_{p_{\eta}}\end{pmatrix}\ :\ \begin{matrix}H^{3}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\oplus\mathbb{R}\\
\oplus\\
r\mathcal{H}^{1,1}\end{matrix}\ \ \longrightarrow\ \ \begin{matrix}\text{\bf Ob}^{5/2}_{p_{\eta}}\\
\oplus\\
(\mathfrak{R}_{p_{\eta}}\cap\mathcal{H}^{1,0})\oplus\mathbb{R}.\end{matrix}.

(8.31)

where
\mathcal{B}=\text{d}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}-\overline{\not{D}}_{p_{\eta}}
is the partial derivative with respect to
v
(Cf.
8.18
), and
\Pi_{p_{\eta}}
is the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection to
\text{\bf Ob}_{p_{\eta}}
, and

\pi_{p_{\eta}}(\phi)=\langle\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}\phi,\Pi_{p_{\eta}}\Phi_{p_{\eta}}\rangle_{L^{2}}\Pi_{p_{\eta}}\Phi_{p_{\eta}}

(8.32)

has rank 1 (
\Phi_{p_{\eta}}
being the eigenvector from Lemma
8.1
with parameter
p_{\eta}
from
8.27
). In writing (
8.31
), we commit the minor abuse of notation of conflating
\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}
and
\text{ob}_{p_{\eta}}\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}
.

If the rank
1
component
\star
did not appear, (
8.31
) would obviously be an isomorphism if
\mathcal{B}
were bounded, since we have shown above that (
8.28
) is an isomorphism, and the bottom right entry is an isomorphism by construction. In fact, the presence of the rank 1 entry makes no difference, because
\pi_{p_{\eta}}=O(\delta_{0})
; indeed,

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{p_{\eta}}\Phi_{p_{\eta}}\|_{5/2}=\|\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}^{\star}P_{p_{\eta}}\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}\Phi_{p_{\eta}}\|_{5/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}^{\star}P_{p_{\eta}}[\mathfrak{d}_{p_{\eta}}(\Phi_{p_{\eta}})+\not{D}_{0}(\Phi_{p_{\eta}}-\Phi_{0})]\|_{5/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|(p-p_{0},\eta)\|_{m_{0}}\leq C\delta_{0}.

To conclude (
8.31
) is an isomorphism, it therefore only remains to show that
\mathcal{B}:H^{3}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\to\mathcal{H}^{1,0}\oplus\mathbb{R}
is bounded. Boundedness into
H^{1}_{\text{b}}
is obvious; for the boundedness of
(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})
into
r^{\nu}L^{2}
, note that since
\varphi\in r\mathcal{H}^{1,1}\cap\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
is polyhomogeneous with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}
, the operator
(r\partial_{r}+\tfrac{1}{2})
annihilates the order
r^{-1/2}
term of
\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
and all other terms are
O(r^{1/2})
so are integrable with the stronger weight in the normal directions. Since
\mathcal{B}
consists of product of
\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
or
\Phi_{0}+\varphi
with terms having integer Taylor expansions along
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
,
\mathcal{B}
is likewise integrable in the higher weight, hence bounded.

If follows that (
8.31
) is an isomorphism for
m=1
. invertibility for higher
m
, thus on
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
follows from bootstrapping using the tame estimate in the next lemma.
∎

Lemma 8.17
.

Hypothesis
(II)
of Theorem
7.4
holds for
\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}
, i.e. there are
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following estimates hold provided
\delta_{0}
is sufficiently small: for
p\in V_{0}
and
(\varphi,\eta)\in U_{0}
the unique solution
u=(v,\phi,\lambda)
of

\text{d}_{(\varphi,\eta)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}u=f

obeys the tame estimate

\|u\|_{m}\leq C_{m}\ \Big(\|f\|_{m+s}+\|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f\|_{m_{0}}\Big).

(8.33)

uniformly over
V_{0}\times(U_{0}\cap\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}})
for all
m\geq m_{0}
.

Proof.

We claim that it suffices show that there are tame elliptic estimates of the following form for
\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}
and
\overline{\not{D}}_{p_{\eta}}
individually: if
\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}v=f_{0}
and
\overline{\not{D}}_{p_{\eta}}\phi=f_{1}
then

\displaystyle\|(v,\lambda)\|_{m}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{0}\|_{m+3/2}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f_{0}\|_{m_{0}}\Big)

(8.34)

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{m}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{1}\|_{m}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f_{1}\|_{m_{0}}\Big)

(8.35)

for
m_{0}=11
.

Indeed, given (
8.34
–
8.35
), one concludes the lemma as follows: write
f=(f_{0},f_{1})
, so that by the decomposition (
8.31
) one has
\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}(v)=f_{0}-\pi_{p_{\eta}}(\phi)
.
Applying (
8.34
) shows

\displaystyle\|(v,\lambda)\|_{m}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{0}-\pi_{p_{\eta}}(\phi)\|_{m+3/2}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f_{0}-\pi_{p_{\eta}}(\phi)\|_{m_{0}}\Big)

(8.36)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{0}\|_{m+3/2}\ +\ \|\pi_{p_{\eta}}\phi\|_{m+3/2}

(8.37)

\displaystyle\ +\ \ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f_{0}\|_{m_{0}}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|\pi_{p_{\eta}}(\phi)\|_{m_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{0}\|_{m+3/2}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f\|_{m_{0}}\Big)

(8.38)

In the last step, we have used that the 1-dimensional image of
\pi_{p_{\eta}}
obeys

\|\pi_{p_{\eta}}(\phi)\|_{m+3/2}\leq\|\phi\|_{1}\ \cdot\ \|\Pi_{p_{\eta}}\Phi_{p_{\eta}}\|_{m+3/2}\leq C_{m}\|\phi\|_{1}\cdot\|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+4}

by Cauchy-Schwartz on (
8.32
), and elliptic bootstrapping of the eigenvector
\Phi_{p_{\eta}}
using the second-order operator from Lemma
8.1
, and
\|\phi\|_{1}\leq\|f\|_{m_{0}}
by Lemma
8.16
.

Similarly, for the second component, (
8.35
) shows

\displaystyle{\|\phi\|_{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{1}-(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})\mathcal{B}(v,\lambda)\|_{m}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f_{1}-(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})\mathcal{B}(v,\lambda)\|_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{1}\|_{m}\ +\ \|(v,\lambda)\|_{m+s}+\|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f\|_{m_{0}}+\|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|(v,\lambda)\|_{4}\Big).

where we have used that there is a (tame) boundedness estimate

\|(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})\mathcal{B}(v,\lambda)\|_{m}\leq C_{m}\Big(\|(v,\lambda)\|_{m+s}+\|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+4}\|(v,\lambda)\|_{m_{0}}\Big)

(8.39)

for the range components. Such an estimate follows from showing
(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}}),\mathcal{B}
are individually bounded tame maps; the first of these is Lemma
8.15
(in which the boundedness is easily seen to tame), and the second follows from interpolation and Young’s inequality (cf. the subsequent Lemma
8.18
). Substituting the previous estimate (
8.38
) on
\|(v,\lambda)\|_{m}
and using that
\|(v,\lambda)\|_{4}\leq\|g\|_{m_{0}}
by Lemma
8.16
then shows that

\displaystyle\|(v,\phi,\lambda)\|_{m}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{1}\|_{m+3/2}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f\|_{m_{0}}\Big)

as desired.

To complete the lemma, we now prove (
8.34
) and (
8.35
). The latter follows from differentiating elliptic estimates in the standard way. To elaborate briefly, we begin with the estimate for
\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}
and the
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
term in the norms. One shows by iterating commutators that there is an elliptic estimate of the form

\|\phi\|_{rH^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}}\leq C_{m}\Big(\|\overline{\not{D}}_{p_{\eta}}\phi\|_{H^{m}_{\text{b}}}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{s^{\prime}}\|\phi\|_{rH^{m-1,1}_{\text{b},e}}\ +\ \ldots\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|\phi\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}\Big)

(8.40)

for each
m
and
s^{\prime}<m_{0}
. Given such an estimate, the
k^{th}
middle term can be absorbed into the
k=0,m
ones by Young’s inequality and interpolation with
m_{2}=m+s^{\prime}
and
m_{1}=s^{\prime}
on the first factor and
m_{2}=m-1
and
m_{2}=0
on the second factor. The tame estimates are then a consequence of induction by substituting the tame estimate on
\|\phi\|_{rH^{m-1,1}_{\text{b},e}}
beginning with the base case provided by Lemma
8.16
, and using that
\|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{s^{\prime}}\leq 1
. The same argument applies for the spaces
r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}
and
\mathcal{H}^{m,0}
using the elliptic estimate and commutation relations from Lemma
8.14
. (
8.35
) follows.

Similarly, for (
8.34
) it suffices to show

\|v\|_{{m+2}}\leq C_{m}\Big(\|\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}v\|_{{m+3/2}}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{s^{\prime}}\|v\|_{{m-1+3/2}}\ +\ \ldots\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|v\|_{3/2}\Big),

(8.41)

and applying the same combination of interpolation and Young’s inequality. (
8.41
) follows from iterating commutators again for each term of (
8.28
).
To use the term
\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(v)
as an example, one has

\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\nabla_{t}^{m}v)=\nabla_{t}^{m}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(v)+(\nabla_{t}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}})(\nabla_{t}^{m-1}v)\ +\ldots+\ (\nabla_{t}^{m}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}})(v).

By the same argument as in Proposition
8.5
and Lemma
8.16
,
v\in H^{m+3/2}
implies that the first term has conormal regularity
3/2
, while each of the remaining terms has conormal regularity at least
5/2
. Proceeding now by induction, assume the estimate holds for
m-1
, and applying the
m=1
estimate from Lemma
8.16
leads to
\nabla_{t}^{m}v
:

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{t}^{m}v\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|\Xi_{p_{\eta}}^{-1}\Pi_{p_{\eta}}(\nabla_{t}^{m}\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(v)+\ldots+\nabla_{t}^{m}F^{2}(\eta^{2},v,\varphi))\|_{{3/2}}

\displaystyle\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{s^{\prime}}\|v\|_{{m-1+3/2}}\ +\ \ldots\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|v\|_{3/2}\Big)

where the induction hypothesis has been applied to all but the first term. As in (
8.39
), the projection
\Pi_{p_{\eta}}
behaves in a tame fashion, and it is easy to check from the construction in Section 4 (e.g. Lemma
4.18
) that Corollary
4.19
also behaves tamely. Commuting
\nabla_{t}
past
\Xi_{p_{\eta}}^{-1}\Pi_{p_{\eta}}
therefore contributes to the lower order terms, and we conclude

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{t}^{m}v\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|\nabla_{t}^{m}\overline{T}_{p,\eta}(v)\|_{{3/2}}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{s^{\prime}}\|v\|_{{m-1+3/2}}\ +\ \ldots\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|v\|_{3/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\|f_{0}\|_{m+3/2}\ +\ \|(p-p_{0},\varphi,\eta)\|_{m+s^{\prime}}\|f_{0}\|_{m_{0}}\Big)

which yields (
8.34
).
∎

Lemma 8.18
.

Hypothesis
(III)
of Theorem
7.4
holds for
\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}
, i.e. there are
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the following holds provided
\delta_{0}
is sufficiently small: for
p\in V_{0}
and
(\varphi,\eta)\in U_{0}
, the second derivative obeys the tame estimate

\|\text{d}^{2}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}(u,\upsilon)\|_{m}\leq C_{m}\ \Big(\|u\|_{m+r}\|\upsilon\|_{m_{0}}\ +\ \|u\|_{m_{0}}\|\upsilon\|_{m+r}\ +\ \|u\|_{m_{0}}\|\upsilon\|_{m_{0}}\cdot(1+\|(p,\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+r^{\prime}})\Big).

(8.42)

for
u,\upsilon\in X
uniformly over
V_{0}\times(U_{0}\cap\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}})
for all
m\geq m_{0}
.

Proof.

This tame estimate follows directly from using the boundedness of the terms comprising
\text{d}^{2}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}
in conjunction with the interpolation inequalities.

As in Corollary
8.10
, the second derivative is given by

\displaystyle\text{d}^{2}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}\Big((v,\phi),(w,\psi)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \ (\mathcal{B}_{\psi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\psi})(v)\ +\ (\mathcal{B}_{\phi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\phi})(w)

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{1}(w^{\prime},v^{\prime})\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime})\nabla\psi\ +\ M^{1}(\eta^{\prime},w^{\prime})\nabla\phi

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{2}(w^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ +\ M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\psi\ +\ M^{2}(v^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)

\displaystyle\ +\ M^{2}(v^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\psi\ +\ M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime})\phi\ +\ M^{2}(w^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})\phi

\displaystyle\ +\ F^{3}(\eta^{2},w,\varphi)\ +\ F^{4}(\eta^{2},v,\psi)\ +\ F^{5}(\eta,v,w,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)

For the sake of the proverbial deceased horse, we will prove the lemma for the term
M^{2}(w^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
; it is straightforward to verify that the same argument applies equally well to the remaining terms.

To begin, we bound that
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
-term in the norm. By Proposition
8.8
Item (C) part (iii), this term is itself a sum of terms of the form
m_{p}(y)w^{\prime}v^{\prime\prime}(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
. Differentiating the part involving
\varphi
of such a term,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\text{b}}^{m}(m_{p}(y)w^{\prime}v^{\prime\prime}\varphi)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\sum_{0\leq k\leq m}\|\nabla_{\text{b}}^{k}(v^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime})\nabla_{\text{b}}^{m-k}(m_{p}\varphi))\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\sum_{0\leq k\leq m}\|\nabla_{\text{b}}^{k}(v^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime})\|_{L^{2,2}(S^{1})}\|\nabla_{\text{b}}^{m-k}(m_{p}\varphi)\|_{H^{2}_{\text{b}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\sum_{0\leq k\leq m}\|v^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime}\|^{1-\tfrac{k}{m}}_{H^{2}(S^{1})}\ \|v^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime}\|^{\tfrac{k}{m}}_{H^{m+2}(S^{1})}\ \|m_{p}\varphi\|^{1-\tfrac{k}{m}}_{H^{2}_{\text{b}}}\ \|m_{p}\varphi\|^{\tfrac{k}{m}}_{H^{m+2}_{\text{b}}}

where we have used the Sobolev embedding
C^{0}\hookrightarrow H^{2}(S^{1})
and then the interpolation inequalities with
m_{2}=m+2,m_{1}=2
. By Young’s inequality with exponents
p=\tfrac{m}{k}
and
q=\tfrac{m}{m-k}
, one finds the above is bounded by

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\left(\ \|v^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{m+2,2}(S^{1})}\|m_{p}\varphi\|_{H^{2}(S^{1})}\ +\ \|v^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{2}(S^{1})}\|m_{p}\varphi\|_{H^{m+2}_{\text{b}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\ \|v^{\prime}\|_{H^{m+4}(S^{1})}\|w^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{4}(S^{1})}\ +\ \|v^{\prime}\|_{H^{4}(S^{1})}\|w^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{m+4}(S^{1})}\

\displaystyle+\ \|v^{\prime}\|_{H^{4}(S^{1})}\|w^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{4}(S^{1})}\left(\|m_{p}\|_{H^{m+4}_{\text{b}}}\|\varphi\|_{H^{4}_{\text{b}}}+\|m_{p}\|_{H^{4}_{\text{b}}}\|\varphi\|_{H^{m+4}_{\text{b}}}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{m}\Big(\ \|v\|_{H^{m+5}(S^{1})}\|w\|_{H^{6}(S^{1})}\ +\ \|v\|_{H^{5}(S^{1})}\|w\|_{H^{m+6}(S^{1})}\ +\ \|v\|_{H^{5}(S^{1})}\|w^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{6}(S^{1})}\ \cdot\ \|(p,\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+6}\Big)\

where we have repeated the interpolation and Young’s steps from above with
m_{2}=m+4
and
m_{1}=4
on both products, and then used the fact that
6\leq m_{0}
so that
\|m_{p}\|_{H^{4}_{\text{b}}}+\|\varphi\|_{H^{4}_{\text{b}}}\leq C
. This shows the desired estimate for
r,r^{\prime}=6
. The same steps apply to the
r^{\nu}H^{m-1}_{\text{b}}
term in the norms (Definition
8.11
) using the commutation relations from Lemma
8.14
. The other terms are similar, with the constant term in
(1+\|(p,\eta,\varphi)\|_{m+r^{\prime}})
on the right hand side arising from the terms not involving
(p,\varphi,\eta)
such as
\mathcal{B}_{\psi}(v)
. ∎

8.6.
Proofs of Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
1.6

In this subsection, we invoke the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem
7.4
to conclude the proofs of Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
1.6
, beginning with the latter.

Proof of Corollary
1.6
.

Lemmas
8.16
,
8.17
, and
8.18
verify respectively that hypotheses
(I)
,
(II)
, and
(III)
from Section
Hypothesis (I)
hold on
V_{0}\times(U_{0}\cap\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}})
. Lemma
8.7
(which extends easily to the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{m,0}
from Lemma
8.11
) shows that
f_{p}
obeys
\|f_{p}\|_{m}\leq C\|p\|_{m+s}
. Moreover, since term in
f_{p}=\not{D}_{p}\Phi_{0}
is the product of functions that are smooth across
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
with the polyhomogeneous
\Phi_{0}
and its derivatives (cf. Appendix
C
), one has that
f_{p}\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is also polyhomogeneous with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}-\tfrac{1}{2}
.

It remains to show that the property (P) of being polyhomogeneous is propagated by the iteration in the sense of Definition
7.3
. Lemma
8.11
and its proof in Appendix
B
show that the smoothing operators
S_{\varepsilon},S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}
preserve polyhomogeneity.
The argument above that
f_{p}\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
, in fact applies equally well to show that
\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
is polyhomogeneous for any pullback parameter
p_{\eta}=F^{*}_{\eta}p
, provided
\varphi
is polyhomogeneous with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}
. This is to say that

\varphi\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p}(\eta,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

(8.43)

preserves polyhomogeneity. To show polyhomogeneity (P) is propagated, we therefore verify that

\displaystyle\varphi\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}\text{ \ , \ }f\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ (\text{d}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p})^{-1}f\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}},

this being the third requirement in Definition
7.3
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}},f\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is polyhomogeneous with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}-\tfrac{1}{2}
, and suppose that

(\phi,v,\lambda)=(\text{d}_{(\eta,\varphi)}\overline{\not{\mathbb{D}}}_{p})^{-1}f\in\mathcal{X}

is the unique solution guaranteed by Lemma
8.16
. By the block-diagonal decomposition (
8.31
) from Lemma
8.16
, this solution obeys

\displaystyle\overline{T}_{p_{\eta}}(v)+\lambda c_{1}\Pi_{p_{\eta}}\Phi_{p_{\eta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi_{p_{\eta}}f

(8.44)

\displaystyle(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}(v)+\overline{\not{D}}_{p_{\eta}}\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})f

(8.45)

where
c_{1}=\langle\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}\phi,\Phi_{p_{\eta}}\Phi_{p_{\eta}}\rangle_{L^{2}}
. The projection
\Pi_{p_{\eta}}
preserves polyhomogeneity, since

\Pi_{p_{\eta}}=\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}P_{p_{\eta}}\not{D}_{p_{\eta}}^{\star}

(8.46)

(by Corollary
2.12
or Proposition
8.15
) is the composition of three operators, all of which individually preserve polyhomogeneity. That each of these preserves polyhomogeneity can be seen by differentiating or solving the expansions term by term (cf.
[
47
, Prop. 7.17]
for a general proof). Just as in the proof of (
8.43
),
\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}(v)
consists of products of functions that are smooth across
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
with the polyhomogeneous
\varphi,\nabla\varphi
. Rearranging (
8.45
),

\overline{\not{D}}_{p_{\eta}}\phi=(1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})f\ -\ (1-\Pi_{p_{\eta}})\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}(v)

(8.47)

and all the terms on the right side are therefore polyhomogeneous with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}-\tfrac{1}{2}
. This implies, again solving term by term (cf.
[
47
, Prop. 7.17]
) that
\phi
is polyhomogeneous with index set
\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}
, with the caveat that we may
a priori
have a logarithm term on the
r^{1/2}
coefficient.

To rule out the appearance of logarithm terms with radial dependence
r^{1/2}\log(r)
, we investigate the first term of the polyhomogeneous expansion, which is obtained by formally solving this initial term. In particular, the non-appearance of logarithm terms is a consequences of a restriction on the
\theta
-Fourier modes that appear with the
r^{1/2}
coefficient. To elaborate, let
(t,r,\theta)
denote the polar Fermi coordinates (Definition
3.4
) of the metric in the parameter
p_{\eta}
. Logarithm terms
e^{ik\theta}r^{1/2}\log(r)
would arise from the right-hand side having terms
r^{-1/2}e^{\pm 3i\theta/2}
. Proceeding by induction, suppose that
\varphi
hadsno such term at the
N^{th}
iteration, and we claim no such term can then appear at the
(N+1)^{st}
stage. Indeed, if
\varphi
has leading order terms
e^{\pm i\theta/2}r^{1/2}
, then the error
f_{N}
at this stage also has only leading order terms
e^{\pm\theta/2}r^{-1/2}
(these leading order terms arise as the leading order of
\nabla_{z}\varphi,\nabla_{\overline{z}}\varphi
times smooth functions of
t
via (
8.43
)). The terms from
\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}
obey the same restriction, and this restriction is respected by the projection
\Pi_{p_{\eta}}
since the same argument applies to each of the three operators (
8.46
). It follows that all the terms on the right side in (
8.47
) have leading order
e^{i\pm\theta/}r^{-1/2}
. Formally solving shows that the correction
\phi
has the same leading order terms as
\varphi
. Since the smoothing operators
S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}
were construction to preserve this property (see Appendix
B
), this closes the induction. We conclude that the property (P) of being polyhomogeneous in the sense of having expansions of the form (
8.2
)-(
8.3
) without logarithm terms is propagated.

By the Nash-Moser Implicit Function Theorem
7.4
, there is therefore an open neighborhood
\mathscr{V}_{0}\subset\mathcal{P}
of smooth parameters such that for
p\in\mathscr{V}_{0}
there exists a unique solution
(\mathcal{Z}_{p},\Phi_{p},\Lambda_{p})
to the equation

{\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}(\mathcal{Z}_{p},\Phi_{p})}=\Lambda_{p}\Phi_{p}

(8.48)

and the triples
(\mathcal{Z}_{p},\Phi_{p},\Lambda_{p})
define a smooth tame graph over
\mathscr{V}_{0}\times\{0\}\subset\mathscr{V}_{0}\times\mathcal{X}
. This completes the proof of Corollary
1.6
in the presence of Assumption
6.12
. In the absence of Assumption
6.12
, the standard Kuranishi framework (see, e.g. Section 3.3 of
[
8
]
) applies to show that the set of parameters for which (
8.48
) holds is described by the zero set of a smooth tame map

\kappa_{p}:\mathscr{V}_{0}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

where
n=\dim(\ker(T_{\Phi_{0}}))
is the dimension of the kernel of the index 0 map from Section
6.3
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

The projection
\pi(\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})\subseteq\mathscr{V}_{0}\cap\mathcal{P}
of the universal moduli space of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinors to the parameter space is defined by the zero-set

\pi(\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})=\Lambda^{-1}(0)\cap\mathscr{V}_{0}

of the eigenvalue
\Lambda:\mathscr{V}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
in Corollary
1.6
, and there is locally a unique
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-harmonic spinor
(\mathcal{Z}_{p},\Phi_{p})
up to normalization and sign for each
p\in\Lambda^{-1}(0)
, hence the projection
\pi
is a local homeomorphism.

To conclude the theorem, we show that Assumption
6.12
implies that the map
\Lambda:\mathscr{V}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
is transverse to 0. To see this, let
p(s)
be a path of parameters with
p(0)=p_{0}
to be specified momentarily. By Corollary
1.6
, such a path implicitly defines triples
(\mathcal{Z}_{s},\Phi_{s},\Lambda_{s})
satisfying (
8.48
) for
s
sufficiently small. Differentiating (
8.48
) at
s=0
yields the relation that

\left(\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}(s),p_{0}}\right)\Phi_{0}+\left(\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}_{0},p(s)}\right)\Phi_{0}+\not{D}_{0}\dot{\Phi}=\dot{\Lambda}\Phi_{0}

(8.49)

where
\cdot
denotes the
s
-derivative at
s=0
. We now choose
p(s)=(g(s),B(s))
so that the derivative
(\dot{g},\dot{B})
has the following properties. Let
\dot{B}
be a smooth perturbation supported on a neighborhood disjoint from
N_{r_{0}}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
such that
\langle\gamma(\dot{B})\Phi_{0},\Phi_{0}\rangle\neq 0
. Given this, we define
\dot{g}
in terms of
\dot{B}
as follows. By Assumption
6.12
, we know that
\text{ob}_{0}\circ T_{\Phi_{0}}:H^{2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\to\text{ker}(\not{D}_{0}|_{L^{2}})
is injective, and the closure of its range has 1-dimensional orthogonal complement. Let
\Phi_{1}
be the normalized spinor whose span is this 1-dimensional space (note that in general
\Phi_{0}\neq\Phi_{1}
, since
T_{\Phi_{0}}
need not have image orthogonal to
\Phi_{0}
). Decompose
\Pi_{0}(\gamma(\dot{B})\Phi_{0})=(c_{0}\Phi_{1},\xi)
, and take the path
g(s)
such that
\dot{g}=\dot{g}_{\eta}
where
\text{ob}_{0}\circ T_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta)=-\xi
, so that

\Pi_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{\Phi_{0}}(\eta))=(0,-\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\Phi_{1}\oplus\text{Im}(T_{\Phi_{0}})\simeq\text{\bf Ob}_{0}

where
\text{\bf Ob}_{0}=\text{\bf Ob}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
is as in Section
4
. Moreover, since
\overline{T}_{\Phi_{0}}
is an isomorphism by Assumption
6.12
, one has
\langle\Phi_{1},\Phi_{0}\rangle\neq 0
. Since
\dot{B}
is smooth and supported away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
,
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
by Item (B) of Corollary
6.6
. By design, the first stage of iteration in this case now requires no correction to
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, hence
\mathcal{Z}(s)=O(s^{2})
(no smoothing is needed in this first stage as
\eta
is already smooth). Taking the inner product of (
8.50
) with
\Phi_{1}
then yields

\langle\cancel{\tfrac{d}{ds}|_{s=0}\not{D}_{\mathcal{Z}(s),p_{0}}\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}}\rangle+\langle-c\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\rangle+\cancel{\langle\not{D}_{0}\dot{\Phi},\Phi_{1}\rangle}=\dot{\Lambda}\langle\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}\rangle

(8.50)

wherein the first term vanishes because
\mathcal{Z}(s)=O(s^{2})
, and the third via integration by parts since
\Phi_{1}\in\ker(\not{D}_{0}|_{L^{2}})
. We conclude that
\dot{\Lambda}\neq 0
and that
\Lambda:\mathscr{V}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
is transverse to 0 at
p_{0}
, hence on
\mathscr{V}_{0}
after possibly intersecting with a smaller open set.

When Assumption
6.12
fails, the standard Kuranishi framework (Section 3.3 of
[
8
]
) applies again to show that the
\pi(\mathscr{M}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}})
is given locally by the zero-set of a smooth tame map

(\kappa_{p},\Lambda):\mathscr{V}_{0}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}

where
n=\dim(\ker(T_{\Phi_{0}}))
is the dimension of the kernel of the index 0 map from Section
6.3
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.5
.
∎

Appendix A
Exponential Decay

This appendix proves Lemma
4.9
, which was used to establish the exponential decay estimates in Section
4
. To recall the notation,
\not{D}_{\circ}
here denotes the Dirac operator on
Y_{\circ}=(S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2},dt^{2}+dx^{2}+dy^{2})
. The lemma asserted that given a solution

\not{D}_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ}u_{\ell}=f_{\ell}

(A.1)

of the second order equation where
f_{\ell}
satisfies the hypotheses (
4.15
–
4.16
), then the solution
u_{\ell}
satisfies (
4.17
). The proof relies on a discretized version of the maximum principle based on similar found in
[
33
, A.2.1]
.

Proof.

The integration by parts arguments of Proposition
2.4
and
2.7
holds equally well on the non-compact
Y_{\circ}
, since the boundary term at
r\to\infty
vanishes (and the
L^{2}
-term is compactly supported). In fact, since
B_{0}=0
and the product metric is flat, the Weitzenböck formula implies that
rH^{1}_{e}
-kernel is empty. It follows, as in Lemma
2.7
that

\not{D}_{\circ}\not{D}_{\circ}:rH^{1}_{e}(Y_{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})\longrightarrow rH^{-1}_{e}(Y_{\circ}\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})

is an isomorphism, hence (
A.1
) admits a unique solution. Since
\not{D}_{\circ}
preserves Fourier modes,
u_{\ell}
automatically satisfies the same Fourier mode restriction as
f_{\ell}
in (
4.15
), and it suffices to prove the decay estimate.

Recall that
A_{n\ell}
denotes the sequence of annuli (
4.7
) from Part (B) of Proposition
4.3
. Let
\chi_{n}
be a cutoff-function equal to
1
on
A_{n_{\ell}}
such that

\text{supp}(d\chi_{n})\subseteq A_{(n-1)\ell}\cup A_{(n+1)\ell}\hskip 56.9055pt|d\chi_{n}|\leq\frac{c|\ell|}{R_{0}}.

Taking the inner product of (
A.1
) with
\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell}
, integrating by parts, and using Young’s inequality yields the following for universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}
, where we denote
B_{n\ell}=A_{(n-1)\ell}\cup A_{n\ell}\cup A_{(n+1)\ell}
as in the statement of the lemma:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{n\ell}}\chi_{n}^{2}|\not{D}_{\circ}{u_{\ell}}|^{2}\ dV_{\circ}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{B_{n\ell}}\langle 2\chi_{n}d\chi_{n}.u_{\ell},\not{D}_{\circ}u_{\ell}\rangle\ dV_{\circ}+\int_{B_{n\ell}}\langle\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell},-f_{\ell}\rangle\ dV_{\circ}

(A.2)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2c_{1}^{2}\frac{|\ell|^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}+\frac{1}{2}\|\chi_{n}\not{D}_{\circ}{u_{\ell}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}

(A.3)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2c_{2}}\|f_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{-1}_{e}(B_{n\ell})}+\frac{c_{2}}{2}\|\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(B_{n\ell})}

(A.4)

where the inner product and volume form
dV_{\circ}
are defined using the product metric
g_{\circ}
.

The
\|\chi_{n}\not{D}_{\circ}u_{\ell}\|^{2}
term on the right may be absorbed into the same term on the left because of the factor of
1/2
. Similarly, by choosing
c_{2}
small, the
\|\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell}\|_{rH^{1}_{e}}
term may be absorbed into the others as follows. The elliptic estimate for
\not{D}_{\circ}:rH^{1}_{e}\to\text{Range}(\not{D}_{\circ})
applied to
\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell}
shows

\frac{c_{2}}{2}\|\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(B_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{c_{2}C}{2}\|\not{D}_{\circ}(\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell})\|_{L^{2}(Y_{0})}^{2}\ \leq\frac{c_{2}C}{2}\left(\ \|\chi_{n}^{2}\not{D}_{\circ}u_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}^{2}+\|d\chi_{n}.u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}\right).

(A.5)

By choosing
c_{1}
sufficiently small (and using that
|\chi_{n}^{2}|\leq|\chi_{n}|
), the
\|\chi_{n}^{2}\not{D}_{0}u_{\ell}\|^{2}
term can again be absorbed on the left hand side of (
A.2
), and the
\|d\chi_{n}.u_{\ell}\|^{2}
term can be absorbed into (
A.3
) by increasing
c^{2}
.

Applying the elliptic estiamte (
A.5
) again without the factor of
c_{2}
and substituting the result of (
A.2
–
A.5
) yields

\displaystyle\|u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\|\chi_{n}^{2}u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(B_{n\ell})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}\frac{|\ell|^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}+\frac{1}{2c_{1}}\|f_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{-1}_{e}(B_{n\ell})}

which shows, invoking the assumption on
f_{\ell}
, that

\displaystyle\|u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(A_{n\ell})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}|\ell|^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\|u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{n\ell})}+\frac{C^{\prime}_{m}}{|\ell|^{2+2m}}e^{-2n/c_{m}},

(A.6)

for a universal constant
C_{1}
. In addition, the Fourier mode restriction on
u_{\ell}
means that the first term on the right obeys

\frac{C_{1}|\ell|^{2}}{R_{0}^{2}}\int_{B_{n\ell}}|u_{\ell}|^{2}\ dV\leq\frac{4C_{1}}{R_{0}^{2}}\left(\int_{A_{(n-1)\ell}}|\nabla u_{\ell}|^{2}\ dV+\int_{A_{n\ell}}|\nabla u_{\ell}|^{2}\ dV+\int_{A_{(n+1)\ell}}|\nabla u_{\ell}|^{2}\ dV\right).

Now set
\mathfrak{u}_{n}=\|u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(A_{n\ell})}
, and choose
R_{0}
so that
4C_{1}/R_{0}^{2}<1/200
. Substituting the above relation from the Fourier mode restriction into (
A.6
) yields the discrete differential inequality

\mathfrak{u}_{n}-\tfrac{1}{100}(\mathfrak{u}_{n-1}+\mathfrak{u}_{n+1})\leq\mathfrak{s}_{n},

where
\mathfrak{s}_{n}=\frac{C^{\prime}_{m}}{|\ell|^{2+2m}}e^{-2n/c_{m}}
.

To conclude, we apply a discrete version of the maximum principle:
\mathfrak{s}_{n}
trivially satisfies

\mathfrak{s}_{n}-\tfrac{1}{100}(\mathfrak{s}_{n-1}+\mathfrak{s}_{n+1})\leq\mathfrak{s}_{n}

because it is positive, thus the difference
\mathfrak{r}_{n}=\mathfrak{u}_{n}-\mathfrak{s}_{n}
satisfies

\mathfrak{r}_{n}-\tfrac{1}{100}(\mathfrak{r}_{n-1}+\mathfrak{r}_{n+1})\leq 0.

(A.7)

Additionally,
\mathfrak{r}_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
by integrability, and it may be arranged (by increasing
C^{\prime}_{m}
) that
\mathfrak{r}_{0}\leq 0
. An interior maximum with
\mathfrak{r}_{n}\geq\mathfrak{r}_{n-1},\mathfrak{r}_{n+1}
would violate (
A.7
), thus by the “maximum principle” we conclude
\mathfrak{r}_{n}=\mathfrak{u}_{n}-\mathfrak{s}_{n}\leq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, thus
u_{\ell}
satisfies

\|u_{\ell}\|^{2}_{rH^{1}_{e}(A_{n\ell})}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}_{m}}{|\ell|^{2+2m}}\text{Exp}\left(-\frac{2n}{c_{m}^{\prime}}\right)

(A.8)

which completes the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Boundary and Edge Regularity

This appendix gives proofs of two facts about regularity in the boundary and edge Sobolev spaces, namely Lemma
8.11
and Lemma
8.13
.

Recall the Fréchet spaces defined in Lemma
8.11
. To restate the assertion of Lemma
8.11
succinctly:

Lemma B.1
.

For
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
there exist smoothing operators

S_{\varepsilon}:C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\to C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})\hskip 56.9055ptS_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}:\bigcap_{m\geq 0}X_{m}^{\prime\prime}\to\bigcap_{m\geq 0}X_{m}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 56.9055ptS_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Y}

satisfying properties (i)-(iii) of Definition
7.1
and preserving the property (P) of polyhomogeneity defined by (
8.2
–
8.3
). Additionally, in Fermi coordinates around
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
,
S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}
does not introduce new Fourier modes in
\theta
.

Proof.

On
\mathcal{X}_{0}=C^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{0};N\mathcal{Z}_{0})
,
S_{\varepsilon}
may be defined as a convolution operator using a Schwartz kernel that smoothly approximates the
\delta
-distribution along the diagonal in
(\mathcal{Z}_{0})^{2}=\mathcal{Z}_{0}\times\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Let
\chi(r)
be a cut-off function equal to 1 near
r=0
and vanishing for
r>1
. Fix a collection
U_{j}\times\mathbb{C}
for
j=1,..,n
of trivializations of
N\mathcal{Z}_{0}
on contractible open sets, and for each
j
, choose nested cut-off functions
\xi_{j},\beta_{j}
such that
\text{supp}(\beta_{j})\Subset\{\xi_{j}=1\}
. Then define

S_{\varepsilon}(\eta)(t):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}(t)\int_{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\chi\left(\tfrac{|t-t^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\right)\beta_{j}(t^{\prime})\eta(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

(B.1)

where the constant
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}
serves to normalize
\chi
in
L^{2}
. Properties (i)-(iii) now follow easily.

The construction of
S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}
is analogous, but now we de-singularize the
\delta
-distribution on the diagonal in the blown-up product defined as follows. Let
B=\mathcal{Z}_{0}\times\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subset Y\times Y
, and let
\mathbb{S}(B)
denote the sphere bundle of radius
r_{0}
in the normal bundle. Define the blown-up product by

Y^{2}_{\text{b}}:=(Y\setminus(N_{r_{0}}\mathcal{Z}_{0}))^{2}\cup\mathbb{S}(B).

This blow-up is a compact 6-manifold with corners, having three boundary strata of codimension 1 consisting of the interiors of
\partial(N_{r_{0}}\mathcal{Z}_{0}\times Y),\partial(Y\times N_{r_{0}}\mathcal{Z}_{0}),\partial(\mathbb{S}(B))
which intersect along codimension 2 corners. This space can be given local coordinates
(s,\rho,\theta,\theta^{\prime},t,t^{\prime})
in a neighborhood of the diagonal, where
s=[r,r^{\prime}]
is a projective coordinate along the blow-up boundary, and
\rho=r^{\prime}
.

Away from these strata,
S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}
can be defined analogously to (
B.1
); near the boundary strata it is defined as a product

S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}:=S_{\varepsilon}^{\theta}\circ S^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}

where
S_{\varepsilon}^{\theta}
is defined by truncation of the
\theta
-Fourier modes in a local trivialization, and
S_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
is given in Fermi coordinates
(s,\rho,t,t^{\prime})
by

S^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\psi)(r,t,\theta):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\chi\int_{Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0}}\chi\left(\tfrac{|s-1|}{\varepsilon}\right)\chi\left(\tfrac{|t-t^{\prime}|}{\varepsilon}\right)\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}(\beta\psi)dt^{\prime}dr^{\prime}

(B.2)

where the factor of
1/r^{\prime}
appears because
|r-r^{\prime}|\sim r^{\prime}s
and the
\delta
-distribution is homogeneous of order
-1
.

The properties (i)-(iii) for the spaces
H^{m}_{\text{b}}
now follow analogously to the compact case. That
S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}
introduces no new Fourier modes in
\theta
is manifest from the definition, and the fact that polyhomogeneity is preserved is a consequence of the pushforward theorem or of direct inspection of the integral (
B.2
) (see
[
18
, Sec. 3.1]
). Since the ratio
r/r^{\prime}
is uniformly bounded where
\chi\neq 0
, the commutators
[\nabla^{e},S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}]
and
[r^{\alpha},S_{\varepsilon}^{\text{b}}]
are uniformly bounded, properties (i)-(iii) for the space
\bigcap_{m\geq 0}H^{m,1}_{\text{b},e}
follows from the equivalent description of the norm (
2.14
). The same applies for the terms
(r\partial_{r}\pm\tfrac{1}{2})\psi
and therefore for the spaces
r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}
and
\mathcal{H}^{m,0}
.

∎

What remains is to prove Lemma
8.13
, which requires several steps.

Lemma B.2
.

If
\varphi\in r^{\alpha}H^{3}_{\text{b}}
for
\alpha>1
then the
\varphi
satisfies the pointwise bound

|\varphi(x)|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{rH^{3}_{\text{b}}}.

(B.3)

Proof.

We first prove the lemma in the 1-dimensional case: consider
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
with the measure
rdr
and suppose that
\varphi\in rH^{1}_{b}(rdr)
has
\text{supp}(\varphi)\subseteq(0,1]
. Then we claim that there is a constant
C
so that

|\varphi(x)|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{\text{b}}(rdr)}=C\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\frac{|\varphi|^{2}}{r^{2}}+|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\ rdr\right)^{1/2}.

(B.4)

This follows from a dyadic decomposition. Since
r
is uniformly bounded on
[1/2,2]
and
H^{1}[1/2,2]\hookrightarrow C^{0}[1/2,2]
by the standard Sobolev embedding, we have
|\varphi(1)|^{2}\leq c\int|\varphi|^{2}+|\nabla\varphi|^{2}dr\leq c\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{\text{b}}}^{2}
. Then, by the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus,

\displaystyle|\varphi(1/2)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\varphi(1)|+\int_{1/2}^{1}|\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)|d\rho\leq|\varphi(1)|+\left(\int_{1/2}^{1}|\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)|^{2}\rho d\rho\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{1/2}^{2}\frac{1}{\rho}d\rho\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\varphi(1)|+(\log 2)^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{\text{b}}([1/2,1],rdr)}

Similarly,
|\varphi(1/4)|\leq|\varphi(1/2)|+(\log 2)^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{\text{b}}([1/4,1/2],rdr)}\leq|\varphi(1)|+(\log 2)^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{\text{b}}([1/4,1],rdr)}
where the second inequality follows from substituting the above. In general, using the estimate on
|\varphi(1)|
we conclude that

|\varphi{(2^{-k})}|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{rH^{1}_{\text{b}}(rdr)}.

(
B.4
) then follows from applying the Fundamental Theorem of calculus again for
x\in[2^{-k},2^{-k+1}]
.

In general, for
\varphi\in H^{m}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0};S_{0})
, the lemma follows from the above by applying (
B.4
) to rays of constant
(t,\theta)
and after using the Sobolev restriction theorem
rH^{3}_{\text{b}}(Y\setminus\mathcal{Z}_{0})\to rH^{1}_{b}(rdr)
, where the one-dimensional space is a normal ray emanating from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
.

∎

Next, we have the following fundamental fact about ODEs. For it, we use the 1-dimensional
b
-spaces
r^{\alpha}H^{1}_{\text{b}}([0,1],rdr)
and
r^{\alpha}L^{2}([0,1],rdr)
defined by the norms

\|u\|_{r^{\alpha}H^{1}_{\text{b}}}=\left(\int_{0}^{1}(|r\partial_{r}u|^{2}+|u|^{2})r^{-2\alpha}rdr\right)^{1/2}\hskip 56.9055pt\|u\|_{r^{\alpha}L^{2}_{\text{b}}}=\left(\int_{0}^{1}|u|^{2}r^{-2\alpha}rdr\right)^{1/2}.

Lemma B.3
.

Provided
\alpha>3/2
then

(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2}):r^{\alpha}H^{1}_{\text{b}}(0,1]\to r^{\alpha}L^{2}_{\text{b}}(0,1]

is an isomorphism, hence there is a constant
C
such that

\|u\|_{r^{\alpha}H^{1}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})u\|_{r^{\alpha}L^{2}_{\text{b}}}

(B.5)

holds.

Proof.

Setting
r=e^{s}
for
s\in(-\infty,0]
the problem is equivalent to the analogous statement for

\partial_{s}-\tfrac{1}{2}:e^{(1-\alpha)s}L^{1,2}((-\infty,0],ds)\longrightarrow e^{(1-\alpha)s}L^{2}((-\infty,0],ds)

which is conjugate to

\tfrac{1}{e^{(\alpha-1)s}}(\partial_{s}-\tfrac{1}{2})e^{(\alpha-1)s}=(\partial_{s}+\alpha-\tfrac{3}{2}):L^{1,2}((-\infty,0],ds)\to L^{2}((-\infty,0],ds).

where
L^{1,2}((-\infty,0],ds)
is the standard Sobolev space on the half-line. The claim then follows directly from integrating by parts since the boundary term
(\alpha-\tfrac{3}{2})|u(0)|^{2}>0
is strictly positive.
∎

We now conclude the proof of Lemma
8.13
.

Proof of Lemma
8.13
.

If
\varphi
is compactly supported away from
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
in
Y\setminus N_{r_{0}/2}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
, the lemma is immediate from the standard Sobolev Embedding Theorem since
r
is bounded below in this region. We may therefore assume that
\varphi
is supported in a tubular neighborhood of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
. Since
\varphi\in r\mathcal{H}^{m,1}\cap\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{X}}
by assumption, we may write

\varphi=A(t,\theta)r^{1/2}+B(t,\theta,r)

in local coordinates, after which it suffices to show the bound for each term individually.

Using Lemma
B.3
by applying (
B.5
) to derivatives and integrating over the
t,\theta
variables leads to

\|r\partial_{r}u\|_{r^{\alpha}H^{m}_{\text{b}}}+\|u\|_{r^{\alpha}H^{m}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})u\|_{r^{\alpha}H^{m}_{\text{b}}}

for
\alpha>3/2
and in particular for
\alpha=1+\nu
. Applying this to
B(t,\theta,r)
and discarding the derivative term on the left shows that

\|B(r,t,\theta)\|_{r^{1+\nu}H^{\text{m}}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})B\|_{r^{1+\nu}H^{m}_{\text{b}}}=C\|(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})\varphi\|_{r^{1+\nu}H^{m}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{r\mathcal{H}^{m+1,1}}

(B.6)

since
(r\partial_{r}-\tfrac{1}{2})
annihilates
A(t,\theta)r^{1/2}
. Then, applying Lemma
B.3
to
B(t,\theta,r)r^{-1/2}
and substituting (
B.6
) shows that

|B(r,t,\theta)r^{-1/2}|\leq\|B(r,t,\theta)\|_{r^{3/2}H^{\text{m}}_{\text{b}}}\leq\|B(r,t,\theta)\|_{r^{1+\nu}H^{\text{m}}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{r\mathcal{H}^{m+1,1}}

and the result for
B(t,\theta,r)
follows after multiplying by
r^{1/2}
.

For the first term, the triangle inequality and (
B.6
) shows that

\|A(t,\theta)r^{1/2}\|_{rH^{m}_{\text{b}}}=\|A(t,\theta)r^{1/2}+B(r,t,\theta)\|_{rH^{m}_{\text{b}}}+\|B(r,t,\theta)\|_{rH^{\text{m}}_{\text{b}}}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{r\mathcal{H}^{m+2,1}}.

Finally, since
\|A(t,\theta)r^{1/2}\|_{rH^{m}_{\text{b}}}\sim\|A(t,\theta)\|_{H^{m}(T^{2})}
, the bound for the first term follows from the Sobolev embedding on
T^{2}
after increasing
m+2
to
m+4
.
∎

Appendix C
Non-Linear Deformation Terms

This appendix proves Proposition
8.8
. Retaining the notation of that proposition and the preceding discussion, Proposition
8.8
asserted in Item (C) that the non-linear terms of
\not{\mathbb{D}}_{p}
may be written.

\displaystyle Q_{p}(\eta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}+\mathfrak{B}_{\varphi,p})(\eta)\ \ +\ \ M^{1}_{p}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})\nabla(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ \ +\ \ M^{2}_{p}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})(\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\ \ +\ \ F_{p}(\eta,\Phi_{0}+\varphi).

Proof of Proposition
8.8
.

The constant (Item A) and linear (Item B) terms are immediate from, respectively, the definition (
8.11
) and the proof of Corollary
5.9
using the pullback metric (
8.14
) in place of
\dot{g}_{\eta}
.

We prove the above formula in Item (C) for the parameter
p=p_{0}
, as the general case differs only in notation. The quadratic terms in Item (C) are calculated by writing the full expression (
8.10
) and subtracting off the linear terms (the constant term vanishes for
p=p_{0}
). To this end, we apply (
8.10
) to
s\eta
, and collect the terms quadratic in
s
. By Remark
5.10
, the pullback metric can be written

g_{s\eta}=g_{0}+s\dot{g}_{\eta}+s^{2}\mathfrak{q}(\eta)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{where}\hskip 28.45274pt|\mathfrak{q}(\eta)|\leq C\big(\ |\eta^{\prime}\chi|+|\eta d\chi|+|\eta\chi|\ \big)^{2}.

i.e.
\mathfrak{q}(\eta)
is comprised of terms that are quadratic and higher order in
\eta
; in particular, it vanishes to second order at
s=0
(we omit the dependence of
\mathfrak{q}
on
s
from the notation). Working in an orthonormal frame with respect to
g_{0}
, the Taylor expansion shows that
\mathfrak{a}
in (
8.10
) and its inverse are given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Id}-\tfrac{s}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}+s^{2}\mathfrak{p}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Id}+\tfrac{s}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}+s^{2}\mathfrak{p}_{2},

where
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
have entries consisting of sums and products of smooth functions depending on the metric and on quadratic and higher combinations of
\eta^{\prime}\chi,\eta d\chi
and
\eta\chi
.

We now substitute these expressions into (
8.10
). Working in an orthonormal frame of
g_{0}
, with indices ranging over
1,2,3
the first term becomes

\sum_{i}e^{i}.\nabla^{g_{0}}_{\mathfrak{a}(e_{i})}=\sum_{i}e^{i}\nabla^{g_{0}}_{i}-\frac{s}{2}\sum_{ij}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}+s^{2}{\sum_{ij}\mathfrak{p}_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}}.

(C.1)

Therefore, in the trivialization of Lemma 5.1, the contribution of this first term is

\displaystyle\not{\mathbb{D}}(s\eta,\Phi_{0}+s\varphi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i}e^{i}\nabla^{g_{0}}_{i}-\frac{s}{2}\sum_{ij}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}+s^{2}{\sum_{ij}\mathfrak{p}_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}}\right)(\Phi_{0}+s\varphi)\ +\ \ldots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\left(\not{D}_{0}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}\Phi_{0}\right)\ +\

\displaystyle s^{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}\dot{g}_{\eta}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}\varphi+\sum_{ij}\mathfrak{p}_{1}(e_{i},e_{j})e^{i}.\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}(\Phi_{0}+s\varphi)\right)\ +\ \ldots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\cdot\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}(\eta,\varphi)+s^{2}\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}(\eta)\ +\ M^{1}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\nabla^{g_{0}}(\Phi_{0}+s\varphi)\ +\ F(\eta,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)\Big)\ +\ \ldots

since
\not{D}_{0}\Phi_{0}=0
, where
\ldots
constitutes the contribution from the remaining terms besides (
C.1
). The quadratic terms of
\mathfrak{p}_{1}
, by what was said above, contains exactly the type of terms asserted to be part of
M^{1}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})
, with
F(\eta,\Phi_{0}+\varphi)
being the terms of higher than quadratic order.

The remaining terms of (
8.10
) proceed in a similar fashion. Explicitly, some (quite a lot actually) of computation shows that they are

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sum_{ij}e^{i}.e^{j}.(\mathfrak{a}^{-1}(\nabla^{g_{0}}_{\mathfrak{a}(e_{i})}\mathfrak{a})e^{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\cdot\frac{1}{4}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta})\ +\ s^{2}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\mathfrak{p}_{1})+\frac{1}{8}\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}((\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{2})\nabla^{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}-2s\mathfrak{p}_{1}).)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{ijkm\ell}\Big[(\dot{g}_{\eta}-2s\mathfrak{p}_{1})_{j\ell}(\text{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}s\dot{g}_{\eta}+s^{2}\mathfrak{p}_{2})_{jm}[\nabla^{g_{0}}_{\ell}(\dot{g}_{\eta}-2s\mathfrak{p}_{1})]_{mk}\Big]e^{i}.e^{j}.e^{k}.\Big)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sum_{ij}e^{i}e^{j}.\left(\mathfrak{a}^{-1}(\nabla^{g_{\eta}}-\nabla^{g_{0}})_{\mathfrak{a}(e_{i})}\mathfrak{a}(e^{j})\right).

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).+\frac{1}{4}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(s\dot{g}_{\eta})\Big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle s^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{4}\sum_{ijk\ell}e^{i}.e^{j}.\Big[\left(\text{Id}+\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{2}\right)\Big(\Gamma_{i}(-\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{1})_{j\ell}e^{\ell}.

\displaystyle+\ (-\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{1})_{ik}\Gamma_{k}(\text{Id}-\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{1})_{j\ell}e^{\ell}.\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle+\ \frac{1}{4}\sum_{ij}e^{i}.e^{j}.\left[\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{2}\right)\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\text{div}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}).+\tfrac{1}{4}d\text{Tr}_{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta})\right)\right].\Big)

where

\displaystyle s\Gamma_{i}e^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{\eta}}_{i}-\nabla^{g_{0}}_{i})e^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{s}{2}\sum_{\ell k}(g_{\eta})^{-1}_{\ell k}\left((\nabla_{i}^{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{1}))_{j\ell}+(\nabla_{j}^{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{1}))_{i\ell}-(\nabla_{\ell}^{g_{0}}(\dot{g}_{\eta}+s\mathfrak{p}_{1}))_{ij}\right)e^{k}.

is the difference of the Levi-Civita connections. The terms linear in
s
combine to yield the remaining terms of
\text{d}\not{\mathbb{D}}
in Item (B), while the
s^{2}
terms are combined into
M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})
or absorbed into
F(\eta,\ \Phi_{0}+\varphi)
. Note that, by the product rule, each term of
M^{2}(\eta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})
contains are most a single instance of the second derivative
\eta^{\prime\prime}
.
∎
